I had just completed my studies at Oxford and was enjoying a short respite before taking full charge of the family estate. My father died when I was still a child; my mother followed within the year. By the time I came of age, I was as solitary as any man could be—without kin, without close friends, adrift in a world that felt increasingly unfamiliar. I knew little of my ancestors’ lives, save one persistent thread: many of them had been scholars, consumed by knowledge, drawn to the unseen and unspoken truths hidden beneath the surface of things.

That same hunger for understanding had taken root in me, though not with the rigor they might have shown. I dabbled in the physical sciences—not out of academic ambition, but from a deep-seated curiosity. The more I studied, the more I saw patterns where none should exist: echoes between physics and philosophy, between ancient myths and modern theories. Hypotheses flickered like stars in my mind, and I often mistook their glow for certainty, building theories on foundations no stronger than dreams.

The house itself was old—older than memory, older than records. Its walls held stories whispered through generations, though none were ever told aloud. The library, however, was its true heart. It had begun long before printing made books common, and over centuries it had grown like a living thing, swallowing rooms whole, claiming corridors, devouring space. Now it occupied most of the ground floor, a labyrinth of shelves and archways, doors leading into shadowed alcoves, steps descending into quiet corners. At its center stood the great hall—a vaulted chamber whose walls rose nearly to the ceiling, lined from floor to crown with leather-bound volumes, some so ancient their spines cracked with age.

It was here I spent most of my days, lost among texts both new and forgotten. I cared less for current discoveries than for how minds had once sought truth—how men like Ptolemy, Dante, Bacon, Boyle, even Darwin and Maxwell, had wrestled with the unknown. To me, those earlier thinkers were closer to the edge of darkness, standing just beyond the vanishing point of human understanding.

One August evening, the sky heavy with rain, I sat reading near a window, back turned to the glass. The storm had passed by dusk, and as the sun dipped below the horizon, a break in the clouds let golden light spill across the lawn. For a moment, the fountain’s spray caught fire, glowing crimson in the fading light.

I rose to look. Then, turning back, my gaze fell upon the portrait hanging in its niche—embedded in the wall of bookshelves like a shrine. It showed an old man with sharp features and eyes that seemed to follow you. I knew him as a distant ancestor, yet never questioned why he stood alone there, apart from the other family likenesses in the gallery. But now, bathed in sunlight, the painting seemed alive. The colors burned brighter, the expression deeper. And for the first time, it *answered* my stare.

Something stirred inside me—no sound, no movement, only a pull toward the far end of the room. I looked.

There, near the highest shelf, stood a tall figure—slender, draped in a dark coat worn thin with time. One hand reached upward, as if searching for a book. Then, in the dimming light, he vanished.

I blinked. My pulse quickened. I told myself it was a trick of the eye, a lingering afterimage. I returned to my reading.

But moments later, needing a specific volume, I went to the shelf—and found only an empty space. Where the book should have been, there was nothing.

I searched the area, calling out, but no one answered. No servant, no animal, no trace.

Yet the next morning, the book was back—exactly where it belonged.

No one in the household could have moved it. No one, I thought, would care enough.

Three days later, something stranger happened.

In one wall, concealed behind false bookbacks, was a narrow door—the entrance to a small closet housing the rarest and oldest manuscripts. The door itself was thick, reinforced, and framed with shallow shelves holding dummy spines. Some bore titles of books long vanished, others absurdly fictional ones. I’d always liked the illusion—it felt like a secret kept by the house itself.

Atop one row, tucked between two shelves, lay a fragment of a book—cut diagonally, half missing, bound in limp vellum. Only one corner remained open, revealing parchment pages filled with handwriting. It had been placed there deliberately, perhaps by a craftsman long dead, to complete the deception.

That afternoon, while reading, I glanced up—and saw it was gone.

Furious, I rang for the butler.

When he arrived, I asked what had become of the volume.

He paled. “I… I don’t know, sir.”

His voice trembled. He had served the family since my father was eight. He was loyal, unquestionable. Yet his hesitation unsettled me.

Later, as I sat again in the library, lost in thought, I looked up—and saw the back of a man disappearing through the masked door. He wore a long, dark coat, worn smooth by decades of use. His form was gaunt, his movements silent.

I lunged across the room, flung open the door, peered into the closet—empty. No exit, no hiding place.

I returned to my seat, shaken.

Then, glancing up again, I froze.

The broken book was back—restored to its spot, fixed as if it had never moved.

I seized it, tugged hard. It wouldn’t budge. Not even slightly.

I rang for the butler once more.

This time, I told him everything—what I’d seen, what I’d found.

He listened, then spoke slowly. “Sir… I’ve heard whispers about him. But not in years.”

“Who?” I asked.

“The old gentleman,” he said. “They called him Mr. Raven.”

He hesitated. “I didn’t want to speak of it. Not until now.”

“I’ve never heard of him,” I said.

“That’s because it stopped,” he replied. “Or so we all believed.”

He explained that when he first came to the house, people talked—whispers in the halls, warnings in the kitchen. A ghost, they said, haunted the library. An old man, always in black, who walked the shelves at night. But over time, the talk faded. No one mentioned him anymore. Not even the maids.

Only one footman left abruptly—claimed he saw the figure, and refused to stay.

An old woman from the village once told him a tale. Of Sir Upward, a nobleman obsessed with forbidden knowledge. And Mr. Raven—his librarian, or perhaps something else entirely. She said Raven wasn’t human. That he was the devil himself, guiding Sir Upward into dark arts. Then, one night, both vanished. Sir Upward was never seen again. But Raven? He lingered. Appearing now and then in the library. Never touching anything. Never speaking.

Some said he hadn’t died. Others insisted a dead man could return—but a man who lived for centuries? That was impossible.

“I’ve never heard he touched anything,” the butler said. “But perhaps… he considers the books his own.”

He paused. “The description she gave… it matches exactly.”

I stared at him. “And you believe this?”

He shrugged. “I don’t know what to believe. But I’ve seen the book move. And I’ve seen the door open when no one was near.”

I asked if he’d ever seen the fragment removed.

“No,” he said. “Never. I always thought it was fixed.”

He walked over, pulled at it.

It didn’t move.

We stood in silence, staring at the broken book—still in place, still immovable—while outside, the wind rustled through the trees, and the library held its breath.

For several days, nothing stirred—no sound, no sign of life beyond the quiet hum of dust settling in the corners of the library. It was perhaps a week later when the events I now recount began to unfold. The manuscript fragment had haunted my thoughts, its edges frayed and unreadable, locked in place by some unseen force. I had tried every method I could think of to free it, but nothing worked. The words remained sealed, as if bound not by thread or wax, but by time itself.

Yet another matter weighed on me: the books in the closet. Their silence felt oppressive, their presence too still. I had long intended to reorganize them, to assess their condition, for the air in that room carried a peculiar chill—one that seemed to whisper of neglect and forgotten things. One afternoon, this intention hardened into action. As I rose from my chair, preparing to begin, I saw him.

Not quite a man, not quite a shadow—only an impression, fleeting and sharp. A figure moved from the doorway of the closet toward the far end of the room: slight, stooped, draped in a shabby dress coat that trailed nearly to his heels. The tails parted slightly with each step, revealing thin legs clad in black stockings and wide, slipper-like shoes. He walked without sound, yet I felt his presence like a breath against my skin.

I followed.

It did not occur to me that I might be chasing a ghost. I knew, somehow, that I was following something real—though what, I could not say. He passed through the library into the hall, crossed to the base of the grand staircase, and climbed upward. I followed closely, silent, breath held. He ascended to the first floor, where the main rooms lay, then continued past them, down a wide corridor, to the foot of a narrower stair leading to the second story. Up that he went, and when I reached the top, I found myself in a part of the house I barely remembered—perhaps never truly known.

I had been an only child, raised in solitude, taken away by my guardian before I could learn the secrets of these halls. When I returned, just over a month ago, it was to reclaim what was mine—yet even then, I had not ventured beyond the familiar chambers. Now, guided by a phantom, I wandered deeper into the forgotten heart of the house.

Through passage after passage we moved, until we came to a door at the bottom of a winding wooden stair. We ascended together. Each step groaned beneath my weight, but my guide made no sound—not a creak, not a sigh. Midway up, I lost sight of him. At the top, there was no trace—no shape, no silhouette. The shadows were thick, but he was not among them. He had vanished.

The garret loomed above me—a vast, echoing space with great beams crisscrossing the ceiling, rafters like skeletal fingers reaching into the gloom. Doors stood half-hidden in the dark, corridors stretched into obscurity, and distant skylights let in dim, cobwebbed light. I stood still, breath catching in my throat. This place was mine, yet utterly alien. A strange thrill ran through me—fear, yes, but also wonder. It was uncharted territory, untouched by memory.

In the center of the garret stood a rough wooden enclosure, unpainted, weathered. Its door hung slightly ajar. Thinking perhaps Mr. Raven waited inside, I pushed it open.

The small chamber within was flooded with light—but not the kind that warms or welcomes. It was the light of abandonment: dull, hollow, as though the room itself regretted existing. Sunbeams, faint and dusty, cut through the air, illuminating motes that danced like tiny spirits. In the center stood a tall mirror, narrow and old-fashioned, its surface coated in grime. The frame was ebony, carved with subtle elegance. Perched atop it, a black eagle with wings spread wide clutched a golden chain in its beak—the chain ending in a single, heavy black sphere.

At first, I studied the mirror’s form rather than its reflection. Then, suddenly, I realized: it showed nothing. No image of the room, no glimpse of myself. The glass seemed to swallow the world around it.

A moment passed. Then—impossible as it sounds—I felt the wall dissolve. Not literally, but in sensation: as if reality bent, folded inward. And then I saw.

Before me lay a wild, desolate landscape. Rolling hills, broken and barren, stretched into the distance. Beyond them, a mountain range pierced the horizon. Closest to me, a flat expanse of moorland stretched out—gray, windswept, empty. The sky above was overcast, bruised with cloud.

I stepped forward, drawn by curiosity. My vision was poor, so I leaned closer to examine a stone in the foreground. That’s when I saw it: a raven, large and ancient, hopping slowly toward me. His feathers were deep purplish-black, streaked with silver-gray, like tarnished metal. He moved with solemn dignity, pecking at the ground as if searching for worms.

I did not flinch. To see a living creature in a painting—no, not a painting. This was no illusion. This was *real*.

I took another step, eager to get a better look. My foot caught on something—probably the edge of the mirror’s frame—and I stumbled forward.

And then—impact.

Nose to beak.

The raven blinked. I gasped.

And I was no longer in the garret.

I stood on a wind-swept heath, bare and endless, under a sky that promised rain. The air was cold, damp, alive. The mirror was gone. The house was gone. Only the moor, the raven, and the vast, indifferent land remained.

I had stepped through the glass.

And the world had changed.

I turned, and behind me—nothing was certain. The world blurred at the edges, like a dream seen through fog or mist. I could not tell where sky ended and earth began, nor whether the shapes before me were clouds or mountains. Only one thing was clear: I saw nothing I recognized.

Believing my eyes deceived me, I reached out, feeling blindly around, stepping forward and back, hoping that touch might anchor what sight had failed to define. But my hands found only air. No wall, no tree, no solid form. Nothing.

Then, from a distance, I noticed the raven. He stood still, watching me with an expression that seemed both curious and amused. A strange instinct made me turn to him—perhaps because he was the only living thing in sight. But then it struck me: how absurd to seek guidance from a bird?

I looked away, overwhelmed. Was I lost? Not just in space, but in reality itself? Had I crossed some threshold where the laws of nature, even the rules of thought, no longer applied? Could a man simply step beyond order and fall into chaos?

Yet I heard wind rustling through low plants. I felt the ground beneath my feet. And there—standing still—was the raven.

“How did I get here?” I whispered—then realized I’d spoken aloud.

“You came through the door,” replied a voice—dry, rough, yet strangely familiar.

I spun around, scanning the emptiness. No one. No shape. My heart sank. Was this madness? Had my mind begun to unravel?

But then I saw it—the raven, looking up at me as if waiting for my answer. The sun didn’t shine, yet he cast a shadow—and the shadow seemed part of him, as if it were not separate, but woven into his being.

I must ask you to bear with me. I am trying to make sense of something that may not be sensible. I was in a place—no, not a place, perhaps, but a condition—where time, matter, identity… everything I knew… ceased to hold meaning. The words I use are too small, too shaped by ordinary life, to carry what I mean. Each phrase feels like a sketch of a truth too vast to draw.

I fear I cannot explain it. I try to write, and already I see how inadequate my language is. The moment I attempt to describe it, the experience slips away—like a dream fading upon waking. What once seemed real shifts into something else, until even its essence becomes unrecognizable.

Still, I must go on.

The raven spoke again. And though he was a bird, I felt he deserved an answer—perhaps more than a man might.

“I did not come through any door,” I said.

“You did,” he insisted. “I saw you with my own ancient eyes!”

“But I saw no door!” I argued.

“Of course not,” he replied. “All the doors you’ve known were doors *in*. This one was a door *out*.”

He paused, thoughtful. “And the stranger thing for you will be—each time you walk out, you go deeper in.”

I stared. “Then tell me—where am I?”

“That,” he said, “is impossible. You don’t know what ‘where’ means. The only way to learn where you are is to begin making yourself at home.”

“But how can I do that when everything is strange?”

“By doing something.”

“What?”

“Anything. And soon. Because until you’re at home, you’ll find it as hard to get out as it was to get in.”

“I’ve found it far too easy to get in,” I muttered. “Once I’m out, I won’t return.”

“You stumbled in,” he said gently. “You may stumble out again. Whether it was unfortunate… remains to be seen.”

“Do you never go out?” I asked.

“When I please,” he answered. “But not often. Your world is half-baked—childish, proud, underdeveloped. It’s not fit for an old raven—at your service.”

I frowned. “So I’m wrong to think a man is superior to a bird?”

“Not necessarily. We don’t waste our minds on generalizations. We take each creature as we find them. Now—it’s my turn to ask you a question.”

“You have every right,” I said. “Because you *can* speak.”

“Good answer,” he nodded. “Now—tell me: who are you? If you know.”

I hesitated. “How should I not know? I am myself. I must know.”

“If you know you are yourself,” he said, “you know you are not someone else. But do you truly know you are yourself? Are you sure you’re not your father? Or—excuse me—your own fool? Who are you, pray?”

I froze.

I realized then—I didn’t know. I couldn’t say. The name I used in my world—what was it? I couldn’t recall. And even if I could, it meant nothing here. Names were just labels, and here, labels fell away.

I had almost forgotten—once, everyone had a name. But now, I was nameless. Unnamed. Unknown.

So I said nothing. It was wiser.

For this raven—this creature—saw beyond appearances. He saw into being.

“Look at me,” he said. “And tell me who I am.”

As he spoke, he turned his back.

And suddenly, I knew.

He was no longer a bird.

He was a man—tall, thin, stooped, wearing a long black tailcoat. Then, in a breath, he turned again—and I saw the raven.

“I’ve seen you before,” I said, feeling foolish.

“How can you say that?” he asked. “You saw me from behind.”

“Have you ever seen yourself from behind?” I countered. “You’ve never seen yourself at all.”

“Then tell me now—who am I?”

“I beg your pardon,” I said. “I believe you were once the librarian of our house. But beyond that… I don’t know.”

“Why apologize?”

“Because I called you a raven.”

“You did no harm,” he said. “To call me a raven—or think me one—is to acknowledge my existence. That is all any being can demand of another.”

He paused.

“Let me give you a lesson: No one can say they are themselves—until first they know that they *are*, and then what *themselves* means. In fact—nobody is himself. And himself is nobody. There’s more in it than you see now—but not more than you need to see.”

He turned to leave. As he walked, I saw the librarian again—not changed, but as if he had simply taken up his shadow.

It sounds mad. But I cannot deny it.

I watched him go—until he vanished. Not into distance, perhaps, but into the heather. Or maybe into silence.

Was I dead? Did I not know it?

Was this the afterlife? The world beyond death?

And how was I to find myself at home?

The raven had said: *Do something.*

But what could I do here? And would that make me someone?

Because now—alas—I was nobody.

I followed the path he took. Slowly, I moved forward.

Soon, I saw a forest—tall, slender pines, standing like sentinels. The scent of pine filled the air. I hurried toward it, eager to disappear into its shadows.

At last, deep within the twilight, I saw something between two trees—a shimmering shape, without color, like heat rising from sunbaked ground, or the vibration of a struck string.

I approached. But as I drew near, it faded. When I stood beside it, I saw nothing—only the trees beyond, their forms uncertain, wavering.

I tried to pass between the trunks.

A jolt—then I stumbled, fell.

When I rose, I stood before the wooden wall of the garret chamber.

I turned.

There was the mirror.

And perched upon it—the black eagle—just as if it had landed moments ago.

Terror seized me.

I ran.

Outside the chamber, the garret spaces looked uncanny. They seemed to have been waiting—waiting for something. It had come. And now, they waited again.

On the winding stair, a shudder ran through me. The house had changed. It felt alive. Watching. Something would leap from behind!

I darted down, hit the wall, fell. Rose. Ran.

On the next floor, I lost my way. I passed the same corridors twice before finding the stairwell.

At the top of the great staircase, I steadied myself. I sat, breathing hard.

No. Never again. I would never climb that final, terrible stair.

The garret above—its presence weighed on the whole house. It sat upon it, heavy, threatening to crush me out.

It was the brain of the building—brooding, full of unseen dwellers. One might appear at any moment in the library where I sat.

I was nowhere safe.

I would sell this place. Let it go.

I would buy a crag in Switzerland—build a simple wooden house, one story high, no garret, no hidden rooms. Guarded by ancient peaks that would send down only rocks, not spirits.

I knew my thoughts were foolish. I even sensed a quiet mockery in them—like a whisper from the raven.

But then—his croak echoed again in my mind.

*If I don’t know my own garret,* I thought, *how can I trust my own brain?*

What is it generating now? What thought will it offer tomorrow? Next month? A year from now?

What lies at the core of my mind?

What is behind my *thinking*?

Am I even there?

Who—what—am I?

I could not answer. Not then. Not now.

I stood, trembling.

I crossed the room to the masked door—the one with the book protruding, half-hidden among soulless, faceless volumes.

It seemed to beckon.

I knelt. Opened it as far as possible.

Nothing.

I lit a taper.

Peered in—like gazing into the jaws of something reluctant.

The manuscript was verse.

Lines broken—beginnings on one page, endings on the other. I could not read a single complete line.

Yet the words—some fragments, some half-sentences—stirred something deep inside.

Not just emotion. Something deeper. Like the scent of an idea. A longing—unfamiliar, intense—to understand what the poem might hold—even in ruin.

I copied a few lines, tried to reconstruct them.

Failed.

Only weariness remained.

That night, I slept deeply—without dreams.

In the morning, the horror of the garret was gone.

But I knew—something had changed.

And I was not the same.

The sun blazed overhead, fierce and unrelenting, but I sensed the sky was holding its breath—something was coming. I reached for my milky sapphire ring, turning it in my palm to catch the reflection of the star within. It shimmered faintly, blurred at the edges, as if veiled by mist. Not a good sign.

I rose from the breakfast table and walked to the window, pressing my forehead against the cool glass. Outside, the lawn glistened with rain that had fallen during the night. A thrush pecked at a snail shell, struggling to crack it open. I rotated the ring again, hoping for clarity, when suddenly—out of the clouded blue—I saw it: a sharp, black eye staring back at me from within the stone.

My breath caught. I dropped the ring. When I picked it up, the eye was gone. At the same moment, the sun vanished behind a thick shroud of vapor. The sky darkened swiftly, the air grew heavy and still, then—without warning—a gust tore through the garden. Lightning split the clouds, followed by a single, thunderous crack. Rain fell in sheets, pounding the earth like a thousand drums.

I opened the window, stepping onto the sill, watching the downpour. And then I saw it: a raven walking across the soaked grass, unhurried, dignified, utterly indifferent to the storm. He moved with purpose, his feathers sleek and black as midnight.

I knew who he was. My heart quickened. I was on the ground floor—safe, for now. But something told me that safety was temporary.

He approached steadily, bowed deeply, then leapt with a flicker of wings onto the windowsill. Without hesitation, he stepped inside, crossed the room, and headed toward a small door tucked between two ancient wings of the house.

I followed, half-expecting him to vanish into the library or climb the stairs. But he didn’t. He stopped before a hidden door leading to a tiny courtyard overgrown with ivy. I hurried to open it for him.

He stepped out onto the porch, stood motionless, and gazed upward at the rain, which now fell in a thin, endless curtain. The second flash lit the sky, followed by a long roll of thunder. He turned his head slowly, eyes locking with mine.

“You hear that?” he seemed to say.

Then he looked back at the storm, nodding slightly, as if pleased.

“Fine weather for the worms, Mr. Raven,” I said, almost without thinking.

“Yes,” he croaked, voice familiar and dry. “The ground will be soft for them to come out and go in. It must be a grand time on the steppes of Uranus.” He glanced upward. “I believe it’s raining there too—was all last week.”

“Why would that make it grand?”

“Because the creatures there are all burrowers—like moles and field mice here. They’ll be for ages to come.”

“How do you know?”

“Any fool who’s been there would know. It’s a sight—until you get used to it—when the earth heaves and something emerges. You might think it a hairy elephant or a deinotherium—but none of them resemble anything we’ve seen. I nearly cried the first time I saw the dry-bog-serpent crawl out—such a head! Such a mane! Such eyes!”

He paused. “But the rain is ending. It’ll stop right after the next clap.” And as if summoned, lightning flashed—then thunder boomed. Instantly, the rain ceased.

“Now we should be going,” the raven said, stepping forward.

“Going where?” I asked.

“Where we have to go. You didn’t really think you’d found home? I told you—no coming and going until you’re truly home.”

“I don’t want to go.”

“That doesn’t matter—much. This is the way.”

“I’m quite content here.”

“You think so. But you’re not. Come along.”

He hopped off the porch and waited.

“I won longer leave the house today,” I insisted.

“You will come into the garden,” he replied.

I gave in—just enough. I stepped outside.

The sun broke through, dazzling. Raindrops sparkled on the grass like scattered diamonds. The raven walked ahead, unfazed.

“You’ll wet your feet!” I called.

“And mire my beak,” he answered, plunging his head into the sod. He pulled out a wriggling red worm, tossed it high into the air.

It unfolded wings—gorgeous, iridescent red and black—and soared away.

“Tut! Tut!” I exclaimed. “You’re mistaken, Mr. Raven. Worms aren’t butterfly larvae!”

“Never mind,” he croaked. “It’ll do for once. I’m not much of a reader these days. I’m sexton at a certain cemetery—no matter where. Can’t keep the spade still, can I? When there’s no one to bury, I dig something up. Only—mind what you unearth before you set it free. No creature should forget where it came from.”

“Why?” I asked.

“Because it grows proud. Ceases to recognize its superiors. No man knows when he’s making a fool of himself.”

“Where do worms come from?” he suddenly asked, as if curious.

“From the earth,” I said. “As you just showed.”

“Last time,” he replied. “But they couldn’t have come from it first—for that never returns.”

I looked up. A small dark cloud hovered, edged in crimson—like sunset blood.

“The sun isn’t setting!” I gasped.

“Oh, no,” he said. “That red belongs to the worm.”

“You see what happens when creatures forget their origin!”

“It’s well if they rise higher, grow larger,” he countered. “And I only teach them to remember.”

“Would you have the sky full of winged worms?”

“That’s the sexton’s duty. If only the rest of the clergy understood it!”

Again, he dug—beak deep into the turf—and pulled out another worm. He threw it aloft. It spread its wings and vanished into the sky.

I turned, horrified. I had just sworn I wouldn’t leave the house. Yet here I was—outside, in a strange land.

“What right have you to treat me this way?” I demanded. “Am I not a free agent?”

“A man is as free as he chooses to be,” the raven replied. “No more, no less.”

“You have no right to force me!”

“When you have a will, you’ll find no one can.”

“You wrong me in the essence of my individuality!”

“If you were an individual, I couldn’t. But you’re only beginning to become one.”

Around me, a pine forest stretched endlessly. I scanned the shadows, searching for any sign—any glimmer—that might lead me home. But how could I call that house *home* when every door led outward, every window revealed nothing but vastness, and even the garden refused to stay contained?

I must have looked shaken.

“Perhaps this will comfort you,” the raven said. “You haven’t left your house. Nor has your house left you. But it cannot contain you. You do not inhabit it.”

“I don’t understand. Where am I?”

“In the region of seven dimensions,” he answered, his throat rumbling, tail fluttering. “Follow me carefully. Don’t hurt anyone.”

“There’s no one to hurt but yourself, Mr. Raven! Honestly, I’d rather hurt you!”

“That you see no one is the danger. But look—see that large tree to your left, about thirty yards away?”

“Of course I do. Why wouldn’t I?”

“Ten minutes ago, you didn’t see it. Now you don’t know where it stands.”

“I do.”

“Where do you think it stands?”

“Right there—where you know it is.”

“Where is ‘there’?”

“You’re bothering me with nonsense!”

“Ten minutes ago, you didn’t see that tree. Now you don shit know where it is.”

“Nonsense!”

“It stands on the hearth of your kitchen,” he said calmly. “Grows straight up the chimney.”

“I know you’re mocking me!”

“Was I mocking you when you saw me in your star-sapphire yesterday?”

“That was this morning—only an hour ago!”

“I’ve been widening your horizon longer than that, Mr. Vane. But never mind.”

“You mean you’ve been fooling me!”

“No one can fool you but yourself.”

“And I refuse to do it.”

“You mistake.”

“How?”

“In refusing to accept what is true. You make yourself a fool by denying truth—and for that, you’ll punish yourself.”

“How?”

“By believing lies.”

“So if I walk to the other side of that tree, I’ll walk through the kitchen fire?”

“Certainly. First, you’d pass through the lady at the piano in the breakfast room. That rosebush is near her. You’d startle her terribly.”

“There’s no lady in the house!”

“Is your housekeeper not a lady? In a certain world, all are servants. Livery is one and infinite.”

“She can’t play piano.”

“Her niece can. She’s there—a gifted girl, a fine musician.”

“I don’t hear music,” I said sharply.

“Can’t you smell it? Those wild hyacinths—long-headed—rest among the piano strings. They give her playing its sweetness. Pardon me—I forgot you’re deaf.”

“Two things can’t occupy the same space at once!”

“Can they not? I didn’t know. I recall now—your people teach that. A great mistake—one of the worst ever made by a wiseacre. Only a man of the world could say such a thing.”

“You’re a librarian,” I snapped. “And you talk nonsense!”

“Plainly, I didn’t read many books. I went through all in your library—once—and came out the other side no wiser. I was a bookworm then. But when I woke, I was among butterflies. I’ve given up reading—since becoming sexton. Ah! I smell Grieg’s Wedding March in those rose petals!”

I walked to the bush, strained to listen. Nothing. But the scent—rose, yes—but layered with something else, something new, musical. Like a melody trapped in perfume.

When I looked up, the raven stood beside me.

“Mr. Raven,” I said, “forgive me. I was rude. Will you show me the way home? I have an appointment with my bailiff. One must not break faith with servants.”

“You cannot break what was broken days ago,” he said.

“Please—show me the way.”

“I cannot. To return, you must pass through yourself. And no man can show another that path.”

I stood silent. Entreaty was useless. My fate was sealed.

So—how would I live in a world where the rules were unknown? Where trees grew through chimneys and worms became birds? It would be strange. Dangerous. But also… adventurous.

And whether I returned or not, I would know two worlds. Two lives. That alone was worth something.

I had done nothing in my old life to justify my existence. That world had gained nothing from me. Here—perhaps I could earn my place. Find my bread. Or at least learn to survive.

And since I hadn’t chosen to come here, perhaps I deserved care here as much as I’d received in the world I’d left. After all, I hadn’t chosen to be born into that world either—and yet I’d inherited a fortune.

So if that world claimed me because I ate, and could eat again—this world had a claim too. Because I must eat. And in return, it would demand something from me.

“There is no hurry,” the raven said, watching me. “We don’t measure time here. But the sooner you begin, the better. I will take you to my wife.”

“Thank you. Let us go.”

And so he led the way—into the forest, into the unknown.

I followed him deeper into the pine forest, where silence clung like mist between the trees. We walked in quiet companionship, wrapped in the hushed reverence of ancient woodlands. The air grew thick with memory, and the shadows stretched long and deep beneath the canopy. As we moved forward, the trees grew larger—older, more solitary, their forms twisted by time, some so gnarled they seemed to wear faces carved by centuries.

Then the forest thinned. A clearing opened ahead, edged with heathland stretching toward the horizon.

“You see that hawthorn?” my guide said at last, his beak pointing sharply.

I looked. “I see an old man,” I replied, “bent and white-headed, standing alone.”

“Look again,” he said. “It is a hawthorn.”

“It’s certainly ancient,” I conceded, “but it doesn’t bloom this time of year.”

“The hawthorn blooms when it chooses,” he answered. “That tree stands in the ruins of the church on your home farm. You were going to give orders to the bailiff about its graveyard, weren’t you—the morning of the thunderstorm?”

“I was,” I admitted. “I told him I wanted the whole place turned into a wild rose garden. No plough ever to come within three yards.”

He paused, as if listening to something beyond sound. Then he whispered: “Listen.”

I strained my ears. Was it the wind? Or was it music—faint, distant, like echoes from a forgotten festival? Or was it only my mind playing tricks?

“They still go there,” the raven murmured.

“Who goes? And where?” I asked.

“Those who once prayed there,” he said. “They return. But not for long, I think.”

“And why do they go now?”

“They need one another to shape their thoughts, to hatch their feelings. So they gather, speak, sing—and then, they say, a great idea rises from their hearts like a ship floating free from the river at high tide.”

“Do they pray too?”

“No. They’ve learned that prayer lives best in silence—within the heart itself. Some people are always praying. But look! Look!” He pointed upward.

A snow-white pigeon soared into the sky, wings beating fast, climbing an invisible spiral into the light. Sunlight shimmered across its feathers like liquid silver.

“I see a pigeon,” I said.

“Of course you do,” the raven replied. “But I see a prayer.” He paused. “I wonder whose heart gave birth to that dove. Someone may have awakened in my cemetery.”

“How can a pigeon be a prayer?” I asked. “I understand it might symbolize one—but a living bird born from a heart?”

“It must puzzle you,” he said. “And rightly so.”

“A prayer is spiritual,” I insisted. “A thought. An idea.”

“True,” he said. “But if you knew other worlds, you’d understand your own better. When a heart is truly alive, it gives birth to living thoughts. There is one heart whose dreams are lives, whose every idea walks and breathes. Some prayers are heavy burdens lifted and dropped again. Others rise in living form—the nearest shape they can take. All things alive began as thoughts. That is why they can serve those who think. When someone says to the Great Thinker, ‘Here is one of your thoughts—I am thinking it now,’ that is prayer—a word from a small heart to the great one.”

He gestured downward this time, to the base of a granite boulder.

I looked—and saw a flower.

I had never seen anything like it. Delicate, tender, full of quiet courage. It reminded me of an anemone, but paler—rose-colored, with a golden center that glowed as if lit from within. Its scent was unlike any I’d known—both new and familiar, like the first breath of dawn on a world both ancient and unborn.

“That is a prayer-flower,” the raven said.

“I’ve never seen such a thing,” I whispered.

“There is no other like it,” he replied. “No two prayer-flowers are alike.”

“How do you know it’s a prayer-flower?”

“By its expression,” he said. “More than that, I cannot tell you. If you recognize it, you recognize it. If not, you don’t.”

“Could you teach me to see one?”

“I could not. Even if I could, what would you gain? You wouldn’t know it through your own eyes or your own soul. Why name a thing when you don’t know it? The universe’s work is to make you so foolish that you finally see yourself as fool—and thus begin to grow wise.”

Still, I knew this flower was different. I felt it in my bones. And in that knowing, I sensed a shadow of the prayer that had shaped it—an echo of a heart speaking softly to the world.

Awe washed over me. Not just at the flower, but at the idea: that somewhere, a heart was listening—not to words, but to beauty, to life, to the silent song of creation.

We had walked for a long while across a barren moorland, where dry grasses and patches of moss clung to the rocky ground like remnants of forgotten life. The sun hung low in the sky, veiled by a thick gray cloud, casting no warmth, only a dim, solemn light. The wind cut through me with an unnatural chill, as if it came not from earth but from some distant, eternal night.

“Here we are at last!” said the raven.

“What a journey!” I replied. “It feels like we’ve crossed worlds.”

“In half the time,” he mused, “I could have flown to Paradise and visited my cousin—the one who never returned from Noah’s ark! Dear, dear, it’s almost winter already.”

“Winter?” I exclaimed. “It seems like just yesterday we left home!”

“That’s because we’ve moved so fast,” he said. “In your world, you’re bound by gravity—like a plumb-line that won’t let go. But here? Here the world spins beneath your feet, and time bends to will. And now—here is my wife’s cottage. She’s kind enough to let me live here, though I call it the sexton’s cottage.”

“But where is your churchyard?” I asked, scanning the endless heath. “Where are the graves?”

The raven stretched his neck, extended his beak horizontally, and slowly turned it in a full circle, silent and still. I followed the motion with my eyes—and then I understood.

There was no church. No tombstones. No walls. Only the wind sweeping over the endless plain, and beyond it, the low, starless horizon.

And yet—it *was* a cemetery. A vast, unbounded burial ground. Wherever the wind blew, there lay the dead. The entire heath was a graveyard. The raven was its keeper—its sexton—overseer of all things laid aside, all things ended. He ruled the quiet of existence itself.

I stood in the grave of the universe. Its boundaries were the open land, its wall the hazy edge of the sky. I had left behind spring, summer, autumn, and sunlight. I had come to winter—the season that waited for me. I had begun my journey in youth, and already, it seemed, I had reached its end.

But I was wrong. For this place held more than one season. Winter slept beneath the ice, wrapped in silence; Spring stirred at dawn, soft and childlike; Summer blazed at noon in radiant glory; and Autumn crept in during the slow fade of afternoon, dying softly into the misty, ghostly dark.

As we neared the cottage, the sun plunged down the western slope, vanishing before we reached the door. In that instant, cold seized me—not just air, but something solid, tangible, like a hand clutching my bones. I staggered forward, crossing the threshold as if escaping death itself.

A gust surged across the moor, slamming against the door just as I shut it. Then—silence. Complete stillness.

Inside, a single candle burned on a plain wooden table. Near the wall stood what looked like a coffin lid, propped upright. But it opened—revealing a door—and from it stepped a woman.

She wore white—pure, untouched white, like snow fresh fallen. Her face was pale, yet not cold. It glowed with a quiet warmth, as if light lived within her skin. Her features were flawless, but her eyes… her eyes drew every thought away from the rest. They were large, deep, dark—so dark they seemed to hold entire galaxies. Each pupil was a night sky, filled with stars that flickered and danced. Around them, the iris curled like twilight, eternal and serene.

Her gaze held not sorrow, nor joy—but presence. A living force. As if she were not merely seeing me, but *knowing* me. I felt exposed, yet safe. Her eyes were not human—they were ancient, divine. They must have come straight from God’s own vision.

“Here is Mr. Vane, wife,” said the raven.

“He is welcome,” she answered in a voice low, rich, and gentle—as if music had been buried deep inside her and now rose to the surface.

I stared, unable to speak.

“I knew you’d be glad to see him,” the raven added.

She stood near the doorway, unmoving, watching me. “Will he sleep?” she asked.

“I fear not,” the raven replied. “He is neither weary nor burdened.”

“Then why bring him?”

“I have my fears it may prove too soon.”

I hesitated, unease rising in my chest. “Surely a man must do a day’s work first!” I said.

She turned to the raven. “He has not yet learned that the day begins with sleep,” she said. “Tell him—he must rest before he can act.”

“Men,” the raven replied, “think so much of what they’ve done that they fall asleep upon it. They cannot empty an egg without turning into the shell and lying down.”

His words made me look again—at the raven. But this time, I saw not a bird.

I saw the librarian.

The same slender man in the worn black coat, broad-shouldered, tail long. I had seen only his back before. Now, for the first time, I saw his face.

It was thin, almost translucent, revealing the shape of the skull beneath. Yet it was alive—more alive than any face I’d ever seen. His pale blue eyes held depth, kindness, sorrow—a haze around them as if he had wept often. But they also sparkled with intelligence, warmth, and humor.

“You knew I wasn’t a raven,” he said, smiling.

“I knew you were Mr. Raven,” I replied. “But I thought you were a bird.”

“What made you think that?”

“You looked like a raven. I saw you dig worms from the soil with your beak.”

“And then?”

“You tossed them into the air.”

“And then?”

“They became butterflies—and flew away.”

“Have you ever seen a raven do that?” he asked.

“No,” I said. “But you did.”

“I told you—I am a sexton.”

“Does a sexton toss worms into the air and turn them into butterflies?”

“Yes.”

“I’ve never seen one do it.”

“You saw me do it.”

He paused. “And I am still your librarian. I was never dismissed. Never gave up the post. Now I serve here too.”

“But you said you were a sexton.”

“I am. It’s much the same work. Unless you are a true sexton, books are nothing but dead bodies. A library is merely a catacomb.”

“You bewilder me,” I said.

“That’s all right.”

For a moment, silence settled. The woman stood motionless, like a statue carved from moonlight, her eyes fixed on me.

Then the sexton spoke again. “Sometimes,” he said, “it’s more convenient to show your bird-self. Everyone—yes, everyone—has many selves: beast, bird, fish, serpent… even tree and crystal. All of them fight to rise to the surface. You can tell a man’s nature by which form appears most often.”

He turned to his wife. I studied him closely. He was taller than before, standing straighter, more composed. His face was pale, yes—but noble. His nose framed the beak that had retreated within; his lips were thin, colorless, yet curved with beauty. Around them, a faint smile quivered—full of love, pity, and quiet amusement.

“We need food and drink, wife,” he said. “We’ve traveled far.”

“You know,” she replied gently, “we can give only to those who ask.”

Her eyes turned to me—unblinking, luminous.

“Please give me something to eat, Mrs. Raven,” I said. “And something—anything—to quench my thirst.”

“Your thirst must grow greater before you receive what will truly quench it,” she said. “But what I can give, I will.”

She went to a cupboard in the wall, brought out bread and wine, and placed them on the table. We sat. The meal was simple—but perfect. As I ate, the hunger faded, replaced by a deeper sensation. The anxiety dissolved. Expectation took its place. My eyelids grew heavy. I felt weary for the first time.

“I haven’t earned this food or this sleep, Mrs. Raven,” I said. “But you’ve given me the one freely. May I now ask for the other? I need it.”

“Sleep,” said the sexton, “is too precious to be earned. It must be given and accepted. It is a necessity, not a reward.”

He paused. “But this house is not meant for passing travelers. Not for mere overnight rest.”

At that moment, a small black cat leapt onto his lap. He patted it softly—like one would soothe a child to sleep. To me, it looked like he was pressing earth over a grave—gently, lovingly, with a lullaby in his soul.

“There’s one of Mara’s kittens,” he said to his wife. “Will you feed it and put it out? She might want it.”

She took the kitten, gave it a piece of bread, and left with it, closing the door behind her.

“How, then, should I use your hospitality?” I asked.

“By accepting it fully,” he said.

“I don’t understand.”

“In this house,” he explained, “no one wakes on their own.”

“Why not?”

“Because no one, anywhere, ever wakes on their own. You cannot wake yourself any more than you can create yourself.”

“Then—will you or Mrs. Raven wake me?”

“We cannot.”

“Then how dare I sleep?” I asked, trembling.

“If you wish to rest here,” he said, “you must not worry about waking. You must sleep completely. Wholeheartedly. Without reservation.”

My heart sank.

He looked at me—his eyes saying, *Will you trust me?*

I met his gaze. “I will,” I whispered.

“Then come,” he said. “I’ll show you your bed.”

As we rose, the woman returned. She picked up the candle, turned toward the inner door, and led the way. I followed close behind her. The sexton came after, silent and watchful.

The moment I crossed the threshold, a chill like that of a frozen vault seized me—cold so deep it seemed to freeze the breath in my lungs. The door clicked shut behind us, sealing us into silence. The sexton murmured something to his wife, and she turned toward us with a change so profound it startled me. Her eyes—once ordinary—now blazed with an inner light, as though they had held too much radiance and overflowed, flooding her face with a luminous beauty. She looked like a vision from beyond time, a being touched by eternity. Her hands, pale and translucent, shimmered faintly, each fingernail glowing like moonstone beneath the candle’s flicker. It was overwhelming; I felt relieved when she turned away.

But the candlelight barely reached beyond our feet. At first, the room was swallowed in shadow. Then, slowly, it caught on something just above the floor—a glimmering shape raised slightly from the ground. A bed? Could any living thing endure such cold and rest here? And if so, why would it ever wake?

Beyond that, other faint reflections danced in the darkness. As we stepped forward, the glow revealed more: one after another, figures lay upon white-draped couches, arranged in long, silent aisles. Each form was still, head turned toward the path we walked. I could not tell gender or age—only that they were all motionless, wrapped in sheets like snowfall.

My heart slowed. Dread settled over me like frost. We moved through row after row, endless corridors of slumbering forms stretching into infinity. They vanished into the shadows, disappearing into the depths of what might have been a cathedral, a mausoleum, or some vast temple of repose. Was this where I was meant to lie? To sleep among those who did not stir, never to be roused? Was this the sexton’s library? Were these his books—living volumes bound in flesh and memory?

“Just one of the cellars I’m assigned to watch,” Mr. Raven whispered, as if afraid to disturb the silence. “Much wine is stored here to age… But it’s dark for a stranger.”

“The moon is rising,” his wife said softly, her voice clear and sweet, yet carrying the weight of ancient sorrow, long buried but not forgotten.

As she spoke, the moon broke through a gap in the wall. Instantly, a thousand points of silver light flared across the chamber—each sheet, each face, each still figure catching its glow. Yet even then, I could not see the edges of the place. The aisles stretched endlessly, narrowing into the distance, lined with solitary sleepers, each alone in their eternal repose.

At first, I thought them dead. But soon I realized—this was deeper than death. This was not cessation. It was suspension. A state beyond understanding.

The moon climbed higher, casting blue-white beams through scattered openings. Where it fell, the sheets gleamed like ice, and the faces beneath appeared almost spectral—pale, serene, untouched by time. Yet none were alike. Some bore the marks of pain, though faint now, as if grief had once burned there but was now cooled into peace. Others wore no trace of suffering at all—just quiet acceptance.

One man lay with dark beard flowing like water from a glacier, his brow smooth as marble, lips parted only by the ghost of anguish. Next to him, a young woman—so beautiful it hurt to look—her features calm, eyes closed, yet her expression still held the echo of farewell, not despair. No sign of illness, no mark of sorrow. Pain had slept here, and never awakened.

Children rested beside adults, their small forms peaceful. But not one infant—no newborns, no infants whose lives had barely begun. Only those who had lived, loved, suffered, and then… paused.

The most striking of all was a woman with silver hair, her face noble and composed—not submissive, but certain. There was no fear, no regret, only a deep, unshakable belief that all had come to pass as it should. On others, the scars remained—faint lines of war, loss, sorrow—but even these bore a plea: *Pardon me—I died yesterday.* Or: *I died only a century ago.*

Some, I knew, had lain here longer than centuries. Not by count of years, but by the stillness in their bones, the depth of their silence. Something older than memory resided in them. Something I could not name.

We came at last to three empty beds. Beyond them, a woman lay—still beautiful, past her prime. One arm rested outside the sheet, palm upward. In its center, a dark stain. Beside her, a man of middle age, strong and broad-shouldered, his hand clenched as if gripping a sword. He looked like a warrior fallen in battle—perhaps defending truth itself.

“Would you hold the candle closer, wife?” the sexton whispered, bending low.

“It heals well,” he murmured, “the nail found nothing to harm her.”

I finally dared to speak. “Are they… dead?”

“I cannot answer that,” he replied, voice low. “I’ve nearly forgotten what ‘dead’ means in your world. If I said someone was dead, my wife would understand one thing—you another. This is merely one of my treasure vaults. Not all are buried underground. Out there on the moor, they lie thick as autumn leaves after the first storm—thick as if the great white rose of heaven had shed its petals across the land. All night, the moon reads their faces—and smiles.”

“But why leave them exposed to the moonlight? Won’t it rot them?”

“Our moon,” he said, “is not like yours—the burnt-out cinder of a dead world. Hers does not decay. She preserves. You see, we do not bury deeply. Our sexton lays the dead upon the earth, not beneath it. Your people pile stones atop them, as if to keep them down. I wait for the hour to ring the resurrection bell—and wake those still asleep. Your sexton watches the clock to call the dead to church. I listen for the cock on the spire to crow: *‘Awake, thou that sleepest, and arise from the dead!’*”

For a moment, I thought him mad—an insane parson lost in delusion. But how could I escape? I was trapped in a realm larger than thought. Here, space bent. Two things could occupy the same place. Why not twenty? A thousand? I dared not follow that line further.

“You seem to perceive differences in your dead that I cannot,” I ventured.

“None of these,” he said, “are truly dead. Some have only just begun to die—to awaken. Others had already begun to live before they came to us. When they are fully dead—when they truly cease—then they will rise and go. Every night, some wake and leave. But I won’t say more. My words only confuse.”

Then he pointed to one of the empty couches. “This one has waited for you.”

“Why this one?” I asked, trembling. “Why not another?”

“For reasons you’ll be glad to know one day.”

“And when?”

“When you wake.”

“But these are all dead,” I protested. “And I am alive!”

“Not nearly enough,” he replied with a smile. “Blessed be the true life—where the pauses between beats are not death.”

“The place is too cold to sleep,” I said.

“Do they feel it?” he asked.

“They sleep well—or will soon. Cold heals them. Their wounds close under its touch. Do not be afraid, Mr. Vane. Turn your back on fear. Face whatever comes. Surrender to the night—and you shall rest. Harm will not touch you. But a good you cannot foresee.”

He stood beside the couch. His wife stood at the foot, candle in hand. Her eyes still shone, but her face had returned to stillness—no longer radiant, only pure, white, and quiet.

“Would they make my bedroom a charnel house?” I cried. “I will not! I’ll sleep out on the heath—it can’t be colder there!”

“Ah,” said the librarian, “but the dead are there too—*thick as autumnal leaves that strow the brooks in Vallombrosa.*”

“I WILL NOT!” I shouted, and in the suffocating dark, the two figures glowed like spirits guarding the sleeping.

They did not answer. Just stood—silent, sorrowful—watching each other.

“Be at ease,” the sexton said gently to his wife. “We watch the flock of the great shepherd.”

Then he turned to me. “Did you not find the air pure when you entered?”

“Yes,” I said, “but oh, so cold.”

“Then know,” he said, voice firm, “that you—who call yourself alive—have brought the scent of death into this chamber. Its air will not heal the sleepers until you are gone.”

They walked deeper into the hall. I was left alone—bathed in moonlight, surrounded by the silent dead.

I turned to flee.

How far it seemed—back through the endless rows. At first, anger burned hotter than fear. But as I calmed, the shapes grew more terrible. I began to run—wildly, recklessly—bursting through the door and slamming it shut behind me.

It closed without sound.

Darkness.

I fumbled along the wall, found another door, opened it—and saw the dim glow of a lamp.

I stood in my library. The masked door was still open. Had I dreamed it all? Or had I escaped into a dream?

Which was real—the world I’d just left, or this one?

Could both exist—separate, yet overlapping?

I collapsed onto a couch and fell into sleep.

In the east, a small window let in the first rays of dawn. By this season, the sun struck a mirror, which reflected the light onto the masked door.

When I woke, the golden beam was there—drawing my gaze.

I sprang up, knowing what lay beyond.

I threw open the door.

Light—like a hunting hound—darted into the closet and leapt onto the gilded edges of a large book.

“What fool,” I muttered, “has placed this book backward?”

But the reflection bounced again—onto a nest of drawers in the corner. One was half-open.

“More meddling,” I sighed, stepping to close it.

Inside: old papers, piled high. The drawer refused to shut.

I pulled out the topmost. My father’s handwriting. Long. Important.

The first few words made my blood quicken.

I carried it to the library, sat in the western window, and read what followed.

I stand before the page, trembling not from fear but from wonder. The sun blazes above, gilding the sky like molten gold; beneath it, the sea stretches in endless blue, calm and ancient. This world—this familiar world of trees, birds, breath, and memory—is all I have ever known. Yet now I know it is only a veil, thin as mist, hiding truths far older and stranger than any myth. At any moment, that veil might lift—and what lies beyond could shatter everything I believe.

It was shortly after my father’s death that I found myself sitting alone in the library. The air was still, thick with dust and silence. My gaze drifted to the portrait hanging among the books—a stern-faced man in dark clothing, whom I had always assumed to be some distant ancestor. I wished I knew more about him. Then, distracted, I reached for a book on the shelf, opened it, and began reading.

When I looked up again, he was there—not walking toward me from the door, but from between me and the portrait.

A thin man, pale as ash, dressed in a black coat worn threadbare at the edges. His face was sharp, his eyes bright with intelligence, and his nose—long, curved, and pointed—reminded me instantly of the jug my sisters used to call Mr. Crow.

“Finding myself near you, Mr. Vane,” he said, voice low and measured, “I took the liberty of calling.”

I blinked. “You’re…?”

“Raven,” he replied, smiling faintly. “Though once upon a time, they called me by another name. But you nearly guessed it—your family has a gift for intuition.”

I frowned. “I don’t recall seeing you before.”

“Oh, but you have,” he said. “Once. When you were a child. In this very room.”

I couldn’t place him. Not clearly. But something in his presence stirred a half-remembered dream.

“You’re saying you were here?” I asked.

“I was,” he said. “And I’ve been here many times since. Though your grandfather—your father’s father—did not care for my company. He never met me. But your great-grandfather? Sir Up’ard, they called him—though no one knows why—was my closest friend.”

My breath caught. “You knew him?”

“I did,” Raven said softly. “And he taught me things no one else could have. Things that changed how I saw the world—and worlds beyond it.”

He paused, then continued: “That closet there”—he gestured toward a small, locked chamber near the back—“held his private collection. A hundred manuscripts, untouched by print. One morning, I sat inside, cataloguing them, when he appeared in the doorway. ‘Come,’ he said. I laid down my pen and followed him—across the hall, down a steep stair, through a passage beneath the house, until we reached a tower he had built in secret. It stood high, hidden, with a single room at the top. The door had a lock so massive it seemed made for giants—but he opened it with a key no bigger than a needle.”

I leaned forward. “What happened?”

“He stepped inside,” Raven said. “And then—before my eyes—he began to shrink. Not just in size, but in presence. Like smoke drawn into wind. Within moments, he was no longer a man, but a speck—then nothing. And beyond him, I saw mountains—blue, clear against a sky of deeper blue. I recognized the land. I had been there before. But never by such a path.”

He looked at me then, his eyes piercing. “Years later, I passed on what he taught me to one of his descendants. And even now, when I wish to return home, I use your house. I must confess—I’ve done so without your permission. But I’ve earned a right of passage, if you will allow it.”

I stared. “So you go through my house… to another world?”

He smiled. “Not *through* it. Into it. That is the difference. Space is not a wall—it is a door.”

I shook my head. “This is madness.”

“Is it?” he said gently. “Or is it simply truth you have not yet learned to see?”

I hesitated. “Then tell me—how do you pass?”

“There is a door,” he said. “One step through it, and you are elsewhere. Not across distance, but across reality.”

“Another world?” I whispered.

“Yes,” he said. “A world where laws differ. Where light behaves differently. Where thought shapes matter in ways we cannot yet imagine. Morals? They remain constant. But physics? Mind? Perception? All change.”

I laughed, though it was not meant to mock. “You speak as if you’ve seen it.”

“I have,” he said. “And I can show you.”

He rose, walked calmly across the room, and ascended the stairs to the garret. I followed, watching his back—the long, dark hair, the wide coat that brushed the floor, the shoes too large for his feet.

The garret was dim, lit only by slivers of sunlight filtering through cracks in the roof. Some parts had no flooring; we stepped carefully from beam to beam. In the center stood a partition, with a door.

Beyond it, a small chamber sloped upward, its ceiling slanting into the roof. And there, on the floor, leaning against the wall, stood a mirror.

Old. Dusty. Its surface dull, save for the eagle carved at the top—wings outstretched, eyes fixed forward, as if watching.

“That is the door,” Raven said.

I approached slowly. The reflection showed us both—me, hesitant, and him, calm as stone. But something felt wrong. The mirror didn’t feel like glass. It felt like a threshold.

“It’s old,” I said. “Useless.”

“Not useless,” he replied. “Only waiting. Its clarity depends not on polish, but on light.”

“But there’s no light here.”

He turned, pulled a chain on the opposite wall. With a creak, the ceiling began to rotate. Slowly, steadily. He checked his watch, then tugged again.

“We arrive almost at noon,” he murmured. “The moment is near.”

After a few minutes, he pulled two more chains—left, right, left again. Then silence.

Suddenly, a patch of sunlight fell onto another mirror on the opposite wall. The rays reflected, crossed the chamber, and struck the dusty surface of the first mirror.

But no light returned.

No glow. No echo.

Just darkness.

“The light,” I said, “where did it go?”

Raven smiled. “Back, perhaps, to where it came from. Or into a realm we have not yet learned to perceive. Light is not just sight—it is sense. And we have senses yet unborn.”

He spoke then of dimensions—more than three, not just length, width, and depth, but others woven into the fabric of existence. Powers within us, dormant, unknown. He spoke of mind and matter as threads in a tapestry, interlaced but not bound. Of doors not made of wood or iron, but of thought, of vision, of memory.

I listened, but understood little. My mind rebelled. This was fantasy. Madness.

Yet as he spoke, I noticed the mirror—once clear, now clouded. A white mist filled its surface.

Then, slowly, the mist thinned.

And beyond it—mountains. Blue. Jagged. Real.

A vast heath stretched beneath them. And on it—a figure, moving fast, vanishing into the distance.

I turned to Raven.

He was gone.

I whirled back to the mirror.

There, reflected in its depths, was a man—his coat flapping, his hair lifted by a wind I could not feel.

It was Raven.

Running away.

I stumbled backward, heart pounding, hands shaking.

The mirror was empty now.

The chamber silent.

The door closed.

And I stood alone—on the edge of a world I had not known existed.

But the question remained:

Was it real?

Or had I, at last, crossed into a dream too vast to wake from?

I placed the manuscript down, comforted by the thought that my father had glimpsed the hidden world beyond ordinary sight—and that he had known Mr. Raven. But then it struck me: I had never learned how my father died. The silence around his passing now seemed ominous. Had he, too, followed Mr. Raven into that quiet realm and never returned? The idea filled me with shame for my own flight.

What wonders might I have uncovered by now—about life, death, and the vast unseen territories beyond perception? The Ravens were not cruel or deceitful; they were simply different. One possessed a rare gift of insight, the other an ethereal beauty. And yet I had doubted them, dismissed them as threats, refused their hospitality! My pride had blinded me to their kindness, and now I was consumed with regret.

Why had I feared the dead? To share in their sacred stillness should have been an honor—one I had proven myself unworthy of. What harm could the sleeping king or the lady with the wound in her palm have done me? I longed for the calm dignity of their faces, the peace of their presence. Overcome, I wept.

Weeping, I collapsed onto a couch and fell into a deep sleep. I woke abruptly, as if summoned by a voice. The house was silent, like an empty church. A blackbird sang on the lawn.

“I must go to them,” I whispered. “I must tell them I am sorry. I will do whatever they ask.”

I rose and climbed the stairs to the garret. The wooden chamber was unchanged—the mirror dimly reflecting the room. It was nearly noon, and the sun would be higher than when I first arrived. I needed to adjust the mirrors. If only I’d seen Mr. Raven do it!

I pulled the chains, letting light fall upon the first mirror. Then I turned to the second. There—faint, trembling, like a landscape seen through rippling water—were the shapes from my earlier vision. I touched the glass. It was solid, impenetrable.

Suspecting polarization, I shifted the mirrors again and again, adjusting their angles until, by chance, clarity emerged. The mountains stood blue and steady, sharp and real.

I stepped forward—and my feet touched heather.

I remembered only vaguely the path to the cottage: through a pine forest. I wandered through thickets and small fir woods, each time thinking I recognized something familiar—but no forest appeared. The sun sank low, and the air grew cold with the approach of winter. Then, joyfully, I saw a dark shape moving toward me. It was the raven.

I rushed to meet him.

“I beg your pardon, sir,” I said, breathless. “For my rudeness last night. Will you take me with you now? I confess—I don’t deserve it.”

He looked up, then paused. “Ah,” he said softly. “My wife does not expect you tonight. She regrets we ever encouraged your stay last week.”

“Let me see her!” I pleaded. “Let me tell her how sorry I am.”

“It is of no use,” he replied. “Your night had not come then—or you would not have left us. It has not come now. I cannot show you the way.”

He paused. “The dead rejoiced beneath their daisies—they all rest among the roots of heaven’s flowers—at the thought of your joy when winter ends and morning returns with its birds. When you left, they shivered in their beds. When the spring of the universe arrives… but that is not for ages. How many ages? I do not know. Nor do I care.”

“Tell me one thing,” I begged. “Is my father with you? Have you seen him since he left this world?”

“Yes,” the raven said. “He is with us. Asleep. That was him—arm on the coverlet, hand half-closed.”

“Why didn’t you tell me?” I cried. “I was so close to him and didn’t even know!”

“And turn your back on him?” he corrected gently.

“I would have lain down at once if I had known!”

“I doubt it,” he said. “Had you been ready to sleep, you would have recognized him.” He continued, “Old Sir Up’ard and your twice-great-grandfather are already gone. Your great-grandfather has been with us for many years. I think he will soon stir. You saw him last night. Though, of course, you did not know him.”

“Why *of course*?” I asked.

“Because he is much nearer waking than you are. No one who refuses to sleep can ever truly wake.”

“I don’t understand,” I said.

“You turned away,” he replied. “And chose not to understand.”

I remained silent. But if I didn’t speak, he would leave.

“And my grandfather—” I ventured—“is he with you?”

“No,” the raven said. “He is still in the Evil Wood, fighting the dead.”

“Where is the Evil Wood?” I asked. “How can I find him?”

“You will not find him,” he said. “But you will hardly miss the wood. It is where those who refuse to sleep awaken at night—to kill their dead and bury them.”

“I still don’t understand,” I insisted.

“Naturally not,” he replied. “Neither do I understand you. I cannot read your heart or your face. When my wife and I fail to understand our children, it is because there is not enough of them to be understood. Only God can comprehend foolishness.”

Then, feeling exposed and utterly insignificant, I said, “Will you at least show me the nearest way home? I know there are many paths—I’ve taken two already.”

“There are indeed many ways,” he said.

“How do I recognize the nearest?”

“I cannot,” he answered. “You and I use the same words, but they mean different things. We often cannot tell people what they *need* to know, because they *want* to know something else—and so would only misunderstand. Home is always so far away in the palm of your hand. And telling you how to get there would be useless. But you *will* get there. You *must*. Everyone who is not at home must go home. You thought you were at home when I found you—if that had been true, you could not have left. No one can leave home. And no one has ever been or will be at home without having gone there.”

“Enigma upon enigma!” I exclaimed. “I didn’t come here to solve riddles!”

“No,” he said. “But you came—and found the riddles waiting. In truth, you are the only riddle. What you call riddles are truths—truths that seem puzzling because you are not true yourself.”

“Worse and worse!” I cried.

“And you *must* answer them,” he continued. “They will keep asking until you understand yourself. The universe is a riddle trying to escape—and you are holding your door shut against it.”

“Then please—tell me what I must do. Where must I go?”

“How should I know your to-do, or the way to it?”

“If I can’t go home, at least direct me to someone like me.”

“I do not know of any,” he said. “The beings most like you are over there.” He pointed with his beak.

All I saw was the setting sun, blinding me.

“Well,” I said bitterly, “I feel terribly treated—taken from my home, abandoned in a strange world, and given no guidance on where to go or what to do!”

“You forget,” the raven said quietly, “that when I brought you, you refused my hospitality—and yet you reached what you call home safely. Now you’ve come of your own accord.”

He turned and walked slowly away, head bowed.

I stood stunned. True, I had come on my own. But wasn’t it with intent to make amends?

My heart ached. My mind held no purpose, no hope, no desire. I watched the raven vanish into the dusk.

Suddenly, he pounced—throwing his full weight onto the ground, digging fiercely with his beak. Then, flapping his wings, he threw back his head—and something shot from his bill, soaring high into the air.

That moment, the sun set. Darkness fell swiftly. But the thing he cast began to glow—a soft radiance, pulsing like a firefly, though larger and brighter, golden-yellow. It drifted toward me.

I turned and followed.

Here, I pause to say that this account is a constant struggle. I strive to describe what cannot be described—not because I lack words, but because the events and beings involved exist beyond the reach of this world’s language. I can only convey how these things affected me—not their essence, but the feelings they stirred within me.

Even this limited expression feels incomplete. I can present only one layer of meaning, one circle of truth, while the reality holds countless depths. A single object might seem to be many things, its core shifting, uncertain—its identity changing with every glance.

I often record only a clumsy approximation of what I felt, knowing no medium in this world can carry such strangeness with clarity or certainty. Even to someone more familiar with this place, I could not guarantee the truth of my experience.

Yet I know—without doubt—that I was witnessing activity. And yet, simultaneously, I was reading a metaphysical argument. The world blurred between action and idea, sensation and symbol.

And so I follow the glowing thing, into the gathering dark, guided not by reason, but by a fragile, trembling hope.

The air turned black as winter closed in around me, sharp and unrelenting. Above, a flickering fire blazed with sudden brilliance—no ordinary flame, but something alive, hovering like a breath caught between worlds. It paused, suspended, watching. As I stepped into its glow, it drifted forward, slow and deliberate, lingering over rocky patches of ground. Each time I looked up, it seemed to grow larger—its form shifting, luminous, until it cast a shadow beside me.

It was no bird, nor insect, but something beyond both: a creature of winged light, broad and nearly square, shimmering with every hue the spectrum could hold—and some that defied naming. I stood transfixed, captivated by its radiant beauty, so entranced that I tripped over a low stone and fell hard, stunned.

When I stirred again, it hovered above me, pulsing with color, its radiance weaving through the darkness like a symphony of unseen light. I rose, but my eyes remained fixed on it, unable to tear away. My foot struck another rock—this time, I sat down, not from injury, but from longing. I wanted it. I needed it. To have it in my hands, to feel its warmth, its magic.

And then—it descended.

Slowly at first, then faster, growing immense as it drew near. I felt the universe offering itself, a treasure too vast for words. I reached out—and grasped it.

But the moment my fingers closed around it, the light vanished. The world plunged into absolute dark. In my hand lay only a cold, lifeless book, its cover cracked open, pages blank. I hurled it into the air—heard it fall among the heather with a dull thud.

I buried my face in my palms, heart crushed. Then, the cold became unbearable. Fear of freezing drove me back to my feet.

Instantly, a faint sensation returned—a whisper of light. “Is it waking?” I whispered, hope flaring like a spark in the void.

No. It was the moon—just beginning to rise, sharp and pale, cutting through the horizon like a blade of silver ice. She gave me sight—but no direction. She did not hover. She did not wait. She offered only choice, blind and cruel.

Her face was full, her gaze piercing. But she was not Earth’s moon. Her light felt alien, strange—perhaps born of a sun I had never known. Every glance upward found her staring, unblinking, as if studying me. At first, I resented it—the intrusion of a silent watcher. Then, slowly, I sensed something deeper: not judgment, but sorrow. A quiet pity. Why was I here? Awake in this endless night?

That was when I truly understood: to be conscious in the cosmos is to be alone—and helpless.

My feet left the heather behind, stepping onto bare, spongy earth—like dry peat, yet unnaturally soft. Beneath me, the ground trembled. Then, a ripple passed ahead, shadowy under the moonlight, like a wave through water. It faded into distance—then, a single swell rose, crested, and broke.

From within burst a beast—tiger-like, but twisted, covered in clots of soil. Its eyes flared, teeth bared in a silent snarl. I froze. Not from fear—not yet. From shock. Then, without warning, it turned and vanished beneath the surface, as though swallowed by the land itself.

“That moon,” I muttered, “is playing tricks on my mind.” I walked on, muttering to myself. “What kind of life exists here? Only dreams? Phantoms? I am walking through a nightmare made real.”

But I didn’t know—then—that the moon was my shield. That her light held these things at bay. That without it, I would have been torn apart long before I took a second step.

“I will not fear what only seems,” I told myself. Yet even as I said it, dread curled in my gut. For the ground beneath me was not solid—it was a sea, and monsters swam below.

A few steps ahead, something began to emerge from the earth. A head—massive, like a polar bear’s—rose slowly, crowned with a mane of white fur, neck red as blood. The body followed, writhing, grotesque, each inch of its length dragging free with a sickening twist. When it was whole, it collapsed, panting, struggling to burrow again.

“Does it feed on the dead?” I wondered. “Why does it not touch me?”

I didn’t know then that the moon paralyzed them. That their power was bound by her gaze.

All night, they came—creatures unlike any other. Some were beautiful in horror: a serpent coiled with feathers of emerald, sapphire, and gold, its scales flashing like stained glass. Others were pure nightmare—limbs too many, faces split, bodies fused. And still, they did not attack. They watched. They lurked. They threatened—but never touched.

I grew numb to their presence. I began to imagine worse horrors than any real one could be. I conjured beasts from shadows, laughing at my own fears—unaware that every breath I took was granted by the moon’s watchful eye.

Her light was not divine. It was not primal. But it was enough. Light, even reflected, even borrowed, still holds dominion over darkness. Even the last echo of a dying sun can bind evil.

Had I known—had I understood—that if the moon set while I was still within her reach, I would be devoured in an instant, I might have run faster. I might have fought harder. But I did not. I only watched her sink, slow and solemn, toward the edge of the hills I had chosen as my goal.

Then—silence.

The ground ceased its wallowing. The burrows lay still.

And there, walking across the pale soil, was a woman.

She moved slowly, wrapped in a mist that shifted like cloth—now forming a robe, now dissolving into vapor. The wind tugged at her, swirling the fog around her limbs. She was beautiful—yet her face bore a terrible duality: pride and pain, dignity and despair. Her eyes were hollow, dead. On her left side, a dark stain pulsed faintly, as if infected. She pressed her hand against it, as though trying to silence a scream.

Her hair fell to her feet, pale gold in the moonlight. Sometimes the wind tangled it with the mist, and I could not tell where one ended and the other began.

Then—she doubled over, clutching her chest. The mist lifted, rising into the air like smoke from a dying fire.

I ran to her.

She convulsed. Her legs detached—snakes, swift and sinuous—slithering away. Her arms fled from her shoulders, also serpentine, vanishing into the dark. Something flew from her back—a bat-like shape—then she was gone.

The ground surged like a storm-tossed sea. Terror seized me. I turned and ran—toward the hills.

I reached the slope just as the moon dipped behind a peak, casting me into shadow.

Behind me, a sound erupted—raw, agonizing, like a cry of thwarted hunger. The first voice I’d heard since the butterfly died.

I turned. Shapes bounded after me—dark, swift, hungry. I sprinted for the ridge, where the moon still shone.

She lingered there—just long enough for me to see.

I climbed, gasping. The creatures panted behind me. One lunged—snarling, jaws wide.

We crashed into the moonlight together.

She flashed—bright, furious—and the beast dissolved into a smear on the ground.

Strength flooded me. I turned, facing the rest.

One by one, they darted into her light—and dropped, howling, crumbling into nothing.

And high above, I saw it: a smile upon the moon’s face—strange, knowing, almost mocking.

I reached the top of the ridge. The moon was sinking, low and distant, fading into the horizon.

The air was clear, crisp, alive.

I descended slightly, found warmth, and sat to wait.

The moon slipped beneath the world.

And once more, darkness claimed everything.

I fell into a deep sleep, and when I awoke, the sun was rising over the edge of the world. I climbed to the highest point nearby and looked back: the hollow I had crossed under moonlight lay silent, empty—no trace of movement, no breath of life. Could it be that beneath this stillness, something terrible stirred? The land before me stretched wide and barren, a vast desert broken only by a distant patch of darker hue—perhaps a forest.
