No smoke curled from hidden fires, no dust marked the passage of beasts or men, no signs of cultivation broke the silence. The sky was clear, unblemished by cloud or haze. I descended and set off toward the shadowed shape on the horizon. If anything lived in this world, it would likely be there. Here, nothing could endure.

The plain proved to be a desolate riverbed, long since dried up. Rock formed its bed—flat in places, cracked and grooved by ancient currents; elsewhere, humped and jagged like broken teeth. Not a drop of water remained. Some channels bore dry moss, and a few rocks were speckled with lichens so hard they seemed carved from stone. The air, once filled with the song of flowing water, now held no sound at all—only silence, thick as death.

It took me the entire day to reach the dark patch. When I did, I found it was indeed a forest—but not a single trickle of water had crossed my path. Yet through the blistering noon, I heard it anyway: the rushing of rivers, the whisper of brooks. A phantom chorus echoed in my ears, so vivid I almost believed my eyes deceived me. The sun dipped below the treetops as I stepped into the woods. It cast long rays between massive, column-like trunks, painting the ground in shifting patterns of light and shadow. This was not the pine forest I had expected. These trees were strange—some familiar, others unlike any I had ever seen. One resembled a eucalyptus in bloom, but its flowers had a rigid calyx shaped like a skull, the lid lifting to reveal a froth-like bloom spilling over the rim. I lay beneath its broad, scimitar-shaped leaves, gazing into the depths of the forest.

Soon, the paths narrowed, the glades closed in. Night fell swiftly, sharp and cold. And what a night it was! How can I convey its eerie beauty, its haunting strangeness?

The tree above me rose tall before branching, its limbs bent low as if to enclose me. As twilight faded, the shapes of the foliage began to shift. In my wandering gaze, the clumps of leaves transformed—not imitated, but *suggested* other forms. A faint wind stirred, setting the branches swaying. Each twig, each leaf moved in rhythm with the branch, creating a dance of motion. Among them, I saw a pack of wolves straining at an invisible leash—greyhounds could not have fought harder to break free.

Another cluster of leaves became a stable full of horses, their heads and necks thrust forward from stalls. Their necks bobbed up and down, restless, then snapped sideways as the wind intensified. Some were bare skulls; one had skin stretched tight over bone. Another had lost its jaw, hanging loose, yet every now and then lifting high as though to ease a bit in its mouth.

Above them, perched on a branch, floated the figure of a woman—arms raised, commanding, imperious. Her form was unmistakable, though made of shadows and leaves. I stared, transfixed. The clarity of these visions unsettled me. What if they weren’t illusions at all? What if they were real, waiting only for my mind to believe?

Then darkness swallowed everything. The wind died. The shapes vanished. I slept.

But sleep brought no peace. Before dawn, I began to hear sounds—a distant rush, mingled with faint cries. They grew louder, closer. From every direction, noise surged into the wood. The place where I lay became the center of a storm. I dared not move. My breath slowed. I feared even the rustle of my cloak might betray me.

At last, the moon crested the trees and slipped into the forest. With her first light, the chaos erupted. A deafening roar filled the air. Shapes flickered in the dimness—figures battling, leaping, screaming. It was a war of the dead.

Skeletons clashed with phantoms. Swords passed through ghostly forms, leaving them trembling but unharmed. Maces shattered bones, yet the skeletons kept fighting—so long as two bones remained joined, they would not fall. Men and horses lay scattered, their bones crushed beneath foot and hoof. White, gaunt steeds charged through the fray. On misty horses and on foot, specters raged—unstoppable, unkillable. Weapons rang, hooves thundered, jaws gnashed. And through it all, voices screamed: curses, prayers, mockery, lies, truths twisted into weapons.

Every creed, every hatred, every lie that had ever fueled conflict echoed in the air. “Truth!” one roared, turning on his allies. “Truth!” he cried again, slashing wildly. Two fighters collapsed side by side, exhausted—then rose and fought anew. No one paused to help the fallen. No mercy. No end.

The moon shone until the sun rose. All night, I watched a woman glide above the battle—now here, now there, one arm outstretched, urging the fight, the other pressed to her side. Her eyes were dark, lifeless. I recognized her—the same figure I had seen in the leaves. She shouted: “Ye are men: slay one another!”

Then, just before sunrise, a breeze swept through the trees. A voice, calm and final, echoed: “Let the dead bury their dead.”

At once, silence. The combatants froze. When the sun rose, there was no sign of battle. Only scattered branches, brittle and dry. The forest stood quiet, still, untouched.

I rose and continued my journey. The morning air was still. No birds sang. No creature stirred. Not a moth, not a mouse, not a squirrel crossed my path. But I walked with caution, my eyes never lingering on any shape. For I knew—any moment, the forest might come alive again. The sounds of digging, of bones crashing, of whispers—still echoed in my mind. Perhaps ten thousand phantoms waited, not to appear, but to be *seen*. And I, too, might be the one who summoned them.

By mid-afternoon, I emerged from the woods. Before me lay another network of dry riverbeds—identical to the first. At first, I thought I had turned around, but soon realized it was another branch of the same ancient stream. I began crossing it, stepping into a wide channel just as the sun set. I sat down, waiting for the moon. Sleep came quickly. I lay on the moss, head resting, and instantly—I heard water. Streams rushing, springs murmuring, waterfalls singing. A soft, liquid melody wrapped around me, lulling me into dreamless rest.

When I woke, the sun was already climbing. The landscape stretched wide—rocky, wrinkled, covered in shadows that ran like stripes across the earth, like the hide of some great beast. As the sun rose, the shadows receded, as if the rocks were drinking in the darkness they had exhaled during the night.

Until now, I had loved my Arab mare and my books more than any living soul. But now, my heart burned for human presence. I longed for those beings of this strange world—the ones the raven had described so vaguely as being closest to my kind. With heavy steps and a hopeful heart, haunted by doubt whether I was even moving forward, I pressed on—north-west and by south.

As I moved along one of the narrow channels, something small and green caught my eye—what looked like a shrub, perhaps the outpost of a hidden forest. I knelt to examine it more closely. It bore tiny fruit, unfamiliar to me, and though hunger gnawed at my belly, I hesitated. I didn’t know what it was, and caution whispered that ignorance could be deadly. But unseen by me, from behind jagged rocks and crevices, hundreds of eyes watched—wide, bright, and full of curiosity.

I pressed on, passing another plant, then another, each larger than the last, until I found myself surrounded by clusters of them. Only then did I realize they were not shrubs at all, but stunted trees—dwarfed yet proud in their quiet growth. The channel narrowed, choked with these strange plants, and I had to leap or squeeze through where I could. In one stretch, a sudden rustling erupted—like wings beating against ivy-covered stone—but when I turned, nothing stirred.

Beyond lay a grove of larger trees, heavy with coarse-looking fruit, standing on the edge of a shallow depression once filled with water. A forest flowed down from the highlands into this hollow, its upper slopes rich with varied timber, while below, almost every tree bore edible fruit. I descended a grassy slope dotted with moss, weary from the journey, and stretched out to rest.

Nearby stood a slender tree, no taller than my hand, laden with tiny apples the color of dawn—rosy, perfect, no bigger than cherries. My fingers reached out instinctively. I plucked one and bit into it. The flavor burst across my tongue—sweet, crisp, alive. I raised my hand for another, when a chorus of voices broke the silence.

“*He likes our apples!*” they cried, laughter ringing like wind chimes over a stream. “*He likes our apples! He’s a good giant! He’s a good giant!*”

I sat up, startled. Around me, above and below, children swarmed—dozens of them, some barely able to walk, others nearly as tall as I was. A few looked older, standing apart, calm amid the chaos. They chattered in clusters, debating, teasing, laughing, their voices sharp and bright, full of intelligence and joy. I understood most of what they said—not because language came easily, but because their hearts spoke plainly.

They had seen me take the first apple. From that single act, they judged me kind. One child, smaller than the rest, stepped forward with a solemn face. “He IS rather big,” he admitted, “but littleness isn’t everything! It won’t keep you from growing big and stupid unless you take care!”

I smiled, though I said nothing. I feared my voice might startle them. Better to listen, to learn.

Then, from a nearby thicket, a little boy emerged—innocent-faced, mischievous-eyed—offering me a massive green apple. The crowd fell silent. All eyes turned to me.

“Eat, good giant,” he said softly.

I sat up, accepted the fruit, and smiled my thanks. I took a bite—then froze. The taste was bitter, sour, almost poisonous. Without thinking, I hurled the apple far away.

A roar of delight exploded from the children. They rushed toward me, tumbling over themselves in joy. They climbed onto my arms, kissed my hands, clung to my legs, wrapped their arms around my neck. I collapsed under their weight, overwhelmed by their warmth and exuberance.

“*Good, good giant!*” they sang. “*We knew you would come! Oh, you dear, strong, kind giant!*”

Their voices rose and fell like waves—joyful, endless. Then, abruptly, silence.

The children drew back. Those atop me scrambled down, helping me rise. A young girl pointed upward, her face grave.

“Get up, good giant!” she urged. “Hurry! He saw you throw your apple away!”

Before she finished, I stood. On the hilltop stood a figure—a man, or something like one, slightly taller than I, with a brutish face and a sneer twisted across his lips. He wore no weapon, yet his presence radiated threat. As he began descending, I started up the slope, seeking better ground.

He growled, low and guttural, and turned toward me. When we met, he held out his hand. I reached to shake it, but he snatched it back, threatened a blow, then offered it again. Now I understood—he wanted the apple I’d thrown away.

I shook my head, made a grimace of disgust, gestured firmly: *No.*

His rage erupted in a howl—*“You dare say my apple was unfit?”*

“I have no quarrel with your tree,” I replied. “But one bad apple may grow on the best.”

Whether he grasped the meaning, I do not know. But he lunged forward, and before I could react, another giant—similar in build and cruelty—appeared from behind. Together, they attacked.

I struck one hard in the face, but the other slammed me from behind. Between them, I was overpowered. They dragged me up the slope into the woods, to their settlement—huts built of fallen branches and rough stones, crude and dark. Into one they thrust me, threw me to the floor, and kicked me.

A woman stood nearby, watching without expression. During my captivity, I learned little about gender among them—they seemed so alike in form and manner that I often wondered if they were not some strange, fungal race—sentient only enough to move, to hate, to hoard.

Their food—roots, bulbs, and bitter fruits—was revolting to me. But to them, refusal was an offense. I was beaten by both men and women for refusing to eat. That night, I lay half-dead, unable to move, but sleep came, and with it, a fragile strength.

At dawn, they dragged me back to the valley. They bound my feet to a tree with a long rope and placed a flat, serrated stone in my left hand. I shifted it to my right. They kicked me, forced it back into my left. Gestures told me: *Scrape the bark off every branch without fruit.* I obeyed, hoping that submission might earn me freedom—or at least time to plan escape.

And fortune smiled. Close beside me grew one of the dwarf-trees, bearing the same sweet, tiny apples I had tasted before. Every few minutes, I stole a fruit. Each one restored my strength, sharpened my mind. Though I labored under their cruel rule, I lived—because of the kindness of children, and the secret gift of a single tree.

I had barely begun my task when I heard faint voices nearby—soft, quick, like the rustle of leaves in a hidden breeze. Then they emerged: tiny figures slipping through the thickets of stunted trees that filled the spaces between the towering giants’ grove. In moments, dozens surrounded me, their eyes wide and bright, their movements swift and light as shadows.

I gestured urgently, signaling that the giants had just left and were still near. But they only laughed—a sound like wind chimes in spring—and assured me the air was clean. “They’re too blind to see us,” one chimed, and the whole group giggled in unison, a chorus of bells ringing across the forest floor.

“Do you like that rope around your ankles?” asked a small boy with curious eyes.

“I wear it so they think I can’t remove it,” I replied.

“They can’t even see their own feet!” he shot back, dancing on his toes with delight. “Just walk slowly and they’ll believe it’s still tied.”

One of the larger girls knelt beside me, her fingers delicate as spider silk, and began untangling the knot. I smiled, thinking no such hands could manage such a clumsy tie—but in seconds, it was undone.

They led me to sit, then offered me fruit—tiny, jewel-like things bursting with flavor. Afterward, the smallest among them swarmed around me, playing games that twisted and turned with wild abandon. I couldn’t work. I couldn’t think. They kept me laughing, spinning, chasing shadows until the sun dipped low behind the trees.

Then came the heavy tread of approaching footsteps. The Little Ones vanished instantly—like smoke drawn into the wind. Only one girl remained, pressing her hand to my arm. “We must be careful,” she whispered. “A single step from one of those monstrous feet could crush a little one.”

“Can’t they see you at all?” I asked.

“They might notice movement—if we were piled atop you like we were just now. But they hate anything alive but themselves… though honestly, they aren’t much alive either.” She whistled once—sharp and clear—and in an instant, not a trace of them remained.

It was my master—the giant who claimed ownership of me—coming to drag me home. He freed my ankles, pulled me to his hut, threw me to the ground, bound my feet again, kicked me hard, and walked away.

I could have fled. I should have. But I had found something precious—something rare in this strange world. Friends. Not just friends, but companions who sang, who played, who watched over me with silent devotion. Their presence made the loneliness bearable, the labor lighter. I couldn’t leave them—not yet.

That night, long after the huts fell quiet, I heard whispers—faint, persistent, circling close. I knew they were there, watching, guarding. From then on, they never let me sleep alone. Often, I woke to find myself cradled in a nest of children—one tucked against my chest, others curled around me like warm feathers. I didn’t shiver in the cold. I didn’t dream of escape. I dreamed of them.

Each morning, before dawn, they brought food to the giant’s door—fruit, nuts, honey-sweet berries—always in silence, always in haste. One morning, I awoke to find myself utterly alone. But then I heard soft steps, muffled laughter, and from the edge of the wood stepped Lona—the tallest, calmest, most solemn of them all. Behind her came the rest, joyous yet hushed, afraid to wake the sleeping giant.

In her arms, she carried a baby boy—his skin pale, his eyes closed, his breath soft as mist. Until now, the youngest had been a girl, perhaps a year old. Now there was a new life.

She placed the infant gently in my arms. His eyes fluttered open, looked at me, then shut again. He slept.

“He loves you already,” Lona said softly.

“Where did you find him?” I asked.

“In the wood,” she answered, smiling. “Where we always find them. Isn’t he beautiful? We’ve been searching all night. Sometimes it’s hard to locate them.”

“How do you know when one is there?”

“I don’t know,” she admitted. “Everyone tells each other at once—but we never know who started it. Sometimes I think someone said it in their sleep, and another heard it half-awake. When there’s a baby in the wood, no one stops to ask questions. And by the time we find it, it’s too late to wonder.”

“Are there more boys or girls among you?”

They didn’t come to the wood—they came *from* it. “We go to the wood and find them,” she corrected. “And yes—we have both. But we don’t count. We wouldn’t like to be counted.”

“Why not?”

“It wouldn’t be smooth. We’d rather not know.”

“And where do they come from first?”

“From the wood—always. There’s nowhere else.”

She knew nothing beyond that. The mystery ended there.

“How often do you find one?”

“Such a joy takes all our gladness,” she said. “We forget the last time. You’re glad to have this one, aren’t you, good giant?”

“Yes,” I said. “But how do you feed him?”

She went to fetch plums—small, round, glowing with color. She held one to the baby’s lips. “He would open his mouth if he were awake,” she said, then lifted him and squeezed the juice onto the surface. The baby sucked instinctively, even in sleep. She continued, slowly extracting every drop until only skin and stone remained.

“There!” she said, voice full of pride. “A world of big apples would be terrible for babies! We wouldn’t stay in it—would we, darling? We’d leave it to the bad giants!”

“But what if the stone gets in his mouth?”

“No mother would do that,” she said firmly. “I wouldn’t be fit to hold a baby.”

I thought then of what she might become—how lovely she would be as a woman. But what happened when they grew? Where did they go?

“Will you tell me where you lived before?” I asked.

“Here,” she said simply.

“Have you never lived anywhere else?”

“Never. We all came from the wood. Some say we dropped from the trees.”

“But why are so many of you still very small?”

“I don’t understand. Some are smaller, some bigger. I am very big.”

“The baby will grow bigger?”

“Of course.”

“And you?”

“I don’t think so. I hope not. I am the biggest. It frightens me sometimes.”

“Why?”

She didn’t answer.

“How old are you?”

“I don’t know what you mean. We are all just that.”

“How big will the baby grow?”

“I cannot tell. But some…” Her voice wavered. “…begin to grow after we think they’ve stopped. That is a frightful thing. We don’t talk about it.”

“What makes it frightful?”

She was silent. Then: “We fear they may be becoming giants.”

“Why would you fear that?”

“Because it is so terrible. I don’t want to talk about it.”

She pressed the baby to her chest, her face tight with worry. I didn’t press further.

Soon, I noticed changes in some of the younger ones—traces of greed, selfishness. The older girls watched them closely, their eyes shadowed with concern. None of them ever touched my tools, never helped the giants. But they cared for me endlessly.

They sang to me—each taking turns, one after another—for hours. They climbed trees to reach my mouth, popping fruit into it with fingers like rosebuds. They guarded me from the giants, always alert, always ready.

Sometimes, they gathered in circles and told stories—simple tales, often without meaning, yet full of rhythm and wonder. Once, a moody child sang a lullaby so sorrowful that tears streamed down my face—though I didn’t understand a word.

The others stared at me, puzzled. Then I realized: I had never seen water in this world—no rivers, no rain, no streams. It had existed long ago, perhaps, but the Little Ones had never known it—until now.

Yet they seemed to sense its origin. A tiny child rushed to the singer, shook his fist in his face, and cried: “Ou skeeze ze juice out of ze good giant’s seeberries! Bad giant!”

One day, I asked Lona: “Why do I never see any children among the giants?”

She blinked, as if trying to grasp the question. “They are giants. There are no little ones.”

“Have they never had children?”

“No. There are never any in the wood for them. They do not love children. If they saw ours, they would crush them.”

“So the number of giants stays the same?”

She burst into laughter—bright, sudden, then fading fast. “No, good giant. WE are THEIR firsters.”

Her joy died. She looked frightened.

“How can that be?” I asked.

“I don’t say. I don’t understand. But we were here. They were not. They go from us. I am sorry—but we cannot help it. THEY could have helped it.”

“How long have you been here?”

“Always, I think. I think somebody made us always.”

I returned to scraping wood. She saw I didn’t grasp it.

“The giants were not made always,” she continued. “If a Little One doesn’t care, he grows greedy, then lazy, then big, then stupid, then bad. The dull creatures don’t know they come from us. Very few believe we exist. They say nonsense!”

She pointed to a boy named Blunty, eating an apple nearly as large as his head.

“Look at him,” she whispered. “He will be the next! Oh, oh—he will soon be big and bad and ugly, and not know it!”

He stood apart, chewing with grim satisfaction. I had always thought him odd. Now he seemed repulsive.

“I’ll take that horrid thing from him!” I shouted.

“It’s no use,” she sighed. “We’ve done all we can. It’s too late. We feared he was growing—he wouldn’t listen. When he refused to share his berries, saying he gathered them himself—that was the sign. He’s a glutton. No hope for him. It sickens me to watch him eat.”

“Couldn’t someone stop him?”

“He may have them if he wants. It’s all the same—to eat the apples, or to be a boy who would eat them. No. He must go to the giants. He belongs to them. Look how much bigger he is than when you first came. Bigger even than yesterday.”

“He looks like that green lump in his hand,” I said.

“It suits what he’s making himself.”

“His head and that thing could swap places!”

“Perhaps they do.”

“Does he want to be a giant?”

“He hates the giants—but he’s becoming one anyway. He likes their apples.”

“Oh, baby,” she murmured. “He was just as sweet as you when we found him.”

“He’ll be miserable when he realizes he’s a giant.”

“Oh, no. He’ll like it well enough. That’s the worst part.”

“Will he hate the Little Ones?”

“He’ll be like the rest. He won unlikely to remember us—or believe we exist. He’ll eat his apples.”

“Tell me what happens next. I understand so little of your world. I come from a place where everything is different.”

“I don’t know about ‘world.’ What is it? Just a word in your big mouth? That makes it something.”

“Don’t mind the word. Tell me what will happen to Blunty.”

“He will wake one morning and find himself a giant—not like you, good giant, but like any other bad one. You won’t recognize him. But I will. He’ll think he’s always been a giant. He won’t know you, or any of us. Peony says the giants have lost themselves—that’s why they never smile. I wonder—are they glad because they’re bad, or bad because they’re not glad?”

“They can’t be glad,” I said, “without babies.”

“No,” she agreed. “And they can’t be happy when they have none. I wonder what ‘bad’ really means, good giant.”

“I wish I knew no more about it than you,” I said.

“But I try to be good,” I added. “I mean to keep trying.”

“So do I,” she said. “And that’s how I know you are good.”

We sat in silence.

Then I asked, one last time: “So you don’t know where the babies come from in the wood?”

“There’s nothing to know,” she said. “They’re in the wood. They grow there.”

“But why do you never find one before it’s fully grown?”

She frowned, thoughtful. “They’re not there until they’re finished.”

“A pity the little sillies can’t speak before they forget everything they need to tell,” I mused.

“Little Tolma, the last before this baby, looked like she had something to say when I found her under a beech tree, sucking her thumb. But she didn’t. She just looked up at me—so sweetly. SHE will never go bad and grow big.”

“When they begin to grow big,” she said, “they care for nothing but bigness. When they can’t grow taller, they try to grow fatter. The bad giants are proud of being fat.”

“So are the ones in my world,” I said. “Only they don’t call it fat. They call it rich.”

“In one of their houses,” she continued, “sits the biggest and fattest of all—so proud no one can see him. The giants go to his house at certain times, shout to him, praise how fat he is, beg him to make them strong so they can eat more and grow fat like him.”

Later, I heard news: Blunty was gone.

I saw a few somber faces among the older ones. But no mourning. No grief.

The next morning, Lona crept to me and whispered: “Look—by that quince-tree. That is the giant who was Blunty.”

“Never,” I said. “But now you tell me—I could almost think it was Blunty staring through fog. He does look stupid.”

“He is forever eating those apples now,” she said. “That’s what comes of Little Ones who WON’T stay little.”

“They call it growing up in my world,” I muttered.

“If only she would teach me to grow the other way—to become a Little One. Shall I ever laugh like them?”

I had had the chance. I had thrown it away.

Blunty and I were alike. He didn’t know he was losing himself. And I—had to be taught mine.

For a time, my only wish was to stay with the Little Ones, to live among them in quiet joy, sheltered from the harshness of the world beyond. But slowly, other thoughts stirred within me—restless, questioning, urgent. I began to feel that I was meant for more than mere companionship; that I carried within me a purpose not yet fulfilled. Was I truly only a caretaker, a gentle giant to feed and protect? Or was there something deeper, something I ought to uncover?

I realized then that I stood in a world unlike any I had known—a realm woven from strange laws and hidden truths. If I wished to repay the kindness of these children, if I wished to be more than a guest in their lives, I must learn more than they could teach me. I must understand the nature of their existence, their origins, the mystery of their stunted growth. And to do that, I would need freedom—to wander, to seek answers beyond the safety of their grove.

It seemed impossible that such beings, so pure and radiant, could be held back by some unseen force. How could life and law clash so violently that perfection required suppression? That growth itself was feared as a curse? Yet the evidence was plain: the Little Ones did not grow. Lona, who appeared the eldest, no more than fifteen, had long overseen a throng of creatures who, though childlike in form and manner, looked upon her as mother. They knew nothing of time, no sense of age. To them, existence was eternal, unchanging. Lona believed she had always been. She embodied both love and law, wisdom without knowledge, guidance without history.

But what I saw as ignorance might simply be my own blindness. Still, one thing was clear: she feared growth above all else. The thought of becoming “bad giants” haunted her—monsters like those who roamed the edges of their world. And so, she guarded them fiercely, keeping them small, innocent, safe.

Yet I could not help but wonder: were they truly safe? Were they growing at all? Or had their development been halted by some unnatural power? I longed to teach them—the beauty of numbers, the rhythm of music, the art of writing down their delicate songs before they vanished into silence. But how could I guide them if I did not first understand them? Knowledge might corrupt the weak—but surely it could uplift the good.

I decided I must leave, at least for a time. Not to abandon them, but to seek understanding. Only then could I return with wisdom to offer. But my departure was not to be peaceful.

One day, while working near the tree, I told them of my desire to journey again—not out of fear, but out of longing to know more. I said I had stayed because of my love for them, not because I was trapped.

At once, they surged toward me—rushing, climbing, piling upon me like a storm of tiny bodies. Three nestled in my arms, two clung to my shoulders, one stood upright on my head, others wrapped around my legs, hands gripping my arms, feet scrambling up my sides. I was buried beneath a joyful avalanche.

And then—silence.

Not one noticed the shadow until it loomed over us. With a cry—“Take care, good giant!”—they scattered like startled birds. They dropped from me, fled up the tree, vanished into the branches. In an instant, the bad giant rounded the trunk and struck me hard across the head with a stick. I fell to the ground, unconscious.

When I awoke, night had fallen. Pale stars blinked above, waiting for the moon. My head throbbed. Thirst burned my throat. I turned onto my side—and the moment my ear touched the earth, I heard water gurgle nearby. The sound made me ache.

Then, suddenly, I was surrounded. Silent children pressed close. Soft fruits touched my lips—delicious, cool, life-giving. One after another, they fed me until the thirst passed.

But then—new sounds. Whispers. Sobs.

I tried to rise. Instantly, a pile of small bodies formed behind me, supporting me. With their strength, I stood.

“You must go,” they said. “The bad giants will come when they see you hurt. They’ll trample you.”

“I think I must,” I replied.

“Go and grow strong,” they urged. “Come back soon.”

“I will,” I said—and sat again.

“No,” whispered Lona, kneeling beside me. “You must go now.”

A boy stepped forward. “I listened at his door,” he said. “He told his wife he found you idle, talking to moles and squirrels. When he hit you, they tried to kill him. He called you a wizard. Said you must be destroyed, or peace will never come.”

I nodded. “I will go. And I’ll return when I know how to help you grow stronger.”

“We don’t want to grow,” they answered, solemn. “We won’t become bad giants. We are strong enough already. You don’t know how strong we are.”

There was no convincing them. Their hearts were fixed. So I rose, and walked slowly up the valley slope.

They followed—forming a procession. Some led, some walked beside me, helping me. Others trailed behind, feeding me along the way.

“You are broken,” they said. “Red juice has run out. Put some in.”

We reached the edge of the valley just as the moon lifted her face above the horizon.

“She comes to watch over you,” Lona said. “She will show you the way.”

As we walked, I asked about the world beyond. They spoke of a great place ruled by a giant-girl queen. I asked if it was a city. They didn’t know. They couldn’t say how far, nor which direction, nor her name. Only that she hated them—wanted to kill them, but could not find them.

“How do you know?” I asked.

Lona said, “I have always known.”

“If she comes,” she added, “we must hide deeply.”

I told them I would go to her, ask why she hated them.

“No! No!” they cried. “She will kill you, good giant! She is a terrible witch—awful, awful!”

I asked where I should go instead.

Beyond the baby-forest, they said, lay a smooth green land—soft underfoot, free of rocks and trees. It stretched toward where the moon came from.

“But how do I reach it?”

“The moon will tell you,” they said.

We climbed the second branch of the riverbed. When the moon reached its zenith, they stopped.

“We’ve never gone this far from our trees,” they murmured.

“Watch your path,” Lona warned. “See inside your eyes so you can find your way back.”

One girl added, “Beware the giant-woman in the desert. You’ve heard of her?”

“No,” I said.

“Then don’t go near her. She is called the Cat-woman. Awfully ugly. And she SCRATCHES.”

The larger ones paused. Then, slowly, they turned and walked away.

The smaller ones ran back first. Then the rest, glancing at me with quiet gravity.

Last, Lona held up the baby. She kissed me gently. Gazed into my eyes. Whispered, “The Cat-woman will not hurt YOU.”

Then she turned and disappeared into the shadows.

I stood still, watching them vanish under the silver light. Then I turned, heart heavy, and began my solitary journey.

Soon, their laughter echoed behind me—like distant bells, ringing through the air, bouncing off the cliffs. I turned once more. They danced ahead, carefree, untouched by sorrow.

Only Lona walked apart, cradling her child.

As I walked, I reflected on them—on their deep love for their home, their refusal to change, their innocence. Once, when I suggested leaving the bad giants’ land, they replied, “But that would be to NOT ourselves.” Place was part of their soul. Without ambition, fear, or greed, they had no reason to desire change. They knew no wrong. And except for their babies, they had never helped anyone but me.

How could they grow?

And yet—why should they?

In trying to improve their lives, might I not harm them? Might I not twist their nature, break their harmony, by imposing my world’s values? Their dread of growth—of becoming giants—might be instinctive, even wise.

To act as a savior is perilous. To bring good, one must first avoid evil. And the first step is to remove the beam from one’s own eye.

I journeyed under the watchful gaze of the moon. As always, she was full—her face unchanging, eternal in its brilliance—and tonight, as she sank beneath the edge of the world, I thought I saw a faint smile upon her silver countenance. Just as her lower rim dipped below the horizon, a cottage appeared in the very center of her disc, painted there as if by invisible hands. Through its open door and window, her light poured forth, warm and inviting. And with that vision came an undeniable certainty: I was expected.

No sooner had I registered it than the moon vanished, the cottage dissolved into nothingness, and darkness crept over the land like a living thing. The path ahead wound through a series of narrow ravines, each one more shadowed than the last. With no sign of dawn, I decided to wait. I settled into a shallow hollow of sand, ate the fruit the children had given me at parting, and fell asleep quickly, lulled by the silence.

I woke abruptly. Above me, stars I had never seen before shimmered across the sky—constellations unknown to my old world. I lay still, gazing upward, when I became aware of a figure seated not far from me, slightly elevated on the slope above. My breath caught. I had been alone—until now.

The shape stood between me and the heavens, outlined sharply against the night. From where I lay low in the hollow, it seemed immense, towering over me like a sentinel of the dark. Then it turned its head—still facing away—and spoke.

“Will you not come with me?” said a voice, soft and deep as a well, unmistakably feminine.

I hesitated, curious but cautious. “Thank you,” I replied, “but I am comfortable here. Where would you have me go? I prefer sleeping under the open sky.”

“There is no harm in the air,” she answered, “but the creatures that wander these lands at night are not ones a man would wish near him while he sleeps.”

“I’ve not been disturbed,” I said.

“No,” she replied. “I’ve sat beside you ever since you laid down.”

That startled me. “Then how did you know I was here? Why do you show me such kindness?”

“I saw you,” she said, still without turning, “in the moonlight just as she disappeared. I see poorly in daylight, but at night my sight is perfect. The shadow of my house would have hidden you—but both doors were open. I was out on the wastes when I saw you enter this hollow. You were already asleep by the time I reached you, and I didn’t wish to wake you. People grow afraid when I appear suddenly. They call me the Cat-woman. It is not my name.”

I recalled the children’s tale—the one about the ugly woman who scratched. But her voice held no menace, only a quiet sorrow, almost apologetic. She could not be truly monstrous.

“You shall not hear it from me,” I said. “Please, tell me what I may call you.”

“When you know me,” she replied, “call me by the name that fits. That will tell me what kind of person you are. Few give me the right one. It is well when they do.”

I smiled faintly. “I suppose, madam, you live in the cottage I saw within the moon?”

“I do,” she said. “I live there alone—except when guests come. It is humble, but I do what I can for those who stay. Sometimes their sleep is sweet.”

Her voice settled into me, calming my nerves, easing the edges of my fear. I rose. “I will go with you, madam.”

She stood at once, and without looking back, began walking ahead. I followed, able just barely to make out her form in the dimness. She was taller than I, though not as tall as I’d first imagined. Yet she never turned her face to me. That made me curious—not fearful. Her voice rang true, clear, and sincere. But how could I name her if I could not see her?

I tried to walk beside her, but she moved faster when I did. No matter how I paced myself, she remained just ahead, always out of reach. Slowly, unease began to creep in. Why did she avoid being seen? Was it because she was hideous? Was I following some terrible, unseen horror through the dark?

A chill ran through me. Almost, I regretted accepting her invitation. Neither of us spoke. The silence stretched, thick and unbearable.

I had to break it. “I’m trying to find a place,” I said, “one I’ve heard of but don’t yet know by name. Perhaps you can tell me?”

“Describe it,” she said. “The stupid Bags know nothing. The careless Lovers forget nearly everything.”

“Where do they live?”

“You’ve just come from them.”

“I’ve never heard those names before.”

“You wouldn’t. Neither people knows its own name.”

“Strange.”

“Perhaps. But hardly anyone anywhere knows their true name. Imagine how many fine gentlemen would stare if they were addressed by the one that actually belongs to them!”

I fell silent, wondering what mine might be.

“What do you think yours is?” she asked, as if reading my mind. “But forgive me—it matters little.”

I opened my mouth to answer, then froze. My name was gone. Not just forgotten—I couldn’t recall even the first letter. This was the second time someone had asked, and both times I’d failed.

“Never mind,” she said gently. “It’s not needed. Your real name is written upon your forehead—but at present it whirls about so wildly that no one can read it. I will help steady it. Soon it will slow… and perhaps settle.”

I was stunned. We had walked for hours, leaving the ravines behind, yet no sign of the cottage had appeared.

“The Little Ones told me,” I said at length, “of a smooth green country, pleasant to walk upon.”

“Yes?”

“They also spoke of a giantess queen. Is that her land?”

“There is a city in that grassy land,” she replied, “where a woman rules as princess. It is called Bulika. But the princess is no girl. She is older than this world, and came from yours—with a terrible history, still unfolding. She is evil, and holds great sway over the Prince of the Power of the Air. Once, the people of Bulika were simple folk—farmers and shepherds. She came among them, and they welcomed her. She taught them to dig for diamonds and opals, to trade with strangers, to abandon their fields and build a city. One day they killed a great serpent. That enraged her, and she declared herself their ruler—becoming a terror to them all.

“At that time, the land was known as THE LAND OF WATERS. The dry channels you crossed were once rivers, and the valley where the Bags and Lovers now grow fruit was a vast lake. But the wicked princess gathered up half the water, sealed it in an egg, and carried it away. Her lap could hold no more. When she left, the rest fled underground, leaving the land parched and barren—just as her heart. Without water, rain does account for nothing. Without rain, springs fail. Since then, the princess has ruled Bulika, keeping her people in constant fear, preventing them from multiplying. Still, they boast of prosperity—skilled in trade, sharp in deceit, loyal only when interests align, proud of their princess, and contemptuous of all others. They believe themselves noble, honest, superior. But the depth of their corruption and arrogance cannot be understood unless you’ve seen them—unless you’ve known the self-deceived, misgoverned wretches.”

“I thank you, madam,” I said. “Now, if you please, tell me of the Little Ones—the Lovers. I long to serve them. Who are they? How did they come to be here? Those children are the greatest wonder I’ve encountered in this world of wonders.”

“In Bulika,” she said, “you may learn something of them. There is an ancient poem in the palace library—no one there can read it, but it plainly states that after the Lovers endure great trials and discover their true name, they will fill the land and make the giants their slaves.”

“By then, they’ll have grown, won’t they?” I asked.

“Yes, they will grow. Yet I think they will not. It is possible to grow and not grow—to grow less and greater at once—even to grow by refusing to grow.”

“Your words are strange,” I said.

“But some words mean more because they seem to mean less.”

“That is true,” she said. “Such words must be understood. If only the princess of Bulika could hear what the silence of the land shouts in her ears every hour! But she is too clever to understand anything.”

“Then when the Lovers grow, will their land have water again?”

“Not exactly. When they are thirsty enough, they will have water. And when they have water, they will grow. To grow, they must have water. Beneath the earth, it flows still.”

“I’ve heard that water twice,” I said. “Once when I rested in the hollow, and again when I fell—nearly killed—by the bad giant. Both times, I heard the voices of the water, and they healed me.”

She never turned her head, but I could hear every word. Her voice echoed the woman in the house of death. Much of what she said slipped from memory, but I forgot my fear entirely.

We walked on, crossing wide stretches of sand, until finally the cottage loomed before us. Its foundation was buried in deep sand, but I saw it was built upon rock. It resembled the sexton’s cottage, but sturdier, with thicker walls. The heavy door opened directly into a bare room, two small windows opposite each other, no glass. My hostess entered, walked straight to the far corner, and wrapped a long white cloth around her head and face. Then she closed the other door—through which the moon had looked—and lit a small horn lantern on the hearth.

“You are very welcome, Mr. Vane,” she said, calling me by the name I had lost.

“Your entertainment will be plain, but since night is not spent and day not near, it is better you stay indoors. Here you will be safe. A little lack is not a great hardship.”

“I thank you,” I replied. “But since you know my name, may I now know yours?”

“My name is Mara.”

I remembered the sexton and his black cat.

“Some take me for Lot’s wife,” she continued, “lamenting Sodom. Others say I am Rachel, weeping for her children. But I am neither.”

“I thank you again, Mara,” I said. “May I lie here on your floor until morning?”

“At the top of that stair,” she said, “you will find a bed. Some have slept better than they expected. Others have lain awake all night and slept all the next day. It is not soft, but better than the sand—and no hyenas sniff about it.”

The stair was narrow and steep, leading to a garret with no ceiling or partitions, and a single low dormer window. Under the sloping roof stood a narrow bed, its white coverlet stark and chillingly familiar—like the couches in the chamber of death. On the table, a dry loaf and a cup of cold water. For one who had eaten only fruit for months, it was a feast.

“I must leave you in the dark,” she called from below. “This lantern is all the light I have. There are things to do tonight.”

“It is of no consequence,” I said. “To eat, drink, lie down, sleep—these can be done in darkness.”

“Rest in peace,” she murmured.

I ate the bread, drank every drop of water, and lay down. The bed was hard, the covering thin, the night cold. I dreamed I was in the chamber of death, between the warrior and the lady with the healing wound.

I woke in the middle of the night, thinking I heard animals prowling. “Desert creatures scenting after me,” I muttered, reassured by the safety of the cottage. But then—a rough purr rose into a fierce howl beneath my window. I leapt from the bed.

Outside, in the full glow of the moon, a tall woman stood, draped in white, her back to me. She bent over a large white animal—like a panther—patting it with one hand, pointing with the other toward the moon, then drawing a line down to the horizon. Instantly, the creature darted off with impossible speed in that direction.

I watched it vanish. Then I looked back—she was gone.

I searched the distance, but saw only a faint white speck—or perhaps only imagined it.

What did it mean? What mission sent that beast?

I shuddered and returned to bed.

Then I remembered: when I’d lain in the hollow, the moon was setting. Now, just hours later, she was high in the sky—full and bright.

“Everything is uncertain here,” I whispered. “Even the heavens.”

Later, I learned this world had multiple moons, each following different paths. But the laws governing them remained beyond my grasp.

I slept again, undisturbed.

In the morning, I found bread and water waiting—this time, a larger loaf. I ate half. Mara sat beside me, wrapped in silence, until I asked how to reach Bulika.

“Go up the riverbed until it vanishes,” she said. “Then turn right until you reach a forest. You may spend a night there, but leave at dawn, facing the rising moon. Keep that direction until you meet a running stream. Cross it at right angles, and go straight until you see the city on the horizon.”

I thanked her, then ventured, “Last night, I saw your messenger. She understood you perfectly—moved so fast, so sure. If I had such a guide…”

She interrupted. “She went to Bulika—the shortest way.”

“How wonderfully intelligent she looked!”

“Astarte knows her work well enough to be sent.”

“Do you have many such messengers?”

“As many as I need.”

“Are they hard to teach?”

“They need no teaching. They are all of a certain breed—but none are alike. Their origin is so natural, it would seem incredible.”

“May I know it?”

“A new one came to me last night—from your head while you slept.”

I laughed. “Everyone here loves mystery.”

“Some chance word of yours sparked an idea—and in this form, she embodies the truth.”

“Then the creature is mine!” I exclaimed.

“Not at all,” she said. “Only what exists through our will can truly be ours.”

“Ah,” I thought, “a metaphysician too.”

I paused. “May I take the leftover bread?”

“You will need no more today,” she said.

“To-morrow, I may.”

She rose and walked to the door. “It has nothing to do with tomorrow—but you may take it if you wish.”

She opened the door and stood holding it. I picked up the loaf—but hesitated, longing to see her face.

“Must I go?” I asked.

“No one sleeps in my house two nights in a row.”

“I thank you for your hospitality,” I said. “Farewell.”

As I turned to leave, she murmured, “The time will come when you must stay with me many days and many nights.”

“Willingly,” I replied.

“Nay—not willingly,” she said.

I thought, *She’s right—I would not choose to return.* But instantly, my heart reproached me. Before I crossed the threshold, I turned back.

She stood in the center of the room. Her white garments flowed like waves at her feet, and the wrappings of her face lay scattered among them. Her face—revealed at last—was beautiful as a starlit night. Her great gray eyes looked upward, tears tracing paths down her pale cheeks. She reminded me of the sexton’s wife—though one seemed untouched by sorrow for ages, the other as if weeping constantly beneath her veil. Yet in her eyes, a quiet hope flickered: *Weeping may endure for a night, but joy cometh in the morning.*

I bowed my head, ready to kneel and beg forgiveness—when I looked up and found myself outside a doorless house.

I circled it, searching for an entrance. Nothing. I stopped beneath a window, about to cry out my confession—when a sudden shriek tore through the air, a wailing howl that froze my blood.

Something leapt from the window above and landed beyond me. I turned—there, a large gray cat, fur bristling, racing toward the riverbed.

I fell face-first into the sand, and within the house, I seemed to hear the gentle sobbing of one who suffered—but did not repent.

I rose, determined to continue my journey, and walked on through the endless desert miles. How I longed for a mountain, or even a towering rock, from which I might gaze beyond the desolate plain and the cracked, lifeless channels—any vantage point that might reveal a glimmer of hope on the horizon. But what good would sight ever do me? It is not what lies outside a man that shapes his fate, but what stirs within him. The event is not in the vision, but in the transformation it brings. To foresee is not to understand—otherwise, prophecy would bloom more often from the soul’s hidden depths.

The sun was halfway to the edge of the sky when I saw before me a jagged, rocky rise. Yet by the time I reached it, the desire to climb had vanished. Instead, I craved only stillness, rest. The sun dipped low, casting its final light, and the air began to darken. At my feet lay a soft, emerald moss—a royal bed beneath a sky of fading fire. I collapsed onto it, and as soon as my head touched the earth, I heard again the distant murmur of water: broken songs rising from buried streams, echoing through stone-choked channels. The music was wild, yet sublime—an ethereal chaos played by the aquarian harp, sending ripples of sound into my ears. What wonders Händel might have woven from such a refrain—the gurgle, the bell-like drip, the melodies that intertwined and then dissolved into one another! As I listened, my eyes wandered up the steep slope above me, tracing the ancient scars where once a cataract thundered down, carving its path through the stone. My heart swelled at the memory of that great tumult—waves dancing in their reckless fall, merging into a single, roaring organ of sound below.

But soon the hidden brooks lulled me into sleep, their lullabies weaving through my dreams.

I woke before dawn, eager to see what lay beyond the ridge. Alas, nothing but a vast expanse of fine, shifting sand stretched before me. No trace remained of the river that had once plunged over the rocks. The wind had filled its course with dust, leveling it into an empty plain. Looking back, I realized the river had split into two branches—one I’d followed to this spot, the other leading toward the Evil Wood. The wood now loomed faintly on the far horizon between them. Before me, the desert stretched endlessly. To the right, however, I saw a subtle rise in the skyline—a hint of forest, perhaps, the one my hostess had spoken of. I sat down and fumbled in my pocket for the half-loaf I had brought. Only then did I grasp her meaning. The bread was no longer food—it had shrunk, hardened, turned to stone. I tossed it aside and set off again.

By noon, I passed a few tamarisk and juniper trees, then stunted firs. As I pressed onward, thickets grew denser, pines taller, until I stood beneath a forest much like the one where the Little Ones found their children—perhaps the same woods, extended across time and space. But what difference did *where* matter, if *everywhere* was indistinguishable from *nowhere*? I had not yet done anything to make any place real. I was not truly alive—I was merely dreaming of living. A consciousness with a view, but no purpose. In my old world, I had been nothing more than this; now I knew it. I told myself: if I glimpsed the mirror here, I would turn away. I would not let it lure me back into the illusion of my former self. Here, I could become something—not just a ghost of memory, but a being shaped by action.

I could not bear the thought of returning—of having begun so many times, yet never completing anything. The Little Ones would face their destinies. The witch I feared, I would never meet. The dead would awaken without me. And I? I would wake to realize I had dreamed—and that all my wandering had led nowhere. I would rather walk forever than end like that.

I ventured deeper into the forest, weary, seeking shelter. The trees were large, standing in orderly rows, almost geometric in their spacing. Between them, open spaces allowed clear sight in every direction. There was little undergrowth. The silence was profound—like a cathedral emptied of worshipers. Occasionally, a swift shadow darted across my line of sight, or a slow-moving creature crept through the distance. Always far away, always unseen. They only deepened the emptiness.

I heard birdsong, saw butterflies—some dazzling in color, others pure white, radiant as snow. When I came upon a clearing where roses bloomed among the pines, I hoped it meant a dwelling nearby. I followed the trail of blossoms, hungry for the voice, the face, the presence of kindred life—any living soul, human or otherwise, whom I might begin to know.

What a hell it must be, I thought, to wander alone—trapped in oneself, unable to expand into another’s existence, bound by the narrow cords of one’s own nature. An eternal prisoner in the dungeon of the self.

I began to understand: one cannot truly live alone—even for oneself—unless in the company of others. And even then, it becomes terrifyingly possible. Evil exists only through good. Selfishness is merely a parasite feeding on the tree of life.

In my old world, I sang alone, often without thinking. Here, no song escaped my lips. There, I sang freely; here, I thought deeply—but never sang. There, I had no close friend; here, even the affection of an idiot would feel divine.

“If only I had a dog to love,” I sighed, staring at the man I once was—the man who chose books over people, pens over faces, stories over lives. If an author appeared while I read his tale, I wished him gone so I could return to the fiction. I preferred the dead to the living, thoughts to thinkers.

“Any man,” I said now, “is worth more than the greatest book.”

I had neglected my brothers and sisters. Now, even the dead were gone—no comfort left.

The forest thinned further. The pines grew immense, their trunks like columns reaching for the heavens. Other trees joined them—richer, more varied. The roses became trees, their blooms astonishing in splendor.

Then I saw it—a shape that seemed a great house or castle. But its form was blurred, uncertain. Was it a trick of shadows and foliage? As I approached, the lines held, yet never clarified. When I stood before it, I still could not say what it was. Not a habitable structure—more likely a ruin swallowed by ivy and roses. Yet I found no trace of masonry, no wall, no foundation. Again and again, I thought I saw architecture—but each time, it dissolved upon closer look.

Could it be that the ivy had consumed a grand edifice, preserving its shape in tangled roots and vines? I could not be sure.

Before me was a rectangular void—a doorway without a door. I stepped through, and found myself in a vast open space—like a hall, but covered in grass and flowers. The walls and roof were made of ivy, vine, and roses. No better place to spend the night.

I gathered dry leaves, piled them in a corner, and lay down. A crimson sunset painted the hall. The night was warm. I stared upward at the living ceiling—branches crisscrossing, leaves forming clouds, patches of higher canopy peering through. My eyes drifted, entangled in the greenery, until the sun sank and darkness crept in.

The red roses turned black. Then only yellow and white remained. When they too faded, stars emerged—hanging in the leaves like live topazes, pulsing with color, flashing, shimmering. I was beneath a tree from Aladdin’s cave.

Then I noticed the nests—tiny heads popping out, chirping softly, vanishing again. Rustlings, whispers, small prayers. As night deepened, the heads stilled. Every mother bird nestled her young. God’s nursery rested beneath the waves of sleep.

A flutter drew my gaze—then an owl swept silently across the sky. I caught only a glimpse, but felt the cool breath of his wings several times. The birds didn’t stir. They knew he sought mice, not chicks.

At midnight, I awoke—roused by a quiet revelry. The sounds were near, yet subdued, almost delicate. My eyes were dazzled at first, seeing nothing. Then clarity returned.

I was lying on my leaves in the corner of a magnificent hall. Before me danced a crowd—men in rich garments, women in flowing robes. None seemed to notice me. Dance after dance unfolded—each movement a story of life: meetings, passions, partings. As a student of Shakespeare, I recognized some—the minuet, the pavin, the hey, the coranto, the lavolta. Their costumes matched the age of their dances.

A moon had risen, casting light through the leafy roof. But so many shadows crossed it that I could barely distinguish faces. Yet something was wrong. I sat up to see better.

Heavens—could those be faces?

They were skulls—bone gleaming, jaws bare, noses truncated, teeth lipless, fixed in grimaces that could never smile. Some teeth flashed white and sharp—murderous. Others were stained, broken, colored by the earth they seemed to have lain in for centuries.

And the eye sockets—were not empty. Each held a lidless, living eye!

In those ruined faces, eyes burned—glowing, flashing, sparkling—every color, every shape, every expression. A proud, dark eye, lustrous and condescending—more terrible for its beauty. A languishing, lovely eye—more repulsive for its longing. And the dim, sad eyes—less incongruous, yet infinitely sorrowful, pulling at the heart despite the horror.

I rose, stepping among them, desperate to understand. Were they souls? Or mere phantasms of the past? Not a gesture, not a glance, betrayed awareness of me. I was invisible to them. Were they aware of each other? Did they see one another as I saw them? Or were they each trapped in their own dream?

Did they know how they appeared—to each other, to themselves? Death with living eyes.

Had they used their faces not to communicate, not to express thought or feeling, but to conceal? Had they made their faces masks—and now, stripped of them, were condemned to walk without faces until they repented?

“How long,” I wondered, “must they flaunt their facelessness before faceless eyes?”

How long would the punishment last? Had they finally begun to love? To wise? To feel shame?

I heard no words. Saw no movement of mouth. Were they silenced because of lying? With their eyes, they spoke—pleading, yearning to be understood. Was it truth—or falsehood—that flickered there?

They seemed to know each other. Did they see one skull beautiful, another plain? They had studied skulls long enough to tell.

My body passed through theirs unimpeded. Was I flesh, and they spirit? Or was I spirit, and they flesh? When a dancer brushed against me, I felt them pass through—man or woman, I sensed their passage, though I could not see.

On some skulls, hair clung—beautifully styled, golden or black, yet horrifying on bone. Often, the outer ear remained, and at its tip, the jewel Sidney called “the pearl of the ear”—a diamond, opal, or pearl—glimmering beneath the dark locks or pale curls, like sunrise on ivory or moonlight on cloud.

I looked down—daintily arched instep. Up—plump shoulders supporting a full neck—only to see it wither mid-way, becoming the fluted shaft of a gibbous skull.

The music grew wilder. The dance faster. Eyes blazed. Jewels sparkled. Color and fire lit the pallid grins, weaving a ghastly tapestry of motion—when suddenly, silence.

Every eye turned to the doorway.

There stood a woman—perfect in form, in posture, in hue—gazing upon them as from a pedestal. The dancers froze—“like one forbid”—struck dead by the vision of life that killed.

“Dead things,” her scornful glance declared, “I live!”

Then, as if stirred by a sudden wind, they turned to one another, broke into new motion, their eyes now glowing with shared triumph.

“You too,” they seemed to say, “will soon grow weak. You will become like us.”

I turned back to the woman—and saw a small dark shadow at her side.

She noticed the change in the dead stares. She looked down. Understood. Pressed her hands to the shadow, gave a stifled cry—and fled.

The birds rustled in their nests. A flash of joy lit the dancers’ eyes.

Then—suddenly—a warm wind swept through, growing stronger. It blew out every light.

Yet the low moon still glimmered on the horizon, sickly trying to shine. And the eyes—so many eyes—still glowed with a turbid radiance. I could still see clearly.

As if each figure were a snowman, they began to collapse. Flesh peeled away in papery flakes, falling like dirty snow beneath their clothes. Garments fluttered down in rags. Skeletons emerged—white, bare, lank—standing amid the ruins.

A faint rattling shiver ran through the naked forms. One by one, the eyes went out.

Darkness closed around me—complete, lonely.

For a moment, the leaves fluttered in unison. Then the wind ceased. The owl floated silently through the silent night.

Not once had I been afraid.

True, anyone crossing into a new world must leave fear behind. But I did not claim courage. I simply was not afraid. I did not know why. Nor did I know why I might have been. I feared not even fear—the most dangerous danger of all.

I stepped out into the wood, ready to continue my journey. Another moon was rising. I turned my face toward it.

I had not taken more than ten steps when something odd caught my eye. I drew closer, curious, and found myself before an ancient carriage—rotted and crumbling, yet still standing upright on its massive wheels. On either side of the pole, where horses once pulled it forward, lay two skeletal steeds, their heads raised like grim sentinels. From those hollow skulls, shriveled reins stretched upward to the bony hand of a coachman seated stiffly upon his tattered seat. The doors were gone; inside, two skeletons sat slumped in opposite corners.

As I watched, they stirred. With a dry rattle of brittle bones, each lurched from the door beside them. One collapsed instantly, lying motionless. The other staggered upright, joints grinding as if rusted shut, and slowly crept along the side of the carriage, gripping the frame for support. Its thin leg bones trembled under its weight. At last, it knelt beside the fallen one and tried to lift it—nearly falling itself in the effort.

After a long struggle, the prostrate skeleton rose into a sitting position. It turned its yellow skull this way and that, then, ignoring its companion, grasped the spokes of the rear wheel and pulled itself half to its feet. There it stood, back turned, hands clutching one knee joint as if it might fall apart at any moment.

The kneeling figure rose next, with great difficulty, and addressed the other.

“Have you hurt yourself, my lord?” it asked, voice distant and muffled, as though carried by a wind that did not belong to this world.

“Yes,” came the reply, rougher and more strained. “You would do nothing to help me—and now this cursed knee is out!”

“I did my best, my lord.”

“No doubt, my lady—but it was bad! I thought I’d never stand again. Bless my soul—madam—are you out in your bones?”

She glanced down at herself.

“I have nothing else to be out in,” she replied. “And you, at least, cannot complain. But what does it mean? Am I dreaming?”

“You may be dreaming,” he said, “but this knee forbids me the illusion. Ha! I see—I too have nothing to walk in but bones. Not so unbecoming for a man, however. I trust these aren’t *my* bones—every one aches worse than the last, and this loose knee worst of all. The bed must’ve been damp—and I far too drunk to know it.”

“Probably, my lord of Cokayne.”

“What? What?!” He stared at her. “You make me think I’m dreaming—aches and all! How do you know the name my bullies used to call me? I don’t remember you!”

“Anyhow,” she said, “you have no right to take liberties. My name is—well, I am lord—tut, tut! What do you call me when I’m… when you’re sober? I can’t recall. Why—what *is* my name? I must’ve been very drunk when I went to bed. Often am.”

“You come so seldom to mine,” she said, “that I don’t know. But I’ll take your word for that.”

“I hope so.”

“If for nothing else.”

“Hoity toity! I never told you a lie in my life.”

“You never told me anything but lies.”

“Upon my honour—why, I never saw the woman before!”

“You knew me well enough to lie to, my lord.”

“I do seem to dream I’ve met you before—but upon my oath, there’s nothing to recognize you by! Out of your clothes—who knows who you might be? One thing I *can* swear—never saw you so undressed before! By heaven, I have no recollection of you!”

“I’m glad to hear it. My recollections of you are less distasteful. Good morning, my lord.”

She turned away, hobbled a few paces, clacking softly, then stopped.

“You’re just as heartless as—any other woman, madam!” he called after her. “Where in this hell shall I find my valet? What was that cursed fool’s name?”

He twisted his bare skull on its creaking neck, still clutching his knee.

“I’ll be your valet for once,” she said, turning back. “What can I do for you? It’s not easy to tell.”

“Tie my leg on, of course, you fool! Can’t you see it’s nearly off? Heigho, my dancing days!”

She searched the ground with her empty eyes and found a piece of tough grass. She wound it tightly around the shattered joint.

When she finished, he tested it with cautious stamps.

“You used to stamp rather differently, my lord,” she remarked, rising.

“Eh? What?” He looked at her again. “Now I think of it—it seems I used to hate you!”

“Naturally, my lord. You hated many people—your wife, of course, among them.”

“Oh, I begin to remember… I must have been somewhere a long time. Really forget—there! Your damned bit of grass is breaking!”

“We used to get on pretty well together, eh?”

“Not that I recall, my lord. The only happy moments I had with you were scattered over the first week of our marriage.”

“Was that the way of it? Ha, ha! Well, it’s over now, thank goodness.”

“I wish I could believe it. Why were we sitting in that carriage? It wakes fear.”

“I think we were divorced, my lady.”

“Hardly enough. We’re still together.”

“A sad truth—but capable of remedy. The forest seems extensive.”

“I doubt it. I doubt.”

“I’m sorry I can’t think of a compliment to pay you—without lying. Judging by your figure and complexion, you’ve lived hard since I last saw you. I can’t surely be quite so naked as your ladyship! I beg your pardon, madam—I trust you’ll take it as jesting in a dream. It matters little, though. Dreaming or waking, all’s appearance. You can’t be certain of anything—and that’s as good as knowing there’s nothing. Life teaches even fools that.”

“It has taught me the fool I was to love you.”

“You weren’t the only one. Women fell for me—had a trick of it. I’d forgotten you were one.”

“I did love you, my lord—a little—once.”

“Ah, there was your mistake, my lady. You should have loved me much—devotedly, savagely—eternally. Then I’d have tired of you sooner—and not hated you so bitterly afterward. But let bygones be bygones. Where are we? Locality is the question. To be or not to be is NOT the question.”

“We are in the other world, I presume.”

“Granted. But which other world? This can’t be hell.”

“It must be. There’s marriage here. You and I are damned in each other.”

“Then I’m not like Othello—damned in a fair wife. Oh, I remember my Shakespeare, madam.”

She picked up a broken branch from a bush, balanced it in her hand, and walked away, tossing her tiny skull.

“Give that stick to me!” he cried. “I need it more than you.”

She gave no answer.

“You mean to make me beg?”

“Not at all, my lord. I mean to keep it.”

She continued her slow retreat.

“Give it me at once! I want it! I require it!”

“Unfortunately, I think I require it myself,” she replied, walking faster now, her joints cracking sharper, bones clinking.

He lunged after her—but stumbled. His knee-grass had burst. He swore and grabbed his leg.

“Come and tie it properly!” he shouted—but only a weak whistle escaped his mouth.

She turned.

“Come and tie it up instantly!” he repeated.

She took another step.

“I swear I will not touch you!” he roared.

“Swear on, my lord. No one’s here to believe you. But pray—don’t lose your temper. Or you’ll shake yourself to pieces. And where to find string enough to bind all your crazy joints? I can’t say.”

She returned, knelt again—first placing the disputed stick beyond his reach.

The moment she re-tied his knee, he lunged for her, fingers clawing at her hair. They slipped off the smooth bone.

“Disgusting,” he muttered, seizing her upper arm-bone instead.

“You’ll break it!” she warned, looking up.

“I will, then!” he snapped, wrenching.

“I won’t tie your leg again next time it comes loose!” she threatened.

He twisted her arm violently—but her bones held better than his.

She reached toward the broken branch.

“That’s right—hand me the stick!” he grinned.

She swung it with such force that a bone in her sound leg snapped.

He fell, choking with curses.

She laughed.

“Now you’ll have to wear splints always,” she said. “Such dry bones never mend.”

“You devil!” he spat.

“At your service, my lord. Shall I fetch you a couple of wheel-spokes? Neat—but heavy, I fear.”

He turned his bony face away. He didn’t answer. Only groaned.

I marveled he hadn’t shattered on impact.

She rose and walked away—not without grace, I thought.

“What can come of this?” I murmured. “They’re too wretched for any world—and this can’t be hell. The Little Ones are here. The sleepers too. What does it mean? Can anything ever be right for skeletons?”

“There are words too big for you and me,” a voice whispered near me. “ALL is one. EVER is another.”

I looked around—but saw no one.

“You are not in hell,” the voice continued.

“Neither am I,” I replied. “But those skeletons are.”

As I spoke, I saw the raven perched on a beech branch above. In the same breath, it dropped—landed on the ground—and became the thin old man from the library, long-nosed, long-coated.

“The male was never a gentleman,” he said. “In this bony stage of decay, with his skeleton showing through his skin, and his character outside his manners—he doesn’t look like one. The female is less vulgar. Has a little heart. But with society’s restraints gone—here they are: just as they were, and always were.”

“Tell me, Mr. Raven,” I said, “what will become of them?”

“We shall see. In their day, they were the handsomest couple at court. Even now, in their dry bones, they cling to that old reputation. To see their faces again—may yet change them. They felt rich while they had pockets—but already feel pinched. My lord once saw my lady as a worthless burden—tired of her beauty, spent her money. Now he needs her to mend his joints. These changes hold roots of hope. Besides—they can’t go far from each other. No others of their kind nearby. They’ll grow weary of hatred—and begin to love. For love, not hate, is deepest in what Love ‘loved into being.’”

“I saw many more like them an hour ago—in the hall nearby.”

“Of their kind—but not of their sort,” he replied. “For years, these two will see none such as you saw last night. Those are centuries ahead. You saw them dressing themselves—barely. They can’t keep clothes long—only part of the night. But they’re growing steadily stronger. Soon they’ll develop faces. Every grain of truth adds a thread to their humanity. Nothing but truth can appear. Whatever is—must seem.”

“Are they upheld by hope?”

“They are upheld by hope—but they don’t know it. To understand it is still immeasurably beyond them.”

His sudden presence surprised me not at all. I was like a child—always wondering, never astonished.

“Did you come to find me, sir?” I asked.

“Not at all,” he said. “I have no anxiety about you. Such as you always return to us.”

“Then please—tell me—who am I, such as?”

“I cannot make my friend the subject of conversation,” he smiled.

“But when that friend is present?” I urged.

“I decline more strongly.”

“But when that friend asks?”

“Then most positively I refuse.”

“Why?”

“Because he and I would be talking of two persons—as if they were one. Your consciousness of yourself—and my knowledge of you—are worlds apart.”

His coat flared open—lapels flying, lappets lifting. I thought I saw the transformation of HOMO to CORVUS unfolding before me.

But the coat closed again.

And he added, almost casually:

“In this world—never trust a person who has once deceived you. Above all—never do anything such a one may ask you to do.”

“I’ll try to remember,” I said. “But I may forget.”

“Then some evil that is good for you will follow.”

“And if I remember?”

“Some evil that is not good for you—will not follow.”

The old man seemed to sink into the earth.

And in an instant, I saw the raven—several yards away—flying low and fast.

I walked on, my face turned toward the moon, who still hung low in the sky, casting her pale gaze into the heart of the forest. She was not bright tonight—no, she was dull and bruised, like a tarnished disc of ancient copper, heavy with sorrow. There was no cloud to share her silence, and the stars burned too fiercely, as if mocking her dimness. “Will this never end?” she seemed to whisper across the dark. We moved in opposite directions, yet for a long time we traveled side by side through the trees. I did not speak to her, my eyes fixed on the ground beneath my feet; but I felt her melancholy watching me, unblinking, unseen. She was the dead light; I, the living shadow.

Then something caught my eye—a patch of white under the wide arms of a tree. I stepped closer, drawn by its stark contrast against the shadows. As I neared, the shape became clearer: it was a body, though so thin it might have been mistaken for a skeleton. I knelt, touched it gently. Cold—yes, but not the cold of stone. It was the chill of life extinguished, of warmth that had once pulsed and now lay still.

At first, I thought it might be one of the wild dancers from the forest hall—some spectral Cinderella lost in the night, abandoned to the strange world outside. But no. This was no ghost. It was flesh, though nearly reduced to bone. The ribs stood out beneath skin stretched taut over every curve, like parchment over a frame. Her lips were drawn back, revealing teeth that gleamed with an unnatural sharpness in the moonlight. Her hair fell long and thick, black as midnight, soft to the touch even in death. She was tall—once graceful, perhaps—and utterly naked, stripped bare by some unseen hand or cruel fate.

How had she come here? Not by choice. She had fallen, surely, when strength left her. And then hunger took what remained. But how could she have wasted so completely? Where were the beasts that would have taken her clothes? Or the savages who might have left her behind? That her body remained untouched was not surprising—what was strange was that it was so perfectly preserved, so undecayed.

I rose slowly, standing tall in the quiet. I could not leave her exposed. Even in death, a woman’s body deserved dignity. A garment, any covering, demanded respect. To let her lie uncovered—exposed to the eyes of predators, to the slow erosion of time—was unthinkable. Yet the earth was hard, packed with roots, and I had only my hands.

At first glance, there was no sign of decay. But what was there to rot? Life had been drained away so thoroughly that nothing remained to break down. Could she still be alive? Might she breathe beneath that stillness? In this world, where dreams walked and rivers sang, anything seemed possible. Still, I must know.

I remembered the grapes I’d taken from the doorway of the forest hall—the last fruit I had. A few remained on the stem. Perhaps their juice could stir something within her. I placed a grape in her mouth, but there was no swallowing, no movement. I laid her upon a bed of pine needles and dry leaves, covered her with my own clothes, and piled more foliage over her. I hoped to trap the little warmth that lingered in her bones, to hold it until the sun returned.

Then I tried again. Another grape. Still nothing.

“Doubt,” I said aloud, “may not inspire action—but it is no reason to do nothing.”

Her skin clung so tightly to her bones that I dared not rub or press. Instead, I crawled into the heap of leaves, drew her close, and held her in my arms. My own warmth was fading, but I gave it all. I spent the rest of the night awake, listening, waiting. Her cold seeped into me, but I felt no return of heat. Had I fled from the sleeping beauties in their silver beds to lie beside this one? I had refused a dream—to find myself bound to a duty far greater.

Beneath the weary moon, I watched the dawn approach.

When the east began to lighten, I saw motion—not shapes, but a ripple near the ground. A large snake glided past, straight and unwavering. Then came what looked like a doe and her fawn. After them, two bear cubs followed by smaller ones. Soon, a herd of horses trotted by—smaller than any I had seen, yet full-grown, perfect in form. Some resembled draft horses, others warhorses, hunters, racers. They moved with purpose. Behind them came dwarf cattle and tiny elephants.

“Where are the children?” I whispered. “As soon as I can, I must go back for them.”

But I could not leave her. Not yet. I placed my hand on her chest. Was she warmer? Slightly. Not enough to prove life, but not colder either. Hope, fragile as it was, did not fade.

The sun rose at last, golden and innocent, peering over the edge of the world as if curious about the stir below. I stood, renewed. I removed the cloth I’d used to shield her face and eyes from the needles. Still, no breath. No twitch. Only silence.

In the morning light, I saw her face clearly—sunken, hollow, every bone defined. Her lips parted just enough to reveal her teeth. The skin was stretched tight, like a mask over a skull. But her hair—thick, black, alive—reminded me that life had once thrived here.

She was not just a corpse. She was a vessel. And perhaps, somewhere deep inside, a spark remained.

I followed the trail the animals had taken. It was well-worn, branching from many paths that merged into one. The trees thinned, and grass spread wide across the land. Ahead flowed a river, small but deep, its surface shimmering with a bluish mist that vanished as it rose. On the other bank, creatures grazed—small, peaceful, feeding in the morning light.

I knelt to drink—but the water was hot, and tasted metallic. I leapt up. This was warmth! The very thing I needed.

I ran back to her. Carrying her was easier than I expected. Though tall, she was astonishingly light—her bones delicate, her flesh gone. I held her like a child, cradling her against my shoulder. Every step I took, I feared she might dissolve into dust.

The water was too hot to plunge her in at once. I laid her on the bank and began bathing her with damp cloth, wetting each limb, each curve of her wasted frame.
