Her age was impossible to tell—only the hair suggested youth. Her eyelids were half-open, just enough to show the darkness beyond. It made her look more dead than ever—like a door cracked open to a room without light.

Still, I bathed her. I kept moving, ensuring no part cooled while another warmed. Gradually, her body grew warmer. At last, I ventured to submerge her—carrying her into the stream, keeping her face above water, letting the current flow around her limbs. Her hand remained clenched, unyielding, though the heat did not loosen it.

Ten minutes passed. I lifted her out, dried her, covered her with leaves, and ran to gather more. When I returned, she had not cooled. The warmth of the stream had penetrated her frame—though it was not life, it was *possible* life.

I searched the hills nearby and found the source: the river emerged from a rock, as if born from within the mountain. Inside, it rose quietly from the ground like a column, flowing along a narrow channel. I crept into the cave and saw a way—using fallen boughs, I could build a bridge over the stream, a platform kept warm by the water below.

I gathered branches, wove them together, layered them with moss and leaves. After several trips, I built a soft, dry, warm couch. Then I carried her to it, placing her gently upon the living bridge. She was so light I almost feared she would vanish into air.

I covered her again, placed a grape in her mouth—this time, I could open her lips slightly. The grape stayed, untouched, but I hoped some juice might reach her throat.

Days passed. Seven days and nights. I sat or slept beside her, always watching. Each morning, I bathed in the hot stream—each time, I felt reborn. I bathed her too, every day, and fed her a fresh grape. Once, a discoloration appeared on her side—I froze. But by morning, it was gone. I continued.

I ate berries and grapes from the forest, but I believed I could survive on the river alone. My dreams were of angels—wounded, unable to fly, who stayed with me, who loved me. But each time I woke, I saw only her: pale, still, untouched by time.

Adam waited for Eve. I waited for her.

I had learned loneliness. Not just absence—but emptiness. A man alone is not a man. He is a need. A possibility. To be whole, he must reflect another soul. Man is not self-sufficient—he is shaped by others, fed by their presence, defined by their love.

Without her, I would have fled to the beasts. Better to walk among them than to live alone. But now—now there was hope.

She might wake. She might see me. She might speak.

And if she did—if she opened her eyes—then I, the poorest of beings, might become human at last.

I awoke one morning from a deep, dreamless sleep, my right hand throbbing with pain. The back of it was swollen, the skin taut and warm, and at the center of the inflammation was a small, triangular wound—clean, precise, like the mark left by a leech. By midday, the swelling had receded; by nightfall, the injury was nearly gone, leaving only a faint scar. I searched the cave with feverish care—every stone turned, every crevice probed—but found no creature that could have caused such a bite. Nothing larger than a beetle dwelled in the shadows.

Days passed in quiet vigil. The body on the stone slab remained motionless, eyes closed, breath still. Yet I knew it was not dead. Decay did not touch it. The air around it held no scent of rot. And slowly, imperceptibly, changes began: the sharp angles of her bones softened, her limbs gained a subtle roundness, her skin lost its brittle, parchment-like quality. If life were returning, it was doing so in whispers. If the tide of existence had once receded to the edge of oblivion, perhaps it now stirred again. My heart swelled with hope each time I saw these signs. But doubt followed close behind. Twenty times a day I leaned over her, seeking movement, listening for breath. Twenty times I despaired. Then I would remember the first moment I found her—cold, hollow, almost beyond recognition—and hope would rise again.

One night, after hours of wakefulness, I rose to step outside. The moon hung full and bright above the trees, casting silver light into the cave. The air inside was cool and clear, but the heat of the day lingered near the walls. As I passed her, I glanced once more at her face.

“Bliss eternal!” I whispered aloud. “Are those… her eyes?”

They seemed to glow—dark orbs, like voids carved from midnight, luminous in their depth, shining through the pale pallor of her cheeks. I froze, breath caught in my chest. I crept closer, pulse hammering. Then I saw—the lids were shut. A trick of the moonlight? A hallucination born of longing? My joy collapsed into sorrow. I turned away, fell to the floor, and wept.

But then—I remembered. Her eyes *had* been open. Just for an instant. Now the narrow slit was gone. Perhaps she had blinked. Perhaps she was awake, holding them closed. Either way, they had moved. Life had claimed them. I lay down, tears drying on my face, and slept.

That night, I was bitten again. I woke gasping, throat parched, body weak. The wound—same shape, same location—was healing as before. I searched the cave anew, more thoroughly than ever, but found nothing. No trace. No sign.

I concluded that some large aquatic creature, perhaps a leech of unnatural size, came from the hot river to feed. “If it seeks blood,” I mused, “then while I am here, it will not touch her.”

The next morning, I fed her as usual—a peeled grape, seeds removed, placed gently between her lips. This time, her mouth shifted. A tiny movement. A reflex. But it was enough. I KNEW she lived.

Hope surged within me. I began to think of clothing—something she could wear when she rose. I went to the forest, searching for materials. Among the dry husks of prickly pear leaves, I found fibrous skeletons, tough and flexible. I gathered them, dried them under the sun, and separated the layers into threads. With the sharp tip of an aloe leaf and natural fibers, I stitched together three layers of the material, fashioning two garments—one for her waist, another for her shoulders. I made sandals from woven strips, binding them tightly.

For days, no further change. But then—she took the grapes more deliberately. Her form grew fuller. Her skin gained warmth, color. Still, her eyes remained closed. And fear returned—not of death, but of something worse: that this growth was not life, but corruption, some monstrous fungus feeding on her flesh.

Then came the third bite. And the fourth. Each time, exactly every three days. Always at night. Always while I slept. Always one bite—on the neck or arm. Never during daylight. I lay awake for hours, listening, watching, but never heard it approach. Never felt it strike. It came like a shadow, left without a sound.

Eventually, I gave up trying to catch it. My strength waned. Blood loss was taking its toll. But I cared little. For across the cave, life was stirring. A soul was awakening. We would leave together. I would recover. She would rise.

The garments were finished. I stood back, admiring my work—clumsy, yes, but functional. I began weaving sandals.

Then, one night, I woke suddenly—drenched in sweat, breath ragged. I struggled to rise, desperate for air. Halfway to the entrance, a soft rustle stopped me.

A voice—soft, low, in my own tongue—spoke.

“I caught the vile thing,” it said. “In the very act.”

She was alive. She spoke.

I choked back my joy. “What creature?” I breathed.

“The creature that was biting you.”

“What was it?”

“A great white leech.”

“How big?”

“Not far from six feet long, I should think.”

“You saved my life—perhaps!” I said, my voice breaking. “But how? How could you touch it?”

“I did,” she replied simply. I thought I saw her shudder.

“Where is it? What did you do?”

“I threw it into the river.”

“It will return,” I warned.

“I do not think I could have killed it, even if I had known how.” She paused. “I heard you moaning. I rose. Saw it at your neck. Pulled it away. Could not hold it. Only managed to hurl it into the water. Heard it splash.”

“We’ll kill it next time,” I said. But then I fainted.

When I regained consciousness, the sun was high. She stood a few paces away, clad in the rough garments I had made. Even in that crude attire, she was magnificent—tall, poised, regal.

I HAD seen her eyes. They had shone all night. Darker than the deepest night, yet brighter than the dawn. Now they burned with cold fire.

She regarded me silently. Her face was pale, unreadable. No emotion—only question.

I rose. “We must go,” I said. “The white leech—”

I stopped. A strange smile touched her lips.

“Did you find me there?” she asked, pointing to the cave.

“No,” I answered. “I brought you here.”

“You brought me?”

“Yes.”

“From where?”

“The forest.”

“What have you done with my clothes… and my jewels?”

“You had none when I found you.”

“Then why did you not leave me?”

“Because I hoped you were not dead.”

“Why should you have cared?”

“Because I was alone. And I wanted you to live.”

“You would have kept me enchanted for my beauty!” she said, scorn lacing every word.

Her tone ignited my anger. “There was no beauty left in you,” I said. “Only bone and skin.”

“Then why not let me die?”

“Because you were of my kind.”

“Of YOUR kind?” she laughed bitterly. “I thought so. But I was wrong.”

“Doubtless you pitied me.”

“Never has woman deserved pity more—or less of any other feeling.”

Her expression twisted—pain, humiliation, fury—too deep for words. She turned away, silent.

Darkness filled her eyes—starless, infinite. The hatred for the one who had restored her had extinguished their light.

“Had you failed to rouse me,” she asked suddenly, without moving, “what would you have done?”

“I would have buried you.”

“Buried me? Buried THIS?” she shrieked, wheeling toward me, arms outstretched, eyes flashing like ice. “You speak of burying me! But I was not dead! I was in a trance!”

“Nay,” I said firmly. “I saw no sign of life. I saw only decay. Had I seen even a hint of rot, I would have buried you at once.”

“Dog of a fool!” she cried. “I was only asleep! Samoil—what a fate! Go fetch the she-savage from whom you stole this hideous disguise!”

“I made it for you,” I said. “It’s ugly, but I did my best.”

She straightened, tall and proud. “How long have I lain unconscious?”

“A woman could not make that dress in a day.”

“Not in twenty. Not in thirty.”

“Ha! How long do you pretend I’ve been unconscious? Answer me.”

“I cannot say how long you’d been there when I found you. But what remained of you was skin and bone. More than three months ago. Your hair was beautiful—nothing else. I did what I could.”

“My poor hair,” she murmured, drawing a great coil of it forward. “It will take more than three months to bring YOU back to life. I suppose I must thank you—though I cannot say I’m grateful.”

“There’s no need,” I said. “I would have done the same for any woman. Or man.”

“How is it my hair is not tangled?” she asked, running her fingers through it.

“It always drifted in the current.”

“Drifted? What do you mean?”

“I could not have revived you without bathing you daily in the hot river.”

She shuddered, staring at the rushing water. Then she turned to me.

“We must understand each other,” she said. “You have done me the two worst things possible—you forced me to live, and you put me to shame. Neither can I forgive.”

She raised her left hand and flung it outward, as if to push me away. Something cold and hard struck my forehead.

When I came to, I was on the ground—wet, trembling, half-drowned in memory.

I stood up, dazed and trembling, my heart a hollow drum in my chest. She was gone—vanished like mist under a rising sun. The silence that followed her departure was heavier than any storm. The woman I had dragged from death’s edge, the one I had nurtured back into life, had fled. And now, the world felt stripped bare, as if all meaning had been torn away.

I could not stay here. Not alone. Was it my fault? Had I wronged her in some way I couldn’t see? I had saved her—not for myself, but because she deserved to live. Yet she turned from me with hatred in her eyes. How could she despise me? I had touched her only with care, with reverence. Like a father cradling a child lost to the world, I had held her through fever and pain. And yet… she rejected me.

Was it pride? Or something deeper?

My thoughts spiraled as I moved forward, following the trail of her steps across the soft grass. I’d just taken a quick plunge into the river—cold water, sharp and bracing—before setting out after her. The leeches had drained me, the blow from her hand had weakened me further, but now, strange as it seemed, strength returned. My limbs felt lighter, more alive. I could keep pace with her without effort.

“Is this the end?” I whispered, the words hanging in the air like a prayer.

Her face flashed before me—the anger, the coldness. I understood her rage at being forced back into life. But beyond that… what had I done? Could modesty itself recoil from kindness? Could a woman who knew every act I’d performed still feel shame toward me? I had never crossed a line. I had never once betrayed trust. And yet, she loathed me.

Had all my labor—my desperate hope—been for nothing? Was I to be repaid only with scorn?

“No,” I said aloud, voice firming. “Beauty must have a heart. Even if buried deep beneath ice and shadow, it must exist. The deeper it lies, the more fiercely it will awaken when stirred. To awaken that heart would be greater than giving her mere life. It would give her purpose. It would give her soul.”

She climbed a gentle hill ahead of me, walking with steady grace, as though she knew exactly where she was going. Then I noticed: she was pulling ahead. I summoned what remained of my strength—and it surged within me. My veins hummed with renewed energy. My body felt almost weightless. I moved like wind over water, catching up swiftly.

She didn’t look back. Not once.

She walked like a goddess of ancient myth—swift, serene, untouched by haste. I closed the distance until I was just three yards behind. Then she turned.

Not abruptly, but with a fluid elegance that made the motion seem eternal. She stopped. Her face was calm. No sign of fatigue. No breathlessness. Only paleness—pure, luminous, like moonlight on snow. Her eyes… they were vast, absorbing the sky, illuminating everything around them. It was nearly noon, yet the world felt cloaked in night, as if an invisible dew had fallen, making stars appear enormous and near.

"Why do you follow me?" she asked, voice quiet, yet edged with command—as if she had never seen me before.

"I have lived," I said, "only for the hope of seeing your eyes again."

"You will not be spared," she replied, coldly. "Stop where you stand."

"Not until I know you are safe," I insisted.

"Then take the consequences," she said, and continued forward.

But as she turned, she cast me a glance—and I froze.

It wasn't anger. Not even scorn. It was something worse: beauty so absolute, so terrifying in its purity, that it struck like a blade. I felt myself crumbling under its weight.

Despair flared, then snapped into resolve. I ran.

I caught up to her.

"Have pity on me!" I begged.

She ignored me.

I followed—like a child chasing a mother who pretends to leave him behind.

"I will be your slave!" I cried, reaching out, placing my hand gently on her arm.

She recoiled as if bitten by a serpent.

I cowered beneath the fire of her gaze—but could not turn away.

"Pity me," I pleaded again.

She resumed walking.

And so I followed.

The sun rose, crested the sky, and began its descent. Hours passed. I did not stop. She did not pause. Not once. Not even to breathe. The day bled into twilight, and darkness swallowed the land.

Still I followed.

We had walked through thick, yielding grass—soft as velvet underfoot—until suddenly, she stopped. Then she collapsed onto the ground.

There was still enough light to see that she was exhausted. Utterly spent.

I stood behind her, staring down.

Did I love her? I knew she was not good. Not in the way the world defines goodness. But I could not leave her.

I knelt beside her.

"Begone!" she hissed. "Do not dare touch me!"

Her arms lay limp at her sides—still, rigid, as if paralyzed.

Then—suddenly—they wrapped around my neck, tight as iron chains. She pulled me down, her lips pressing against my cheek.

A sharp pain pierced me—deep, electric. It pulsed through my veins. I could not move. Not a muscle.

Slowly, the pain faded.

A warmth spread through me—a dreamlike numbness, sweet and heavy. Then… nothing.

Darkness.

When I came to, the moon hung low, pale and distant, casting no light—only a faint glow in the black sky. My cheek stung. I touched it. Wet. Blood.

My neck ached. There, too, a damp spot.

I sighed, weary beyond measure.

I turned my head slowly.

And saw it.

The moonlight—once dim—now gathered around her. She stood bathed in a shimmering halo, glowing like a spirit from another realm.

I rose, unsteady, swaying toward her.

"Down!" she commanded, voice sharp as steel. "Follow me a step—if you dare!"

"I will," I murmured, voice cracking with effort.

"Set foot inside my city," she warned, "and my people will stone you. They do not welcome beggars."

I did not hear her.

Weak, half-conscious, I did not know I was moving—yet the distance between us shrank.

She stepped back. Raised her left hand. Clenched it.

And struck me across the forehead.

It was like being hit by a hammer forged from iron.

I fell.

Then I rose—cold, soaked, but clear-headed, strong.

No wound. No pain.

But why was I wet?

I looked around.

The moon had not risen higher. Time had barely passed.

She stood several yards away, her back to me.

She was doing something. I couldn’t tell what.

Then—she glowed.

And I realized: she had stripped off her clothes.

She stood naked in the moonlight—white as bone, radiant as starlight.

One moment she stood.

Then she fell forward.

A streak of white shot across the field—fast, low, silent.

At the same instant, the moon blazed to life, full and bright.

And I saw.

It was a creature—long-bodied, swift, bounding across the grass in great, curved leaps. Dark spots trailed along its back, like shadows clinging to it—perhaps from trees or foliage it had passed beneath.

"God of mercy," I breathed. "Is that thing heading for the city?"

From afar, I heard it—a sudden eruption of screams, of terror, spreading through the darkened town. The pale beast leapt from house to house, tearing, destroying, devouring.

I watched, frozen.

Then—behind me—another shape shot past.

Faster than the first.

Pure white. Larger. More powerful.

Its path led straight to the place where the lady had fallen.

My mouth went dry. I ran.

But by the time I reached the spot, there was no one.

Only her clothes—lying in the moonlight, abandoned.

I stared after the second beast.

It moved with impossible speed—leaping long distances, skimming the earth like a ghost. It followed the same path as the first, vanishing into the horizon.

But where was she?

Had the first beast attacked her silently? I hadn’t heard a scream.

Could it have carried her away?

Impossible. A creature bearing such a burden could not run so fast. And I would have seen it.

Yet—no trace.

I searched the area—thoroughly, desperately. Nothing.

Then I turned, and set out after the beasts.

Not knowing what I would find.

But knowing I could not stop.

The moon dimmed beneath a creeping veil of cloud, and from the sudden shadow emerged a figure—pale as bone, clutching a child to her chest. She ran with hunched shoulders, her movements frantic, every step trembling with fear. I moved along the same path, but she did not see me. Her breath came in ragged gasps; her eyes were wide with dread. "She’s being hunted," I whispered. "Something terrible is after her."

To chase would only terrify her further. Instead, I stepped into the trail ahead, blocking the unseen pursuer’s path.

For a moment, I stood still, watching her vanish into the mist. Then—behind me—a swift, silent rush. Before I could turn, something leapt over my head, striking my forehead with a sharp blow that sent me crashing to the ground. I rose instantly, blood trickling down my temple, but all I saw was a flash of white disappearing into the dark.

I gave pursuit, though my vision blurred with pain. But only a few steps brought a cry—raw, gut-wrenching—that tore through the night like a blade. My pace quickened. If it was too late… then nothing I could do would matter.

Soon, through the fog-softened moonlight, I saw a low, shuddering shape approaching. At first, I thought it another predator—crawling forward in broken leaps, dragging itself with unnatural effort. It moved on three legs, its left forepaw lifted high, trembling. Its skin gleamed white, dotted with dark oval marks. A faint rushing sound followed it—like water falling onto grass.

As it passed, I saw the source: blood streamed from the raised paw, trailing behind in a thin, red river that soaked into the earth beside me.

“It’s wounded,” I murmured. “Someone else must have struck it.”

Yet, at the sight of its suffering, pity gripped me. Even if I had held an axe, I could not have raised it.

It limped out of sight, bleeding still. “If it keeps losing blood,” I thought, “it won’t last long.”

I pressed on. Perhaps I could still help the woman. Maybe I’d find the one who had saved her.

A little farther, I found her—sitting on the grass, cradling the child. The baby slept, untouched.

“Can I help?” I asked.

She jumped, startled, reaching for her feet as if to flee.

I dropped to the ground. “Don’t be afraid. I was chasing the beast. But you’ve already beaten it—it passed me moments ago, bleeding so badly it can’t live long.”

Her voice trembled. “There’s no hope for that. Do you know whose beast she is?”

I hesitated. Strange thoughts stirred—whispers I’d heard, rumors of creatures bound to power beyond nature—but I said only, “I don’t know. Who is she?”

“The princess’s leopardess,” she replied. “And I killed her.”

“You? How?”

“I pounded her foot with a stone. As hard as I could. Did you not hear her scream?”

“I did. But I thought it was you.”

“No,” she said softly. “It was the leopardess. My voice was gone. When I saw her jaws near my baby’s neck, I grabbed a rock and crushed her paw.”

I stared. “You’re brave.”

She looked up, her face pale in the dim light. “I had no choice. That beast… she wouldn’t stop until she took both of us.”

“Tell me about her,” I said. “I’m new here.”

“If you’re going to Bulika,” she warned, “you’ll learn soon enough. But I will never return.”

“I am going to Bulika,” I said. “To see the princess.”

“Then beware. You’d better not go. But perhaps…” She paused. “The princess is kind—she says she is. But she has secrets.”

A rustle. The clouds thickened, swallowing the moon. I could barely make her out now.

“You’re not a threat,” I said. “What oath would you want?”

“She’s not of Bulika,” she whispered. “I can tell by your voice. And I’m not either. We trust no one there. They don’t believe in mercy. But I like your voice. And my baby sleeps. No mark. She’s safe.”

She touched the child’s cheek, her fingers shaking. “They say the princess has two white leopards. I’ve seen only one—the spotted one. Everyone knows her. If the princess hears of a newborn, she sends her. The leopard drinks the infant’s blood. Then the child dies… or grows up hollow, broken. I stayed because she was away. I thought I could wait. But she must have taken the beast with her—and returned just as I fled.”

She leaned forward. “I heard the SNIFF-SNUFF behind me. And I ran. Oh, how I ran!”

But the child lived. Unharmed.

“Where are you going?” I asked.

“To where no one ever speaks,” she said.

“And why does the princess do this?”

“She fears a prophecy,” the woman whispered. “That a child will kill her. So she refuses marriage. She sends witches to poison women’s dreams, to give them bitter herbs that prevent birth. Some say she trades with the Shadows to end the race. At night, we hear the beast prowling. She senses where babies are born—lies at doors, waiting. There are words that repel her… sometimes. But not always.”

She rose abruptly. “We must go. The beast may have reached the palace. She might send the other.”

“Let me carry your child,” I offered.

She didn’t answer. When I reached for the infant, she pulled her closer, shielding her with her body.

“I still don’t understand,” I said, walking beside her. “How could such a creature bleed so much?”

“Don’t go to the palace,” she warned. “At night, the walls whisper. Sounds like the dead trying to scream—but their mouths are sealed.”

She stopped. Turned. “Farewell.”

No more words. No glance back. Her footsteps faded into the grass, swallowed by the dark.

I stood alone, listening to the silence, wondering what waited in Bulika—and what fate awaited the child who had escaped the beast’s fangs.

I had lost all sense of direction, wandering in a state of restless confusion, when I stumbled into the path of a leopardess—her paw submerged in a crimson tide that surged against my ankles like a small stream. The force of it startled me, and I recoiled instantly, driven not only by instinct but by a vague, chilling suspicion: whose blood was this? Yet I did not flee entirely. Instead, I followed the sound—the steady, whispering rush of liquid—upward along its course, trusting it to lead me toward Bulika.

But soon doubt crept in. No beast, however vast, could sustain such a flow unless every vein in its body were torn open and replenished from some endless source. That made no sense. I dipped a finger into the current. It was cool, clear, and utterly unlike blood. It was water—softly murmuring, flowing unchannelled across the grass as if guided by unseen hands. A river without banks, born of mystery.

Still, I dared not drink. The taste of it might be poison, or worse—some elixir of illusion. But the mere touch of moisture on my skin revived me. My steps grew lighter, my breath steadier. I walked on, drawn by the faint glow of dawn breaking through the night’s veil.

Then, just as the sky began to pale, I saw it: a city rising against the horizon—its towers ancient, its walls weathered by time itself. It stood silent, solemn, as though carved from dreams long forgotten. I looked back at the stream. It was gone. Only the bent grass marked its path, and scattered pools glistened where it had once flowed. The fountain must have ceased somewhere near—perhaps within the city’s embrace.

Around Bulika lay gardens thick with unfamiliar plants—vegetables I couldn’t name, growing in neat rows beneath a sky too still for birds. There was no water here, no flowers, no signs of animals. The gardens pressed close to the walls, separated by mounds of gravel and refuse—waste thrown from above, perhaps by those who lived within.

The nearest gate stood half-open, unguarded, unbarred. Its hinges groaned with age, fixed in place as if neither opened nor closed since the beginning of time. I stepped inside.

The street stretched before me—long, empty, silent. Not a soul stirred. No footsteps echoed. No breath broke the stillness. Had I entered a city of the dead?

I turned back, climbed over heaps of dust, crossed several roads leading to other gates. I would not return until life stirred again. What was I seeking? What did I hope to find? I didn’t know. But I needed to see her again—the woman I had awakened. Not for love, not for companionship. She had awoken something dark in me—fears, suspicions, a pull toward the unknown. And yet, her presence held a power I could not resist. To understand her—to grasp even a fragment of her nature—was to gaze into wonders beyond imagination.

But I was reckless. Man should not seek knowledge at the cost of his soul. Yet I had done something irrevocable. I had brought back a force that was meant to die. And now, I bore responsibility for what might follow. I had learned she hated children. The Little Ones—were they in danger?

That was why I came. To learn their fate. To protect them. To understand.

Eventually, movement stirred in the city. I passed through another gate, wandered down narrow alleys lined with tall, shadowed houses, until I reached a small square. There, I sat on the base of a pillar crowned by a grotesque bat-like figure, watching the world wake.

Soon, figures appeared—slow, indifferent. I spoke to one. He glared, spat a curse, and walked on. I rose and continued deeper into the maze of streets, finding more people, all moving with the same dull rhythm. No children. Not one.

Then, near a gate, I encountered a group of young men—tall, broad-shouldered, with faces like those of the giants I’d met before. They surrounded me, eyes narrowing, then shoved, laughed, threw stones. I endured it. I didn’t want conflict. I wanted only to observe, to survive.

I appealed to others—those who seemed kinder—but none stopped. I was poor. That was enough. In Bulika, poverty was a crime. Deformity, sickness—they taxed these things. And the laws of their princess, which ensured the weak served the strong, were obeyed with grim pride.

Finally, I fled. No one chased me past the gate. Except one—a slow, clumsy man sitting there, eating bread. He picked up a stone to throw. But in his clumsiness, he hurled the bread instead.

I took it. He didn’t dare come after me. Beyond the walls, they were cowards.

I walked a short distance, lay down in the grass, ate the bread, and fell asleep. The sun warmed me, renewed my strength. When I woke, it was night. The moon hung high, bright and familiar—like an old friend. I thought it was the same moon that had watched over me during my first terrifying night in this world.

A cold wind blew from the city gate, carrying with it a sour, rotten smell. But it didn’t chill me. The sun had filled me with warmth.

I crept back into the city. Those few still awake huddled in corners, shivering against the blast.

I walked slowly through the narrow streets, when suddenly—a flash of white leapt across the road ahead. A great shape, glowing under the moonlight, bounded forward and vanished into the shadows.

I turned down a side lane—so narrow it barely allowed passage—but it led me to a wider street. As I stepped out, I saw it again: the creature, following something that moved like a man.

It was a shadow—but not like any shadow I had known. It was flat, two-dimensional, yet solid. Opaque. Where it stood, nothing behind it could be seen—not because it blocked light, but because it *consumed* sight. In darkness, it was blacker than night. In moonlight, it wore its own shadow like a cloak—no separate outline, no trailing form. It was a being of pure absence.

And behind it, stalking silently, was the leopardess—white as bone, radiant in the silver light. Her shadow slid beside her, dark and smooth, like ink on paper.

They moved together. From shadow into light. The Shadow deepened, becoming almost void. The leopardess blazed, glowing with inner fire.

I stood opposite, bare feet tapping softly on stone. The leopardess never glanced my way. But the Shadow—once, it seemed to turn. I lost its profile. For a heartbeat, I saw only a sharp vertical line, a blade of darkness.

Then the wind struck me—cold, sudden, piercing. It passed *through* me. I shuddered. My heart pounded like a pebble trapped in a child’s rattle, bouncing wildly against the walls of my chest.

And in that instant, I knew: I was no longer alone. Something had seen me. Something older, darker, far more real than I had ever imagined.

I stepped into a narrow alley, seeking refuge beneath the shadow of a low archway. The moonlight spilled across the cobbled stones, silvering the air like frost. I paused at the mouth, gazing out into the stillness—then she appeared, gliding silently behind me. She stopped, trembling, and turned to look out as well, her breath quick and shallow in the quiet night.

A few heartbeats passed. Then, with a sudden flash of movement, a great leopard darted across the archway. Its coat was pale, marked with dark splotches like ink on parchment, and its eyes gleamed with cold intelligence.

The woman pressed close to me, her body rigid with fear. My chest tightened with pity. Without thought, I reached out and drew her gently against me.

“If it comes here,” I said, voice steady, “I’ll hold it off. You run.”

“Thank you,” she whispered, her voice barely audible.

“Have you seen it before?” I asked.

“Many times,” she replied, still shaking. “She belongs to the princess. You’d know her if you were from Bulika.”

“I’m not,” I said. “But why is she allowed to roam free?”

“She’s kept in chains,” she answered, “her mouth muzzled, her paws wrapped in crocodile leather. But she escapes often—always at night. And when she does… she hunts children. Sucks their blood. There aren’t many mothers left in Bulika, thank the stars.”

She broke down then, tears streaming down her face. “I wish I were home,” she sobbed. “The princess returned only last night—and already the beast is loose! How will I get inside? It’s *me* she wants—I can feel it! She’ll be waiting at my door!”

She clamped her hands over her mouth, ashamed. “I shouldn’t speak to a stranger like this.”

“All strangers aren’t cruel,” I said softly. “And this creature won’t touch you while I’m here. By the time she finishes with me, you’ll be safe indoors. At least you have a home to go to. That wind is terrible.”

“You’ll be sheltered too,” she said, wiping her eyes. “If you take me safely home, I’ll give you a place to rest. But we must wait.”

We stood in silence for a moment, listening to the wind moan through the alleys. Then I began to ask questions.

She told me that the people of Bulika did nothing but dig for gems beneath their homes. They were rich beyond measure, yet they never made anything themselves—everything came from distant cities.

“Why don’t you work?” I asked.

“Because it is a disgrace,” she said flatly. “No one in Bulika works. It’s known by all.”

“But how do you stay rich?”

“Our ancestors saved for us,” she explained. “We never spend. When we need coin, we sell a few jewels. We’ve so many, buried deep beneath the city, that we’ll never run out.”

“And what about the poor?”

“There are some,” she admitted, “but we forget them. Once someone falls, they vanish from our minds. That’s how we keep wealth—by ignoring those who lose it. We intend to remain rich forever.”

“But what happens when your mines are empty? When your gems are gone?”

“We have more than enough,” she said confidently. “And new ones grow beneath the earth. The day of scarcity will never come.”

“What if outsiders attack? What if a foreign army takes everything?”

“No one dares,” she replied. “They fear our princess. She protects us. She holds power over land, sea, sky—and fire, perhaps. No force could ever defeat her. She rules without question, without equal.”

As she spoke, she glanced back again and again, her eyes wide with dread.

I asked why her people hated strangers so fiercely.

“The presence of a foreigner defiles the city,” she said. “We are older, nobler, purer than any other race. Therefore, no outsider may stay after nightfall. We cast them out.”

“But then how can you let me in?”

“I’ll make an exception,” she said. “For you.”

“Is there no place in Bulika for travelers?”

“There would be,” she said, “if such a house existed. But it would be torn down. The owner burned. Purity cannot be preserved unless the unworthy are kept far away. Dignity is fragile.”

She told me the princess had ruled for millennia—her reign unbroken, her power absolute. She commanded nature itself, shaped storms and rivers with a word. No law bound her. No court judged her.

At last, she seemed ready. We slipped through twisting lanes, avoiding the silent streets, until we reached her home—a tall building between two others, atop a steep flight of narrow steps. She climbed slowly, and I followed.

Halfway up, she froze. Her breath caught. Then she bolted forward, scrambling the rest of the way. I reached the top just as the door slammed shut behind her.

I stood alone on the landing, stunned. Between the two houses, the space was barely long enough to lie down. Exhausted, and no longer caring whether I profaned the city, I collapsed onto the stone floor, letting the wind and shadows claim me.

Beneath the stair, the moonlit street stretched cold and silent, the wind moaning through the alleys like a restless spirit. But within my sheltered nook, not a breath stirred. I was settling into rest when—without warning—my eyes flew open. There, just cresting the top step, was the gleaming head of the creature I’d seen trailing the Shadow: pale as snow, luminous in the night.

She froze the moment our gazes met. Then, tail first, she began to retreat. I leapt up—and she had no room to turn. With a desperate twist, she flipped backward, landing on her feet in an instant, and vanished down the stairs before I could reach her.

I raced after her, scanning the empty street from end to end. Nothing. No sign. Only the wind and the moon. Returning to my hard cot, I clenched my fists. Two predators now haunted Bulika—one spotted, one not. One was a beast; the other, perhaps, something worse.

I wouldn’t risk much for any adult here. But a child? A child’s life might be worth every breath I had left. I vowed to watch that door all night.

Then came the slow creak of the latch. I rose quietly, slipped inside. The woman who stood there was not the one I’d helped. It was the desert figure—the muffled one, face hidden beneath layers of cloth.

Without speaking, she led me into a stone chamber, bare and cold. She pointed to a rug. I wrapped myself in it and lay down. She shut the inner door, then opened and closed the outer one behind her. Moonlight bled through the cracks, the only light in the silence.

I couldn’t sleep. Soon, a low moan began—muffled, ragged. It swelled, then broke into a cry. A newborn’s cry. Then—a shriek, sharp and terrible.

I sprang up and burst into the passage. From another doorway emerged the white leopardess, clutching a baby in her jaws—carrying it as if it were her own cub.

I lunged at her. She dropped the infant with a whimper. It fell onto the stone, wailing.

At the sound, the muffled woman appeared. She stepped over us—over the animal, over me—picked up the child, and disappeared into the shadows.

When she returned, she lifted me off the beast, opened the door, and gently pushed me out. Behind me, the leopardess followed.

“She has failed me,” I thought bitterly. “Given me to the beast to deal with at her leisure. But we’ll see about that.”

I ran down the stairs, half expecting her to leap upon me. But she kept pace, calm, quiet. At the bottom, I turned to grab her—she leapt over my head. When I faced her again, she was crouched at my feet.

I knelt. Reached out. Stroked her flawless white fur. She licked my bare feet with a rough, dry tongue—like a dog, yet more tender.

I patted her, held her close. A surge of warmth flooded my heart. Could she betray me? Perhaps. But if I turned from every gesture of affection—fearing it false—how would I ever know true love when it came?

I stood. She rose beside me.

Then—thud. A heavy squelch echoed in the street. A shapeless mass lay in the middle of the road, pulpy, half-dissolved. I ran to it. Beneath the ruin, I recognized the form of a woman—her body thrown from a window above.

I looked around. There, on the opposite side of the street, walked the Shadow. And behind him—again—came the white leopardess.

I followed. My heart urged compassion: perhaps she wasn’t free. Perhaps she was bound.

As I drew near, she turned—snarling, fangs bared. Instinct made me step back. Instantly, she resumed her place behind the Shadow.

I approached again. Again, she lunged—eyes blazing emerald fire.

A third time. This time, she snapped—bit me.

My breath caught. I gasped.

And in that moment, she turned her head, glanced at me—not with rage, but with something like sorrow. As if to say: *Why did you make me do this?*

I turned away, ashamed. I had wasted too long. I had to act.

Night or no night—I would go to the palace.

From the square, I had seen it: towering above the city, encircled by walls and ditches, more fortress than royal seat. But now, its defenses were crumbling. Gates stood wide. The drawbridge was gone. The chasm below was dry. For centuries, it had been abandoned—forgotten.

Silence pressed in. So deep it felt alive. To enter would be to break a spell. To speak—to scream—might wake horrors beyond imagining.

I wandered the walls like a stray. Then found a recess with a stone bench. Sheltered from the wind, I lay down. Cold seeped into my bones—but exhaustion won. I slept.

I woke to something leaping onto me—warm, heavy, breathing against my face. A rough tongue lapped at my cheek.

*The white leopardess,* I thought. *Come to drink my blood.* Why not? What good would resistance do? It would cost me more to fight than to yield.

So I stayed still.

But no pain came. Instead—warmth. A gentle heat spread from her body, pressing against mine. Her breath, soft and clean, wrapped around my face. Not wild. Not cruel. Tender.

I realized—this was no predator. This was care.

I turned, like a child seeking comfort. Threw my arm over her. And sank into dreamless sleep.

When I stirred, I thought I was home. Warm. Safe. The scent of flowers, of earth, of memory. I rubbed my eyes. Opened them.

Stone. Cold stone. The heart of the cursed city.

I sat up. Had I dreamed her? Or had she truly lain beside me?

Her warmth lingered. Still present.

I rose. A new hope stirred in me—vague, unformed, but strong.

One truth was clear: I must find the princess.

I had saved her life. She had given me a second chance—though at great cost to myself.

That bond—however fragile—gave me courage. I would face her. Whatever she was.

I circled the ancient fortress, passing through the open gates and crossing the deep, moat-like ravine that gaped beneath me like a wound in the earth. Beyond lay a vast courtyard paved with stone, lined at regular intervals by towering trees resembling poplars—tall, straight, and silent. At its center stood one tree taller than the rest, its upper branches fanning outward in a way that hinted at a palm’s grace. Between the trunks, I caught glimpses of the palace: long and low, with soaring towers at each corner and a massive dome rising from the roof, half as high as the towers themselves. The architecture was unlike anything I had seen—strange, yet unmistakably rooted in an Eastern tradition, perhaps Indian in origin.

The main entrance was a low arch shaped like a flattened oval. No guards stood watch. The doors hung wide, inviting. I stepped inside unchallenged into a great hall, elongated and elliptical, dimly lit. Near one wall sat a cage, within which rested a colossal leopardess. Her body was white, marked with dark oval spots, her head cradled on her paws. A steel collar encircled her neck, her mouth muzzled, her paws wrapped in thick cloth. Yet she stared at me with eyes wide open—pupils canoe-shaped, irises a deep green—unblinking, unmoving. Her tail lay rigid behind her like a bar of iron. I could not tell if she lived or was merely a statue carved from flesh and shadow.

From this chamber, two narrow corridors branched off. I chose one, only to find it splitting into a maze of twisting, uneven passages. In a tight space where barely two could pass, a young page collided with me. He recoiled in fear, but upon seeing my face, he stiffened, puffed out his chest, and demanded, “What is your business?”

“To see the princess,” I said.

“A likely thing!” he scoffed. “I haven up with her highness this morning myself!”

I seized him by the nape and shook him. “Take me to her now—or I’ll drag you until I find her. She shall know how her servants treat her guests.”

He glanced at me, then suddenly began pulling me forward like a blind dog guiding its master. We arrived at a large kitchen, where several servants moved sluggishly, their eyes half-lidded, barely awake. I expected them to attack, but instead they froze, staring—not at me, but at something behind me. Their faces grew pale, their breaths shallow.

I turned. There stood the white leopardess, watching them with stillness that seemed to hold the weight of a thousand deaths. Then, slowly, one servant recovered. I released the boy and turned to him. “Take me to the princess,” I said.

“She has not left her room, my lord,” he replied.

“Tell her I am here, waiting for audience.”

“Your name, sir?”

“Say only that one who knows the white leech seeks her.”

He hesitated. “She will kill me if I deliver such a message. I cannot—I dare not.”

“You refuse?” I asked.

He glanced at me, then fled toward the inner chambers.

The others remained transfixed, too afraid to look away from the beast.

I approached the leopardess, who now stood near me, muzzle lowered to my heel. Her fur gleamed like milk in the dim light, a warm radiance in the gloom. I knelt and gently stroked her. She looked up—just that movement sent the servants scattering like frightened birds. She rose on her hind legs, placed her paws on my shoulders, and I embraced her. But then she pricked her ears, broke free, and vanished into the shadows.

Moments later, the servant returned. “Please follow me, my lord,” he said.

My heart thudded. This was no ordinary encounter. I followed him through winding halls until we reached a vast, pitch-black chamber. The walls were invisible. Only a single patch of floor reflected faint light. Above, at great height, an oval opening pierced the ceiling, framed by joints of black marble. The floor itself was laid with tightly fitted slabs of the same dark stone. Later I would learn that the entire elliptical wall was also made of black marble, swallowing any light that touched it. The roof curved like the upper half of an ellipsoid, and the aperture above was positioned over one focus of the ellipse below.

For a moment, I thought I saw faint red lines across the darkness—but when I blinked, they were gone.

Then, suddenly, a figure appeared in the center of the void. Light erupted around her, flaring from her presence as if she carried the sun within her. She wore a soft white robe, and her hair fell in a waterfall of jet-black strands, cascading over the obsidian floor. Her eyes were pools of luminous black; her arms and feet glowed like polished ivory. She smiled—innocent, radiant—as though she had just stepped from childhood into womanhood.

“Alas,” I thought, “how wrong I was to judge her danger! Is this the woman who struck me, scorned me, abandoned me?”

I stood in the dark, watching her. She stood in the dark, searching for me.

Then she vanished.

“She does not recognize me,” I thought.

But in the next breath, her eyes flashed into mine—direct, piercing, alive.

“You have found me at last,” she said, placing her hand on my shoulder. “I knew you would.”

My body trembled—not from cold, but from a storm of emotions I could not name. I felt drawn to her, repelled by her—both at once.

“You shiver,” she said softly. “This place is cold for you. Come.”

I did not speak. She had silenced me with beauty. She held me with sweetness.

She took my hand and led me to the spot of light. Again, she blazed forth—radiant, undeniable.

“You’ve grown brown since I last saw you,” she said.

“This is almost the first roof I’ve been under since you left me,” I replied.

“And whose was the other?” she asked.

“I do not know the woman’s name.”

“I would gladly learn it. Hospitality is not strong in my people.”

She led me through the darkness to a curtain of black silk. Beyond it, a white staircase ascended. She guided me to a chamber of breathtaking beauty.

“How you must miss the hot flowing river,” she said. “But there is a bath in the corner—no white leeches here. At the foot of your couch, you’ll find a garment. When you come down, I’ll be in the room to your left at the stair.”

She left me standing, questioning my own judgment. How could I treat this woman as evil when she greeted me like a sister? What had changed her? She had struck me, mocked me, fled—but now welcomed me with warmth. She had reviled me, yet claimed she knew I would return.

Did she know my doubts? Did she know I needed answers?

Could she give them?

Could I believe her?

And yet—her hospitality was real. I had earned it. I could accept it—for now. Until I judged her truly.

Could such beauty and such darkness exist in one soul?

If so—how?

I stood before the bath, its water clear as crystal, sparkling in the corner where it sank into the marble. It looked celestial. I had not seen water since leaving home—except the stream, the cottage draughts, the pools near the wounded leopardess. This was pure, untouched.

I stepped in.

At once, a delicate scent filled my mind—strange, sweet, yet unsettling. I paused. Had she drugged it? Enchanted it? Was she manipulating me?

I remembered the crushed paw of the leopardess—the mother fleeing, the howl echoing, the limping beast.

But what mattered where it came from? Was the water not sweet? Was it not the same as the pitcher-plant’s dew, secreted for the weary traveler?

Water came from heaven. Why care about the well?

Still, I did not return to the bath.

I dressed in the white wool robe, embroidered at the neck and hem, and descended to the room she had named.

It was round, entirely of alabaster, windowless. Light seeped through the walls and dome, soft and pearlescent, like moonlight on mist. Shadowy forms drifted across the surfaces—like clouds over a sky of gray-blue.

She waited, draped in a silver-embroidered robe, stitched with rings, discs, rectangles, lozenges—close together, like armor of stars. It fell from her neck to her feet, but the sleeves were open, revealing arms like ivory.

On a table of ivory lay cakes, fruit, a jug of milk, a crystal carafe of pale rose wine, and a white loaf.

“We do not kill to eat here,” she said. “But I think you will like what I offer.”

I told her nothing could please me more.

She seated herself on a couch beside the table and gestured for me to sit beside her. She poured milk into a bowl, handed me the loaf, and filled two silver goblets from the wine jug—goblets of exquisite, twisted design.

“You’ve never drunk wine like this,” she said.

I tasted it—and was stunned. Every flower of Hybla and Hymettus had given its spirit to this elixir.

“And now,” she continued, “since you can listen, I must try to make myself understood. Our natures are different—so different that it may be hard. Men live to die. We—those like me—live to live. Old age terrifies you; to me, it is a longing. The older we grow, the closer we come to perfection. Yours is fleeting, fragile; ours is endless ripening.”

She paused. “I am not yet ripe. I have lived thousands of your years—how many, I do not count. Eternity cannot be measured.”

“Many lovers have sought me,” she went on. “None have I loved. They wanted to possess me—like men in my city seek gems. But when you found me… I found a man.”

She leaned forward. “I tested you. You passed. Your love was true—but not ideal. Pity is not love. No woman, in any world, returns love for pity. That kind of love… it repels me.”

She looked at me with sorrowful eyes. “I knew if you saw me as I am, you’d love me—to have and to8hold. I refused that. I wanted a love that endured beyond hope, beyond indifference, beyond hatred. So I wore cruelty, pride, ingratitude. When I left you, I made sure you could no longer follow from pity. I no longer needed you. But you must prove yourself—either priceless or worthless. Follow me without expecting anything—without gratitude, without even pity. Just be present. Be patient. And if you do not, then I have failed. I yield.”

She hid her face in her hands, trembling.

But I saw a flicker behind the sorrow—a spark of calculation. I did not believe her.

She rose, arms raised high, eyes blazing like lightning.

“What you have made me is yours!” she cried. “I will repay you as no woman ever has. My power, my beauty, my love—take them all!”

She dropped to her knees, arms across my knees, looking up at me.

Then I noticed—on her left hand, a clumsy glove. In my mind’s eye, I saw claws and hair beneath it. A hand bruised, clenched tight. I looked at the other—perfect, lovely, flawless. If I did not hate her, I might love her.

I turned my gaze away.

She rose abruptly.

“To me, she may be true,” whispered my vanity.

For a moment, I was tempted to love a lie.

A subtle scent brushed my skin—more than air, less than perfume.

I looked up.

She stood before me, arms waving in a gesture both graceful and strange.

Then—a roar, terrifying, shaking the very walls. The alabaster trembled. The princess flinched.

“My wine was too strong,” she said, voice quivering. “I should not have let you drink so much. Go sleep now. When you wake, ask me anything. I will answer.”

She led me up the stairs.

“I don’t wonder the roar startled you,” she said. “It startled me. For a moment, I feared she had escaped. But that is impossible.”

Yet I knew—somehow—that the roar had come from the white leopardess. And it was meant for *me*, not her.

She smiled as she left me at my door. But as she turned, I saw it—fear, deep in her beautiful eyes.

I collapsed onto the bed, my mind racing through the tale she had told me. In her moment of vulnerability, a single careless word had unlocked a mystery—revealing how I had found her in the forest: the leopardess leapt over, the princess lay helpless on the riverbank, and the flowing water had shattered her self-imposed spell. Her own words now painted the danger that threatened the Little Ones—and I had unwittingly saved their greatest enemy. The realization struck me just as sleep claimed me. Perhaps the wine had not been entirely harmless.

When I awoke, night had fallen. A soft lamp hung from the ceiling, casting a gentle glow across the room. A deep, dreamlike calm washed over me. I felt suspended, adrift on a twilight sea far from shore. Life itself was pleasure. There was no pain—until there was. A sharp, searing sting pierced my heart. Another followed—more intense, more cruel. I couldn’t move my hand to touch the wound; something held it down. The pain faded, but my body remained numb. Something vile pressed upon me—something loathsome. I tried to fight, but my will was trapped, powerless. I gave in, lying still.

Then I felt a delicate hand on my face, gently pressing my head into the pillow. A heavy weight rested across my chest. I breathed easier as it lifted. My eyes fluttered open.

The princess stood above me, one foot on the bed, gazing into the room with a distant, dreamy expression. Her eyes were clear, her lips tinged with satisfaction. She wiped a smear of red from her mouth. Our eyes met. She leaned forward and struck me across the eyes with a handkerchief—like dragging a blade across glass. For a moment, darkness swallowed my vision.

A dull thud echoed—the sound of a large, soft-footed creature landing after a short leap. I blinked, and saw the long sweep of a tail vanishing through the half-open door. I lunged after it.

It was gone. I raced down the stairs, bursting into the alabaster hall. The moon hung high, bathing the space in pale silver light—like the inside of a sun-bleached moon. But the princess was nowhere.

I needed her presence. Only then could I defend myself. Without her, I was helpless—a captive offering, a living well for a demon’s thirst. She had shown kindness only to exploit me. My waking eyes didn’t fear her—but when they closed, she would come.

I searched blindly through the darkened corridors, hands outstretched. At last, I touched a curtain. I pushed it aside and stepped into the black hall.

There, an eerie gathering awaited. Though the walls, floor, and ceiling were lost in impenetrable darkness—deeper than any starless night—I could vaguely make out figures moving within the void. Shapes flickered in and out of existence: a woman with radiant eyes peering from a skull, a warrior mounted on a skeletal steed, grotesque burrowing phantoms rising from the shadows. No pattern emerged—only chaos. Forms merged, split, danced, dissolved. Shadows moved independently, guided by their own silent wills.

I scanned the crowd, searching for her. But she was not there—not in this shifting, surreal nightmare. Where was she? What was she doing?

No one noticed me as I wandered. Despair crept in. I turned away, tracing the wall with my fingers. Groping forward, I reached a curtained entrance leading to the vestibule.

Moonlight filtered through, illuminating the leopardess’s cage. Inside, two forms writhed in silent combat—one human, one beast—locked in a desperate embrace. Before I could react, the leopardess stepped out of the cage, calm and composed, walking toward the open door.

I followed, glancing back—only to see the leopardess still crouched motionless in the cage, exactly as I’d first seen her.

Above, the moon rose full and bright. The shadow I’d seen the night before walked through the trees toward the gate. Behind him came the leopardess, her tail swaying like a banner.

I trailed them silently, unseen. They passed through the gate and descended into the city, quiet under the moon’s gaze. The moon seemed still—expectant.

The shadow moved straight to the stair where I had lain the night before. He climbed without pause. The leopardess followed.

I quickened my pace. Then—a cry of horror. A soft, heavy object fell between me and the steps. At my feet lay a body—blackened, crushed, unmistakably the woman who had brought me home and locked me out.

Frozen, I watched as the spotted leopardess bounded down the stairs, a baby clutched in her jaws. I lunged forward, ready to snatch it from her.

But from behind me, a flash of silver cut through the moonlight. The white leopardess—like a bar of molten moonlight—shot forward and seized the other by the neck.

The baby dropped. I caught it. I stood, transfixed, as the two cats clashed.

What a battle! One moment the spotted leopardess dominated, the next the white one twisted free. Neither roared—only growled, whimpered, snarled—each movement followed by frantic scrambling of claws, teeth gnashing, bodies wrestling for purchase on the stone.

The spotted one was larger, but the white proved more enduring. She never released her grip. Slowly, the spotted leopardess began to fail. From her throat rose a howl—deepening, twisting—until it became the agonized wail of a woman at the edge of death.

The white leopardess loosened her jaws. The spotted one pulled back, rising on her hind legs.

And there, bathed in moonlight, stood the princess. Shadows surged across her form—spots fleeing, converging, dissolving into her eyes. The last few streaked into her hair, vanishing like mist before dawn. She shone, pure and radiant, save for the thin trail of blood running down her throat—each drop marking the fury of her foe’s fangs.

She turned, took a few steps with the stride of a goddess, then fell—her skin once again speckled—and fled into the night at a gallop.

The white leopardess turned too. She leapt toward me, tore my arms apart, caught the falling child, and vanished into the street, racing toward the gate with the baby cradled in her jaws.

I turned and followed the spotted leopardess, catching only a fleeting glimpse of her as she vanished over the hillside toward the palace gate. By the time I reached the entrance hall, the princess was in the midst of pulling on the robe she’d left discarded on the floor. Her wounds had stopped bleeding, the blood now crusted and dry from the wind that had chased her across the open ground. When she saw me, a flicker of anger crossed her face—then she looked away. But after a moment, she forced a smile and said, “A small mishap! I heard the cat-woman was back in the city, so I went down to drive her off. But she brought one of her monstrous creatures with her—it leapt at me before I could react, and its claws dug into my neck!”

She shivered, and though I knew she lied, I couldn’t help feeling pity. The wounds were real, and the hollow look in her eyes reminded me of how she’d appeared in the cave beneath the mountain. A pang of guilt stirred within me—why hadn’t I stayed to help her? My expression must have given it away.

“Child of folly,” she said, her voice edged with false sweetness, “you’re not crying, are you? Wait here. I’ll be just a moment. I need something for these scratches.”

But I didn’t wait. I followed close behind. Out of sight, I feared what she might do.

The instant she stepped into the black hall, a low buzzing filled the air—as if hundreds of voices murmured in unison. Then, like a beam of light sweeping across a darkened stage, illumination began to move through the chamber. One section would glow brightly, then fade into shadow, while another emerged, shimmering with sudden clarity. Groups of figures danced, fought, or stood still—scenes I recognized, scenes I had lived or witnessed. And there, in each tableau, was the princess: watching, urging, participating.

The skull-headed dancers moved across the grass in the forest-hall—there she stood at the doorway. In the Evil Wood, the battle raged—she was there, inciting the clash. Yet all the while, she remained motionless before me, head bowed, shoulders slumped.

The shifting light continued, revealing more fragments: the heath, the hollow, the mist curling around her feet as she walked in circles, trying to embrace the vapour that slipped through her grasp. Then, abruptly, the old librarian approached. He paused, stared at her. She collapsed—not physically, but in spirit—her limbs dissolving, her form vanishing into thin air. A scream tore through the chamber, echoing from every wall. At the same moment, the real princess—still standing before me—crumpled to the ground, unconscious.

I turned and fled. No longer would I try to save her.

Standing trembling beside the cage, I understood: in that black ellipsoid, I had entered her mind. I saw the tip of the leopardess’s tail twitch once—then nothing.

Still trying to steady myself, I heard the princess speak beside me. “Come,” she said. “I’ll show you what I need.”

She led me into the courtyard. I followed, numb, half-dreaming. The moon hung high, near the zenith, its silver light brighter than the sun’s absent gold. Through the trees, we came to the tallest—the central tree, unlike the others. Its branches converged at the top, forming a tight cone that seemed to point directly at the sky.

She stood beneath it, gazing upward. “On the very top,” she whispered, almost to herself, “grows a tiny blossom. It would heal my wounds instantly. If only I could become a dove for a moment—I could fly up and fetch it. But look—there’s a serpent among the leaves. Its bite would kill a dove far worse than a tiger’s fang would harm me.” She spat the words out. “How I loathe that cat-woman!”

Then she turned to me, her face softening into one of her most radiant smiles. “Can you climb?”

The smile vanished as quickly as it came. Her expression twisted into sorrow, pain etched deep. I should have walked away. But when she touched her wounded neck, I felt my resolve break.

I studied the tree. Along its trunk, stubs jutted out—remnants of fallen leaves, like the scars of a palm. “I can climb that,” I said.

“Not barefoot,” she insisted. “You’ll fall.”

I had left my sandals in my room, chasing the leopardess. “It doesn’t matter,” I replied. “I’ve walked barefoot for years.”

She knelt, tore the wide embroidered borders from her robe, and bound them tightly around my feet—one at a time. “You’ve left the ends loose,” I said.

“I have no knife to cut them,” she answered. “But they won’t tangle.”

I turned to the tree. The cold wasn’t severe in Bulika after sunset—especially compared to the chill near the sexton’s cottage—but as I climbed, the temperature dropped. Higher I went, colder I grew. Among the branches, my hands and feet turned numb. There was no wind, yet the tree felt unstable—each limb trembled under my touch, as if about to snap.

When I rose above the canopy, bathed in moonlight, I searched for the blossom. Then—suddenly—I was drenched. Water poured over me from nowhere. The next instant, I was thrown violently—spun, tossed, rolled—like a leaf in a storm. I sank, gasping, choking, pulled downward by invisible currents. I tumbled, caught, spun again, until finally I crashed onto solid ground.

“Told you so,” croaked a voice beside my ear.

I wiped the water from my eyes and saw the raven perched on the rim of a vast stone basin. Dawn’s pale light glinted off his sleek feathers as he gazed down at me with quiet composure. I lay half-submerged in the fountain’s cold embrace, propped up on my elbows just enough to keep my face above the surface. The fountain—my father’s grand creation—rose like a steel spine from the center of the lawn, hurling a torrent of water a hundred feet into the air before it burst into a crown of mist and foam.

“YOU TOLD ME NOTHING!” I snapped, bristling at the chill of his words.

“I told you to do nothing that anyone you distrusted asked of you,” he replied calmly.

“Tut! How was a mortal supposed to remember such a thing?”

“You’ll not forget the consequences of forgetting it,” said Mr. Raven, leaning forward over the edge. He extended a hand. I grasped it—and instantly found myself standing beside him on the dew-drenched grass, soaked through and shivering.

“You must change your clothes at once,” he said.

“A wetting doesn’t matter where you come from—though here, it brings its own troubles.” He turned and strode toward the house, now a raven again, moving with regal poise. The front door stood open, inviting.

“I don’t have much to change into,” I laughed, remembering I’d left my robe behind when climbing the tree.

“It’s been a long time since I shed a feather,” he remarked, without looking back.

Inside, silence reigned. No one stirred. I went to my room, pulled on a dressing gown, and made my way to the library. As I stepped in, the librarian emerged from the inner closet. I collapsed onto a couch. Mr. Raven drew a chair close and sat beside me. For a moment, neither of us spoke.

Then I broke the stillness. “What does it all mean?”

“A good question,” he said. “No one knows what anything *is*. A man can only learn what it *means*—and whether he does depends on how he uses it.”

“I’ve made no use of anything yet.”

“Not much. But you know that fact—and that’s something. Most people spend lifetimes learning they’ve learned nothing, and done less. At least you’ve never lacked the desire to be useful.”

“I wanted to help the children—the Little Ones,” I said.

“I know you did—and you began in the wrong direction.”

“I didn’t know the right way.”

“That’s true—but you’re to blame for not finding it.”

“I’m ready to believe whatever you say… once I understand it.”

“If you had accepted our invitation, you would have known the right path. When a man refuses to act where he stands, he must travel far to find his purpose.”

“I’ve traveled far—and found nothing. I left the children to learn how to serve them, and all I’ve learned is the danger they’re in.”

“When you were with them, you were where you could have helped. You abandoned your duty to search for it. It takes wisdom to know when to leave—fools learn to return at once.”

“Do you mean… I could have helped the Little Ones by staying?”

“Could you teach them anything by leaving them?”

“No. But how could I teach them? I didn’t even know where to start. Besides—they were far ahead of me.”

“True. But you weren’t a measuring stick for them. If they knew what you know—let alone what you might have known—they’d be beyond you, utterly out of sight. Yet you saw they weren’t growing—or growing so slowly they hadn’t even grasped the idea of growth. They feared it! And you had never seen children stay children.”

“But I had no power to make them grow!”

“You could have removed the obstacles to their growth.”

“What are they? I don’t know. I thought perhaps it was lack of water.”

“Of course it is. They have none to cry with.”

“I would have spared them that need.”

“Ah—just like every foolish philanthropist! But Mr. Vane, without tears, your world would never have become worth saving. You admit you suspected they needed water—why didn’t you dig them a well or two?”

“It never occurred to me.”

“Even when the sound of underground waters reached your ears?”

“I think it did once. But I feared the giants might harm them. That’s why I endured so much from the beasts myself.”

“And in doing so, you nearly taught those noble creatures to fear the Bags! While they fed you, comforted you, worshipped you—you submitted to brutish men! You gave them a coward as their hero! A greater injustice you could hardly commit. They gave you their hearts—you owed them your soul! By now, you might have made the Bags mere laborers for the Little Ones!”

“I fear you may be right, Mr. Raven. But I truly believed more knowledge might harm them—make them less innocent, less pure.”

“They gave you no reason to fear such a thing.”

“Isn’t a little knowledge dangerous?”

“One of your world’s favorite lies. Is man’s greatest knowledge more than a little? And if so, is it therefore dangerous? The belief that knowledge is inherently great makes any amount of it seem more perilous than ignorance. To know all things wouldn’t be greatness.”

“At least I served them out of love—not fear.”

“Granted. But you should have served the Little Ones, not the giants. You should have given them water—then they would soon have shown the giants their proper place. In the meantime, you could have forced the giants to cut down two-thirds of their coarse fruit trees to make space for the delicate ones. You lost your chance with the Lovers, Mr. Vane. You speculated about them instead of helping them.”

I sat in silence, shame burning through me like a cold fire. He was right—my dealings with the Little Ones had been reckless, thoughtless. I had failed them as a neighbor, and worse, as a protector.

Mr. Raven spoke again, his voice low but cutting: “You wronged them not only by your neglect, but by your cruelty. For those creatures, slavery would have been progress. A few lessons—taught gently, even with a branch from their own trees—could have made them strong, wise, capable. Instead, you left them helpless.”

“I didn’t know they were afraid,” I whispered.

“And does that excuse you?” he asked sharply. “The man who acts based on another’s fear is himself no better than a coward. And now—look what has come of it! By now, they might have stood against the princess, perhaps even the giants—though those were never much to worry about. But you? You let her mock them. And now, because of your connection with her…”

“I hate her!” I burst out.

“Did you tell her so?”

Again, I said nothing.

“You’ve been unfaithful—even to her,” he murmured. Then, suddenly, he leaned forward. “Hush. We were followed from the fountain, I think.”

“No one,” I insisted. “Only a ragged-looking cat—she vanished into the bushes.”

“A Persian,” Mr. Raven corrected. “A magnificent one, though soaked and matted, looking far worse than just disreputable.”

“What do you mean?” I gasped, dread coiling in my chest.

“There was a blue Persian around the house—she fled at the sound of water. Could she have been after the goldfish?”

“We’ll see,” he said. “I know cats. There’s something in this room that will reveal her true nature—if I’m not mistaken.”

He rose, walked to the closet, returned with a book—once torn, now whole. I stared. It was intact, untouched, as if never damaged.

“Where was the other half?” I choked.

“Sticking through into my library,” he replied simply.

I held my tongue. One more question might drown me in a sea without bottom. Time was too short.

“Listen,” he said. “I’m going to read a few lines. There’s someone here who won’t enjoy hearing them.”

He opened the vellum cover. The parchment was ancient, stained with time, one page darkened across two-thirds of its surface. He turned slowly, searching for a passage. At last, he began.

But what follows is not what he read—it is only the echo of it in my mind. The words were in a language I’d never heard, yet I understood them perfectly. I could not write them, nor fully name their meaning—only shape them, dimly, through memory:

*“If I found a man who could believe
In what he saw not, felt not, yet knew—
From him I’d take substance, form, and view,
And clothe myself in truth, made real and near.”*

He turned a leaf.

*“In me was every woman. I held power
Over every living man’s soul—
More than any woman ever dared or wore.
I could do what none could, none could ever know.
All women, I, the woman, surpassed,
Outsoared, outsank, outreigned, in hall or bower.”*

Another pause. Another turn.

*“Though unseen, unheard, untouched by hand,
Though never breath stirred his hair or skin,
I could bind his mind, his spine, his brain,
With bonds that Death could not unbind—
Nor life, however long hope was deferred.”*

Then again:

*“By his side I lay—no body, no form—
I breathed not, saw not, felt not, only thought.
Yet I made him love me, hungering
For something he could not name, nor find.
I sang a song with no sound—into his soul—
Lay heartless against his heart,
Giving nothing, yet receiving all,
As he poured his being into me,
To make me real, to give me flesh.
Thus, first, I stole into his living mind.”*

A strange cry tore through the air—feline, wretched, raw.

I jolted upright, eyes darting about. Nothing.

Mr. Raven turned pages, undisturbed.

*“Sudden I woke—knowing not the horror
That gripped me—not like serpent, but like vapor—
Moist, foul, filling soul and blood and bone.
My being lay still, trapped in sickening doubt.
I knew my past—but not my now.
I knew what I had been, but not what I was.
On my brow, I felt the touch of something gone—
I was a dead despair, alive yet dying—
A life mocking life with mockery and pain.”*

Another cry—uglier, more agonized.

He paused. Then continued:

*“I knew I was a queen. Once, I wore splendour
That shone even in darkness, blind and deep.
On neck and arms, jewels blazed,
And men said my closed eyes shed light
That killed the diamond in its silver cage.”*

The cry came again—this time louder, deeper.

“I thought some vile thing was in the room,” the librarian muttered, glancing around. Then, without hesitation, he turned several pages and read:

*“Hideously wet, my golden hair
Fouled my hands—I’d have given rubies, all newly dug,
To have it cut. No eyes had seen such beauty—
No waist had worn such grace.
For a sip of water from a horn,
I’d have given my sapphires—blue as dawn.
Nay, I’d have traded opals for a peasant’s smock—
Clean, coarse, simple—anything to hide
My rotting shroud. Once, I heard a cock
Crow proudly on a green hillside—
Over my coffin. From afar, a ghostly answer
Came back—mocking, hollow, like a dream.”*

Once more, the beast howled.

“I thought something foul was here,” Mr. Raven said, scanning the room. Then he turned a few more pages and read:

*“I had bathed in milk and honey-dew,
In rain from roses that ne’er touched earth,
Ointed with amber nard.
Never a flaw, never a mark—
Not a hair too many, not a spot,
Not a scar, not a sign of want.
I feared the sun—its bronzing light—
So I sat in shadow, in reflected glow,
Bathing in moonlight, slow and soft,
Tinting my skin to ivory tone.”*

Then, abruptly:

*“But now—darkness everywhere.
No phantom-flash from my eyes.
My fingers sank into pulp,
My body drowned in slime—
A mash of rotten horrors…”*

At that moment—a shriek. A monstrous, clammy fur bristling, tail thick as rope, eyes blazing green like chrysoprase, claws tangled in the carpet—out burst a great white cat, racing toward the fireplace.

Mr. Raven moved faster than thought. He threw the manuscript between her and the hearth.

She froze. Crouched. Eyes locked on the book.

But he kept reading, voice steady, gaze fixed on the pages:

*“Ah, the two worlds—so close, yet so apart!
Had I lived bodiless, untouched by sense,
Kept my heart clean from defilement—
Then I might have escaped the canker,
Scaped life-in-death, scaped sorrow’s endless moan!”*

And then—the howl. A prolonged, guttural scream of agony so terrible we both clapped our hands over our ears.

When silence returned, Mr. Raven walked to the hearth, picked up the book, and stood before the cat. With a pointed finger, he held her gaze.

She did not move.

He took a half-burnt stick from the ashes, drew a symbol on the floor, placed the manuscript back in its case—then looked at her with quiet certainty.

Now we have her, he seemed to say.

Then, stepping over her, he spoke in a voice calm, solemn, ancient:

“Lilith. When you came here, seeking your evil will, you did not know into whose hands you were delivering yourself. Mr. Vane—when God created me—not from nothing, as fools claim, but from His own eternal glory—He gave me an angelic splendour to be my wife. She lies there—her first thought was POWER. She called union with me slavery, refused to bear children for Him who gave her life. She bore one child—and then, puffed with pride, demanded I worship her. When I refused, she spilled her own blood to escape me, fled to the enemy’s ranks, ensnared the great Shadow, made him her slave, and became queen of Hell.

What she is now—she knows best. But I know too. That one child she fears, hates, seeks to destroy—claiming rights over what God sent through her into His new world. She knows no creation—only consumption. Like crystal shaped by force, or worm split into two—she knows nothing of making. The vilest of God’s creatures, she lives on blood, on souls, on life—yet cannot create. She consumes, kills—but cannot destroy what is truly born.”

The cat lay still, eyes blazing beryl-green, fixed on him. His eyes held hers—unblinking, unyielding.

“Then God gave me another wife—not an angel, but a woman. To her, light is as darkness.”

The cat screamed—horrible, tearing—then began to grow. Larger. Bigger. Spots flickered across her fur. Her form twisted. A leopardess roared—shaking the walls.

I leapt up.

Mr. Raven did not flinch.

“It is only jealousy,” he said, calm as stone. “Jealousy self-born, frustrated, fruitless. For I stand here—your master, whom you refused as husband—while my beautiful Eve lives, hopes immortally. Your hated daughter lives too—beyond your sight, one day to become what you call her destruction. Even Lilith shall be saved through childbearing. You exult that my human wife brought us despair, bore me a race of suffering—yet my Eve repented, and now shines brighter than any woman or angel. My world groans, yes—but it is the nursery of our Father’s children. I have repented. I am blessed. But you—Lilith—have not. Yet you must.”

He paused.

“Tell me—do you find the great Shadow beautiful? Do you know how long you yourself will remain beautiful? Answer me—if you can.”

Then I understood.

Mr. Raven was Adam—the old man, the new man. His wife, tending the dead, was Eve—the mother of all, the lady of the New Jerusalem.

The leopardess reared. Her spots danced. Then—she stood revealed. Radiant. Perfect.

“I AM beautiful—and immortal,” she declared. And she looked like a goddess.

“As a bush that burns and is consumed,” Adam answered.

“What is under your right hand?”

Her arm rested across her chest, hand pressed to her side.

A spasm crossed her face—swift, sharp, gone.
