“Only a leopard-spot that lingers,” she said. “It will follow the others soon.”

“You are beautiful because God made you. But you are the slave of sin. Take your hand from your side.”

She lowered it. As it fell, she met his gaze—fierce, trembling, refusing surrender.

He looked at the spot.

“It is not on the leopard,” he said. “It is in the woman. And it will not leave until it eats to your heart—and your beauty flows out through the wound.”

She glanced down. Shivered.

“Lilith,” Adam said, his voice softer now, pleading, “hear me. Repent. And He who made you will cleanse you.”

Her hand trembled back to her side. Her face darkened.

She cried—the cry of hope vanishing.

It turned into a howl.

She writhed on the floor—again a spotted leopardess.

“The evil you plan,” Adam said, “you shall never achieve. Good is the universe, not Evil. The battle may rage for ages—but it must end. How will you fare when Time itself dissolves in the dawn of eternity? Repent. I beseech you. Repent, and be once more an angel of God.”

She rose. Stood tall. A woman again.

“I will not repent,” she said. “I will drink the blood of your child.”

I watched her—but when Adam spoke, I turned to him.

He stood towering above her. His face changed. His voice became thunder.

“Down!” he commanded. “Or by the power given me, I will melt your bones.”

She collapsed. Shrunk. Diminished—until she was again a gray cat.

Adam seized her by the scruff, carried her to the closet, and hurled her inside.

He drew a strange sigil on the threshold, shut the door, locked it.

Then he returned—old librarian again—eyes red-rimmed, shoulders slumped, wiping away silent tears.

And I, standing there, knew nothing more. Only that the world had shifted beneath my feet.

“We must be vigilant,” he said, “or she will outwit us once more. She could deceive even the chosen.”

“How can we guard ourselves?” I asked.

“Every way,” he replied. “She fears her child—therefore hates her—and is here on a mission to destroy her. To her, childbirth is not a beginning but an ending: each new generation is a foe to the one before. Her daughter represents, in her eyes, a channel through which her own imagined immortality bleeds away. And so, from the moment of her birth, she has pursued her with relentless hatred. Yet her schemes have only led to the opposite: within the realm she claims as her own, a thriving colony of children has emerged—one that looks to her daughter as its heart, its leader, its protector. My Eve longed for this child, would have nurtured her as a mother does her firstborn—but we were not yet ready to guide her. She was taken into the wilds, and for countless ages we knew nothing of her fate. But she was tended by divine hands, played with by young angels, and lived without care—until she found a baby abandoned in the woods, and the maternal instinct stirred within her. Since then, she has gathered others, one by one. That love has never been satisfied. Her family is her entire world, and no children have ever known a better guardian. Her authority over them is absolute, though she herself is unaware of it—except in quiet watchfulness, in silent service. She no longer remembers life before them; she believes she came from the forest, the first of the line.”

“You saved the life of her enemy—and thus, your life now belongs to her and hers,” he continued. “The princess was coming to annihilate the Little Ones. But as she crossed the stream, vengeance caught up with her. She would have died had you not intervened. You did. And if she had dared return across the water, she would now be raging among them. But there was another path—a way into their sanctuary through the three-dimensional world. Yet she had barred herself from it by destroying her former body. She could only re-enter through direct contact with someone bound to that world. You gave her that chance—her first in all her long years. With every step you climbed, her hand, barely touching, brushed against your muffled feet. Now, in that chamber, she waits, watching, ready to escape the moment she can.”

“She cannot know about the door!” I protested. “She can’t possibly know how to open it!”

But my voice lacked conviction.

“Hush,” whispered the librarian, raising a hand. “She hears through anything—through walls, through silence, through thought itself. You must go at once. Make your way to my wife’s cottage. I will stay and guard her.”

“Let me go to the Little Ones!” I cried.

“Beware,” he said gently but firmly. “Go to my wife. Do as she says.”

His words made no sense to me. Why rush toward what might only delay? If not to protect the children, why go at all? Still, I believed him—just enough to ask questions, not enough to obey.

“Tell me first,” I said, “why you chose *that* place to imprison her? The night I fled from your house, I ran straight into that very closet.”

“The closet is no closer or farther from our cottage than any other place,” he replied.

“But,” I insisted, struggling to grasp the logic, “how is it that when you please, you draw a whole book from that same door—when I saw only half of one? The other half, you told me, extends into your library. When you return it to the shelf, won’t it again pass through? Doesn’t that mean the two places where parts of the same book exist simultaneously must lie close together? Or can one part of the book exist in space—somewhere—and the other outside it—nowhere?”

“I regret,” he said, “I cannot explain it to you. There is no capacity in you to comprehend such things. Not only is the phenomenon beyond your understanding—it is fundamentally inaccessible. Even I grasp only fragments. Yet you constantly experience realities you neither understand nor can understand. You think you do, but your so-called understanding is merely familiarity. You accept them not because you grasp them, but because they are unavoidable. They are there, and they affect you. Truth is: no man truly understands anything. When he admits his ignorance, that is not the start of understanding—but the beginning of the ability to one day reach it. These matters are unfamiliar to you, so you do not pretend to understand. Neither I nor anyone else can help you understand here. But perhaps I can help you believe.”

He walked to the closet door, gave a low whistle, and stood listening. A moment later, I heard—or seemed to hear—a soft fluttering of wings. Looking up, I saw a white dove alight briefly on the top shelf above the portrait, then drop gracefully onto Mr. Raven’s shoulder, resting her head against his cheek. Only the subtle movement of their heads betrayed that they were speaking. I stared, transfixed—then, suddenly, the bird was gone. Mr. Raven returned to his seat.

“Why did you whistle?” I asked. “Sound here isn’t sound there, surely.”

“You’re right,” he said. “I whistled so you’d know I called her. Not the sound, but the meaning reached her. There is no time to lose—you must go.”

“I will,” I replied, rising.

“You will sleep tonight at my hostelry,” he said—not a question, but a command, gentle yet firm.

“My heart is with the children,” I answered. “But if you insist—”

“I do.”

“I can do nothing otherwise,” he added. “I’ll accompany you to the mirror and see you off.”

He rose. Then came a sharp jolt from within the closet—the leopardess had thrown herself against the heavy door. I looked at him.

“Come,” he urged. “Now.”

We reached the library door just as a shrieking yell echoed behind us, mixed with the scraping of claws on oak. I hesitated, turning back.

“To think of her lying there alone,” I murmured, “with that terrible wound.”

“That wound will never heal,” he sighed. “It will gnaw at her heart. Annihilation is no death to evil. Only good replacing evil can kill it. An evil thing must live with its nature until it chooses to become good. That is the only true slaying.”

I remained silent until a strange sound reached us.

“If she breaks free!” I gasped.

“Hurry,” he said. “I’ll follow immediately after you leave—and I’ll rearrange the mirrors.”

We ran, breathless, into the wooden chamber. Mr. Raven fastened the chains, adjusted the hood, then set the mirrors in their proper alignment. He stepped beside me before the standing mirror. Already, the mountain range emerged from the mist.

Then, with a demonic roar, the massive form of the spotted leopardess burst between us—leaping through the mirror as if it were air, settling into a swift, steady gallop.

I turned to my companion, horrified. “You need not run,” he called. “You can’t catch her. This is our way.”

As he spoke, he turned in the opposite direction.

“She has more magic at her fingertips than I care to know,” he added quietly.

“We must do what we can,” I said, and ran—but as I watched her shrink in the distance, I stopped, returning to him.

“Doubtless we must,” he replied. “But my wife has warned Mara. She will act. You must sleep first. You’ve given me your word.”

“I intend to keep it,” I said. “But surely sleep isn’t the first priority? Action comes before rest!”

“A man can do nothing he is not fit to do,” he said calmly. “See? Did I not tell you Mara would act?”

I followed his gaze and saw a white speck moving at an angle to the leopardess’s path.

“There she is!” he exclaimed. “The spotted one is strong—but the white is stronger.”

“I’ve seen them fight,” I said. “It didn’t seem decisive.”

“How could eyes that have never slept judge such a contest?” he replied. “The princess did not admit defeat—she never does—but she fled. When she finally confesses her last hope is gone—that it is impossible to resist—then her dawn will begin. Come. Come. He who cannot act must make haste to sleep.”

I stood motionless as the final trace of the white leopardess dissolved into the night, a ghostly shimmer fading beyond the edge of sight. Then I turned—reluctantly—to follow my guide. Sleep? What did I have to do with sleep? Reason was constant across all worlds, and reason told me that no living man should lie down among the dead when dawn called him to action. Who would wake me? And how could I trust myself to rise at all? In that house, the sleepers drifted through morning into noon, noon into dusk, untouched by time. I murmured my doubts aloud, but still I walked. There was no other path.

The librarian moved ahead in silence, and I followed, equally quiet. Time stretched thin between us. The sun dipped below the hills, and darkness began to seep into the world like ink poured into water. A chill crept into the air—a cold born of tombs and stillness—and my spirits sank with it. The figure before me grew thinner, more indistinct, until I could no longer see his long coat or hear the whisper of his stride through the heather.

Then came the sound: slow, deliberate flaps of wings—the raven’s voice in the wind. Now and then, fireflies flickered into being, and glowing moths rose like sparks from an unseen flame. At last, the moon emerged, creeping slowly over the distant horizon.

“You are weary, Mr. Vane?” the raven said, landing on a stone. “You must meet the horse who will carry you tomorrow.”

He gave a sharp whistle through his beak, long and hollow. A dark spot appeared on the face of the half-risen moon. Moments later, the thunder of hooves rolled through the silence—soft yet swift, like distant drums beneath the earth. From the very disc of the moon, the horse burst forth.

His mane streamed behind him like a wave torn apart by the sea, frothing silver-white in the lunar glow. His tail whipped the air, blinding the moon’s eye with each sweep. He stood nineteen hands high—massive of bone, taut of hide, rigid with muscle. He looked not like a beast of flesh, but a creature forged in death’s own forge. The holy Death himself might ride such a mount.

In the pale light, he seemed a skeleton stitched together with shadow. Yet he moved with the grace of a winged thing, silent and swift. As he neared, his pace slowed. His mane and tail settled around him like smoke calming after a storm.

I had always loved horses—not just one, but every horse I’d ever seen. I had spent every coin upon them, never sold one. This creature, though terrifying, stirred in me a hunger so deep it burned. Not desire for possession—but something darker, sharper: covetousness. It was evil, I knew, in any world. I wouldn’t steal him. But if I could buy him, I would, regardless of law or fate.

I reached out and touched him. My fingers brushed the ridges of bone beneath skin as smooth and tight as satin, reflecting the moon’s shape like a mirror. I stroked his pointed ears, whispered secrets into them, breathed into his red nostrils—life into life. In return, he breathed into mine, and we were bound.

His eyes—blue-filmed, like those of the dead—held within them glowing coals. The raven watched, wings half-spread, pleased.

“That is well,” he croaked. “Be friends with him. He will carry you better tomorrow. Now—we must go home.”

My longing to ride him flared into fever. “May I not mount him now?” I asked.

“By all means,” the raven answered. “Mount and ride.”

The horse lowered his head, gentle as a lover. I gripped his mane, pulled myself up, aided by the jutting bones beneath. “He could outrun any leopard in creation!” I exclaimed.

“Not tonight,” the raven replied. “The road is treacherous. But wait now—gain comes later. To run is easier than to stay. Go on, my son. Straight to the cottage. I shall arrive before you. Your mother will rejoice to see her son on such a steed.”

I sat silent. The horse stood still, immovable.

“Why delay?” the raven asked.

“I cannot resist the urge to chase the leopardess,” I confessed. “I can barely hold myself back.”

“You have promised.”

“The debt to the Little Ones feels heavier than my word to you.”

“Then yield to temptation, and you bring harm to them—and to yourself.”

“What does it matter to me? I love them. Love harms no one. I will return.”

But truthfully, I had forgotten the children. I was drunk on the horse, on power.

Eyes gleamed in the dark. Adam stood beside me, in his true form. I heard the strain in his voice—the anger held back by will.

“Mr. Vane,” he said, “do you not know why you’ve done nothing worth doing?”

“Because I’ve been a fool,” I answered.

“In what?”

“In everything.”

“And which is your greatest folly?”

“Bringing the princess back to life. I should have let her die.”

“Nonsense,” Adam said. “You could not have known she was evil. And you never brought anyone back to life—how could you? You are dead.”

“Dead?” I cried.

“Yes. And you will remain so until you choose to die.”

“Back to your riddles again,” I scoffed.

“Listen,” he said softly. “The only foolish thing you ever did was to flee from our dead.”

I pressed the horse’s ribs. He surged forward like a gust of wind. I patted his neck, and he spun in a tight arc—low to the ground, like a cat circling prey, his mane brushing the heather.

Through the dark, I heard the raven’s wings—five quick beats—and he landed on the horse’s head.

The horse stopped instantly, digging his hooves into the earth.

“Mr. Vane,” the raven croaked, “think! Twice already evil has struck you—once from fear, once from carelessness. Breaking your word is worse. It is a crime.”

“The Little Ones are in danger because of me!” I shouted. “But I will not break my promise. I’ll return. I’ll stay in your house as many nights, days, years as you wish.”

“I tell you again—you will do them harm if you go now.”

But the horse’s strength had infected me. A false power pulsed through me, unearned, unreal. I could not hear.

“Would you take away my last chance to make amends?” I roared. “This time there will be no running. It is my duty. I will go—even if I die for it!”

“Go then, foolish boy,” the raven croaked, laced with fury. “Take the horse. Ride to ruin. May it lead you to humility.”

He spread his wings and vanished into the sky.

I pressed the horse’s ribs again. “After the leopardess,” I whispered.

He turned his head, sniffed the air, then stepped forward—slow at first, then faster. He broke into a trot, then a gallop. In moments, he flew.

He saw in the dark. Never stumbled. Never faltered. I sat as though atop a wave, feeling every muscle ripple beneath me. His joints were springs; his movements flowed without jar. He ran not with feet, but with wind. The air rushed past like a storm.

We crossed the cursed hollow like a bolt from a crossbow. No beast dared stir. They knew the hooves that thundered above.

We climbed the hills, plunged down their slopes. Over rocky chasms, he leapt without hesitation, his gallop unbroken.

The moon, halfway across the sky, watched with solemn sorrow.

I rode in triumph—proud, powerful, free. We neared the river’s many channels. The horse cleared them one by one—sometimes two in a single stride—then gathered himself for a great leap.

As the moon reached the peak of her arc, something terrible happened.

She began to roll—like the wheel of Fortune cast by gods. Faster and faster she tumbled. Her face—human, wide-eyed—twisted upside-down, then righted, then flipped again. One moment her forehead faced the sky, the next her chin. I stared, paralyzed.

From the ravines came the howl of wolves.

A cold dread crept into my chest. My confidence cracked.

The horse kept racing—ears forward, nostrils flaring, drinking the wind.

But the moon… the moon rolled like a broken chariot-wheel down the slope of heaven. With every turn, the world dimmed.

Then—she vanished over the edge of the world. Light died.

We were mid-leap when darkness swallowed us whole.

The horse’s head dropped. Momentum carried him across the channel, but he collapsed on the bank—limp, still, lifeless.

I dismounted, knelt beside him, searched his body. No broken bones. But he was gone. Not dead. Unmade.

I sat upon his body, burying my face in my hands.

The night turned sharp with cold. Beneath me, the body stiffened into ice. The wolves drew near—their low growls echoing through the silence, their padded feet whispering over stone, their breath hot and ragged in the air. I saw them: a ring of glowing eyes, narrowing, closing in. My end had come.

I rose—only to realize I had no weapon, not even a stick. They surged forward, jaws wide, eyes blazing with hunger. I stood still, helpless, waiting for the end. For a heartbeat, they paused over the horse—then came at me.

Then, like a sudden storm from the sky, a green-eyed shadow fell upon them. A host of cats, led by a great gray one whose presence I felt before I saw him—only his eyes, fierce and luminous, burned through the dark. I knew him instantly. And I knew his size, his strength, his fury.

A battle erupted—silent but violent, its chaos carried only by soundless movements and flashes of light. I wanted to flee, but where? Every step would be a stumble. And both predator and feline could scent me, see me, in the blackness.

Then the howling ceased. The cats’ cries grew louder, wilder. Soon, the soft pad of retreating paws told me the wolves were gone. The cats had won.

But now they turned on me.

Teeth sank into my legs. In seconds, I was swarmed—dozens of bodies leaping, biting, clawing. They clung to me like living vines, tearing at my flesh, scratching with hooked claws that left deep scores. I fought back with desperate rage—ripping, crushing, kicking—but they held fast. When I tried to throw them off, they clung to my hands like barnacles. I stomped, clawed their eyes, locked my fingers around their necks—but not one would let go.

They found new places to bite, new angles to scratch. They spat and hissed in my face—until, driven mad by pain and terror, I collapsed onto the ground. Then they abandoned my body and attacked my face.

I rose—and they fled from it, turning instead to my legs once more.

In agony, I broke free and ran—no direction, no thought, just movement through the thick dark. They followed in a living tide, brushing against me, leaping up, but no longer mauling. When I fell—often—they waited for me to rise. When I slowed, they renewed their assault.

All night, they drove me forward—yet guided me carefully. No gullies, no traps. I passed the Evil Wood unseen, leaving it behind in the shadows.

At last, dawn broke. I emerged beyond the river channels, standing on the edge of the orchard valley. Joy surged through me. I called out to my tormentors—only to find none remained.

I collapsed onto the moss and slept.

I awoke to a kick—then another. Bound hand and foot, I was once again the prisoner of the giants.

“What better fate?” I whispered, laughing bitterly. “Who else should I belong to?”

Another kick silenced my sarcasm. With repeated prods, they helped me to my feet. Six of them surrounded me. They untied my legs, lashed ropes to each ankle, and dragged me away.

I walked as best I could, but when they pulled both ropes at once, I fell. Each time, they kicked me back up.

They led me straight to my old task. Tied to a tree, my arms freed, they placed the hated flint in my left hand. Then they lay down and began pelting me with fruit and stones—mostly missing.

If I’d been free, if I’d had that stick lying nearby—I would have torn them apart. But I couldn’t move. Not yet.

“Still,” I thought, “the Little Ones will come at night.”

That gave me strength.

All day, I labored under the sun. At dusk, they bound my hands and left me tied to the tree.

I slept deeply, but woke often—each time from dreams of being buried beneath a mountain of children.

Morning came. The giants returned, with their kicks and their brutish presence.

Near noon, weak from hunger and exhaustion, I heard rustling in the brush. Then—laughter.

Not any laughter. That bell-like, clear, joyous sound that stirred my soul.

I cried out in delight.

Instantly, the forest exploded. Trumpeting like baby elephants, neighing like foals, bellowing like calves—through the trees came the Little Ones.

On tiny horses, small elephants, miniature bears. But the sounds came from the riders, not the beasts.

Among them walked larger boys and girls—a woman cradling a baby who crowed with laughter.

The giants sprang up—only to be met with a hail of sharp stones. Horses charged their legs. Bears hugged them mid-thigh. Elephants wrapped trunks around their necks, pulled them down, and trampled them—just as they’d done to others, but never before had they received such treatment.

In moments, my ropes were cut. I was lifted into a sea of arms, embraced by countless little ones.

For hours, I saw no giants.

They made me sit. Lona came, wordless, and fed me—sweet red and yellow fruits, the most delicious I’d ever tasted. I ate while the entire colony guarded me.

Then two of the largest elephants were brought forward. Side by side, their trunks were hooked together, their tails tied. The animals could have undone it easily—but they didn’t. They understood. They stood still, parallel, obedient.

I climbed between them, settling into the hollow formed by their backs. They leaned inward, balancing the weight, creating a gentle, rocking litter.

My feet dangled past their tails, but my head rested on an ear of each. Smaller children mounted the elephants’ backs, forming a guard along the ridge. Then, in procession, we moved forward.

I did not ask where we were going. I surrendered myself to their care—almost as happy as they were.

At first, they chattered, laughed, played tricks. But when I closed my eyes, they grew quiet—so silent it was almost loud.

I woke to music.

A sudden burst of birdsong filled the forest. We were passing through the woods where the babies were found—the same stretch that ran from the valley to the warm stream.

A tiny girl sat beside me, her bare feet near my face. She stared at me, then spoke—others listened closely.

“We make a petisson for king,” she said.

“What is it, my darling?” I asked.

“Shut eyes one minute,” she replied.

“Certainly! Here goes!” I said, closing my eyes tightly.

“No, no! Not fore I tell oo!” she cried.

I opened them. We talked, laughed—for another hour.

Then, suddenly: “Close eyes!”

I obeyed. The elephants stopped. I heard soft scurrying, a rustle, then silence—deep, complete silence. In that world, silence is sometimes louder than sound.

“Open eyes!” twenty voices shouted at once.

I opened them.

Only the elephants remained. No children.

I knew they were fast—too fast for giants to catch. But when I raised myself, scanning the open, bushless forest, I saw nothing—not a trace.

I stared in stunned wonder.

The sun had set. Darkness crept in. Yet soon, birds began to sing—more and more, until the air trembled with song.

“I know,” I murmured, “it must be them. But how can children make birds sing?”

Then, glancing up into the tree above, I caught motion among the leaves.

White spots flickered in the dark. The music faded. A wave of childish laughter rolled through the air. Then—white spots everywhere. The trees were full of children!

They tumbled down—some dropping from branch to branch so fast I thought they’d fall. But they landed safely, laughing wildly.

I stepped from my litter. Instantly, I was surrounded—target of every prank, every joke, every joyful attack.

With stately calm, the elephants moved toward their sleeping place.

When the noise finally subsided, I asked, “Why did I never hear you sing like this before? Even when I thought it was you, I couldn’t believe it.”

One of the wildest answered: “Ah, but we weren’t birds then. We were run-creatures, not fly-creatures. We hid in bushes. But when we came to no-bushes—only trees—we had to build nests. When we built nests, we became birds. And when we were birds, we had to do bird-things. We asked the birds to teach us their noises. They taught us. Now we are real birds!”

He pointed upward. “Come see my nest. It’s not big enough for king—but big enough for king to see me in it!”

I smiled. “I cannot climb a tree without the sun to show me the way. When it returns, I’ll try.”

“Kings seldom have wings!” I added.

“King! king!” he shouted. “Oo knows none of us hasn’t no wings—foolis feddery tings! Arms and legs is better.”

“That’s true,” I said. “I can climb without wings—and carry straws in my mouth to build my own nest.”

“Oo knows!” he replied, already sucking his thumb.

Moments later, I heard his voice, high up in a giant walnut tree: “Up adain, king! Dood night! I seepy!”

And then—silence.

Until morning.

I lay beneath a great tree, watching the children drift off one by one—some alone, others in pairs or small clusters—as they climbed into their nests high among the branches. They were always so weary at night and so refreshed come morning that sleep and waking held equal joy for them. I, too, was tired, yet I could not close my eyes. Lona had not said goodnight, and I knew she would come. The moment I’d seen her again, I’d been struck by how much she resembled the princess—so unmistakably the daughter Adam had spoken of. But where Lilith’s beauty was dazzling, sharp, and regal, Lona’s was gentle, softened by innocence, deepened by the quiet strength of motherhood.

“She must be busy,” I murmured to myself, “with the child of the woman I met fleeing.” That woman, Lona had already told me, was not half a mother—too afraid, too lost. Still, I waited.

She came at last, sitting beside me without a word. For a while, there was only silence—the kind that speaks louder than words. She stroked my face, my hands, as if tracing something sacred. Then, softly, she began to tell me everything that had happened since I left.

The moon rose as we spoke, filtering through the leaves in fleeting glimmers, catching her face—thoughtful, burdened, yet radiant with a love that made even sorrow seem holy. How such a creature could have sprung from such a mother—how such a woman could have come from such a princess—was beyond understanding. Yet then, blessedly, she had two parents. Or perhaps three?

She drew me not with grandeur, but with what in me mirrored her own nature. And I loved her—not as a woman, but as one who, no matter how far she grew, would always remain a child at heart. I now knew, when I left her, that the hope of seeing her again had been my only solace. Every word she spoke pierced my soul like truth itself—purifying, illuminating.

She told me that after I departed from the orchard valley, the giants began to believe, just a little more, that their tiny neighbors truly existed. And with belief came hostility. The Little Ones sometimes saw them stomping through the forest, furious over imagined signs of our presence—while we stood nearby, laughing at their blind rage.

But soon, their anger turned practical. They started destroying the trees that fed us—our fruit, our life. This drove the mother of all the Little Ones to act. She sent the sharpest of them to listen at night, and they learned: the giants believed I was hiding near, waiting to strike once I recovered. They thought I’d return under cover of darkness to kill them while they slept.

So the plan formed: make the giants believe we had fled. We would move into the deeper forest—beyond their reach, but still close enough to visit our old trees at night. The only problem? The forest offered little shelter, no thick underbrush to hide in.

Yet she reasoned: where birds lived, so could we. The Little Ones had long shared kinship with all creatures, learning from the wild. Why not live in the treetops? Why not build homes where hollows couldn’t be dug? If birds could nest in the highest branches, why not we?

She spoke to them, and they listened—eagerly. They already climbed trees; they had watched birds weave their nests. The forest trees, though tall, did not look alien. They reached higher than the giants’ trees, arms outstretched, some even bending downward, as if beckoning. Perhaps, in the tallest, they might find the bird who laid baby-eggs and sat on them until they hatched—then dropped them down, letting the little ones burst forth.

Yes, they would build sleep-houses in the trees. No giant would ever look up. And once they believed we were gone, they would stop destroying our trees—and forget us entirely.

That is exactly what happened. Soon, the Little Ones were as comfortable in the canopy as the birds themselves. The giants, convinced we had vanished, returned to their old indifference. They forgot their trees. They stopped believing in us.

Lona asked if I had noticed how many of the children had grown. I said I hadn’t—but I could believe it. She insisted it was true, and added that the real proof was in their minds. Since moving upward, their thoughts had matured—something that eased the fear she first felt upon realizing their physical changes.

At twilight, and later under the moon, they would descend to the valley and gather fruit for the next day. The giants never ventured out then—twilight was darkness to them, and they loathed the moon. If they could, they’d have extinguished it.

But soon, the Little Ones discovered that fruit gathered at night didn’t keep well. So a new idea took root: instead of pretending to leave, why not force the giants to leave?

They had already explored the forest, making friends—or at least acquaintances—with its animals. Knowing some were strong, wise, and docile, others swift and manageable, they set about winning their aid. With playful affection, they called horses and elephants "Brother Horse," "Sister Elephant." Before long, each animal had a name.

It took time to say "Brother Bear"—but that came. And recently, she had heard a child cry, “Ah, Sister Serpent!” as a snake bit him for playing too roughly.

Most avoided caterpillars—except to watch their transformation. But when the chrysalis opened and wings emerged, every child rushed forward to call it "Sister Butterfly," celebrating its change with a word meaning something like *repentance*—as if rebirth were sacred.

One moonlit evening, while gathering fruit, they found a woman seated on the ground, cradling a baby. It was the same woman I had met fleeing toward Bulika. At first, they mistook her for a giantess who had stolen one of their infants—since all babies were theirs, in their eyes.

Furious, they swarmed upon her, striking wildly but carefully, never harming the child. She tried to flee, but a boy threw himself down and held her feet.

Regaining her senses, she recognized them—the children who had offered her shelter—and surrendered the baby.

Lona appeared, taking the infant into her arms. While the woman noted how gently the children struck, avoiding the baby, the Little Ones observed how fiercely the woman clung to her child—just as they would have done. They concluded: this must be a good giantess, like the gentle giant.

Once Lona held the baby, the children brought the woman fruit, showering her with childish affection.

The woman, torn between fear and hope, had nowhere else to go. Returning to the city meant certain exposure—princess Lilith would discover who had crippled her leopardess. But here, among these kind creatures, she found peace. She decided to stay—no need to care for the child, and perhaps a way to return to her husband, rich in jewels and rarely cruel.

Now I must add, from memory and guesswork, what Lona told me of her.

Like all of Bulika, she knew the legend: the princess lived in terror of a child destined to destroy her. But none understood the horror of the means she used to preserve her youth—nor how her failing body forced her to use them more often. So they saw her growing hatred of children not as cruelty, but as a sign of approaching doom. Though no one dared act, hope stirred.

And so, an idea formed in the woman’s mind: either fulfill the prophecy, or use it to regain her place.

What better chance than these children? Marvelously brave, unlike the cowardly Bulikans, who trembled before animals. If she could inspire them to attack the city, she might escape during the chaos, slip unseen into her home, and wait for the outcome.

If the children succeeded in driving out the giants, she would then suggest a greater goal—seizing the city itself.

By nature, they were unfit for war. They rarely quarreled, never fought. They loved all living things, hated pain and suffering. Yet they were easily led. They could be taught anything within their strength.

So she began—teaching the smallest to throw stones at a mark. Soon, all were captivated, growing skillful.

The first result? My rescue.

While gathering fruit, they found me asleep. They ran home, held council, and returned the next day with elephants and horses. They overwhelmed the few giants guarding me and carried me away.

Jubilant, the smaller boys boasted loudly; the older ones were quieter. The girls, though their eyes flashed brighter, were less talkative than usual.

The woman must have felt encouraged.

We talked most of the night—about the children’s growth, what it meant. I had long known Lona’s gift for seeing truth. Now I marveled at her wisdom. Had I been more like a child myself, perhaps I wouldn’t have been so amazed.

Dawn was still far off when I heard faint rustling, scrambling. I propped myself up and saw dozens of Little Ones descending from their nests. They vanished into the dark, and silence fell again.

“What are they doing?” I asked.

“They think,” Lona replied, “that even the stupid giants might search the woods. So they’re gathering stones—to defend themselves.”

Stones were scarce in the forest. They had to scatter far to find enough. They’d carry them back to their nests, ready to hurl from the trees when the giants came.

They expected them at dawn. If they came earlier, it would mean war—expulsion. One side would have to leave. But the outcome was clear.

“We don’t mean to kill them,” Lona said. “Their skulls are too thick—we couldn’t hurt them. And even if we did, it wouldn’t matter. They’re barely alive. If one died, his giantess would forget him in three days.”

“Do they throw well enough that it could happen?” I asked.

“Wait till you see them,” she said, pride warming her voice.

“But I haven’t told you,” she continued, “about what happened the night before last.”

We had returned from gathering fruit and were asleep in our nests when we were woken by terrible sounds—beasts fighting.

The moon shone bright. In seconds, our trees glittered with hundreds of tiny eyes, watching two enormous leopardesses—one pure white, the other black-spotted—ripping at each other with teeth and claws.

From the way the spotted one’s back arched, I guessed she’d been climbing when the white one leapt on her.

When I first saw them, they were rolling beneath my tree. I climbed down to the lowest branch and watched.

The children were enthralled. Some cheered one, some the other. We’d never seen such beasts before—we thought they were only playing.

But slowly, their growls faded. I realized they were fighting to the death. I wished neither survived.

Then the children saw blood streaming from their flanks and throats.

And what do you think they did?

They scrambled down, rushing to comfort them. A crowd gathered around the two beasts, patting, stroking them.

I descended too—but they were too absorbed to hear me. Before I reached them, the white leopardess stopped fighting and roared—a shriek that sent the children flying into the trees like birds.

Before I could climb back, the two were locked in battle again.

This time, the white one won. The spotted one fled. Whitey came and lay at the foot of my tree.

Then, after a minute, she rose and paced slowly, as if searching for the other.

I woke several times. Each time, I saw her still there.

In the morning, she was gone.

“I know both,” I said. “Spotty is evil. She hates the children, wants to kill them. But Whitey loves them. She attacked only to scare them away—so Spotty couldn’t catch any. Don’t fear Whitey.”

By then, the children were returning—noisy, laden with stones. They climbed back to their nests, struggling under their loads. In moments, they were asleep.

Lona retreated to her tree. I stayed where I was, sleeping deeply—knowing the white leopardess might still be somewhere in the woods.

I woke just after sunrise, lying in thought. Two hours passed. Then, truly, the giants began to appear—three or four at a time, straggling in. I counted over a hundred.

The children were still asleep. To wake them would draw attention. I stayed silent.

But one giant stumbled upon me—fell, rose. I thought he’d pass on, but he began searching.

I sprang up and struck him hard in the chest.

His roar woke the children.

Instantly, a storm broke—like hail—of stones. Not one hit me. Every stone found its target.

He fell. Others approached. The storm spread. Each giant, stepping into the range of a tree, became a target—hit from all sides.

Soon, nearly every giant lay prostrate.

A jubilant song, like a chorus of birds, rose from fifty trees.

Elephants charged in. The children descended like monkeys. Within moments, each elephant bore three or four children. They walked over the fallen giants, who lay roaring.

Tired of the noise, the elephants swung their trunks—once, twice—then left.

All day, the giants remained where they fell—silent, motionless.

The next morning, they were gone.

Never again did the children see them.

They moved to the far end of the orchard valley—and never entered the forest again.

Victory won, the woman from Bulika began to speak of the city—its weakness, its corruption under the rule of a cruel princess, and the fear that gripped its people. In just a few days, the children were buzzing with talk of Bulika, though they had no real understanding of what a city even meant. It was then that I first grasped her purpose—though not yet the reason behind it.

The idea of claiming the city stirred something deep within Lona—and in me too. The children were growing fast, their minds sharpening, their bodies strong. I saw no true obstacle to success. And then there was Lilith—the terrible one, whether woman or beast. I knew her flaw: her daughter. I understood how the ancient prophecy haunted the people of Bulika, binding them in dread. Whatever risk there might be, it seemed worth taking. For if we succeeded—how could we doubt it?—the Little Ones would cease to be mere children and become a young nation, ruled by justice, guided by righteousness. They would govern the wicked with strength and mercy, becoming the redemption of an entire land.

Yet I must confess—my own ambitions stirred as well. It felt right that Lona should sit upon the throne once held by her mother. Naturally, she would make me her partner, her advisor, her consort. I would spend my life serving her. Together, with the Little Ones at our core, what noble world might we not build?

And there was another dream—one foolish, perhaps impossible—of trade between worlds, of gems exchanged across realms. But I knew better than to pursue it; such a thing would only bring harm.

Remembering Adam’s plea, I suggested to Lona that finding water might help speed the children’s growth. She thought otherwise. “We don’t know what water might do to them,” she said. “It might bind them to new needs. They are who they are without it. When we have the city, we’ll seek water.”

So we prepared. We drilled, we trained, we reviewed our ranks—our merry, eager troops. Lona took charge of supplies, organizing food and provisions. I taught the children war: how to throw stones, use slings, draw bows. Some of the larger boys carried arrows; the girls wielded aloe-spikes—tough as steel, sharp as needles—mounted on long shafts. Their task was to protect the youngest among us.

Lona had grown much, though she did not seem to notice. She remained the tallest, her hair long now, her form nearly that of a woman—but still, she kept every grace of childhood. When we met again after our long separation, she laid down her infant, wrapped her arms around my neck, and clung to me silently, her face radiant with joy. The child whimpered—she sprang to him, cradling him instantly. To see her with any restless or stubborn child was to see a gentle grandmother, full of patience and love.

I felt as though I had known her always—before time itself. My memory of my own mother was faint, but in my mind, she looked like Lona. Any image of sister or child I conjured bore her face. She was my heart’s wife. She rarely sought me, but was always near, within hearing. Everything I did, every thought I had, I shared with her. I rejoiced in knowing she worked beside me, independent yet ever close.

She never neglected a child—not one. And my love only deepened my sense of duty. To love her and serve her were not the same, but inseparable. She might suggest a course for me, ask my counsel—yet neither of us ever imagined doing anything but what must be done. Her love poured over me—not in words or touch, but in a quiet, unshakable presence, like the devotion of a sacred creature.

I told her nothing of her mother.

The forest teemed with birds—beauties of plumage so dazzling they made up for the lack of flowers, which seemed unable to grow without water. Their feathers littered the ground, and one day, a thought came to me: I would make a garment for Lona from them.

I gathered the feathers, binding them in overlapping rows. She watched, silent, appreciative of my choices, never asking what I was making, but clearly waiting for the result. In a week or two, it was finished—a long, loose mantle, fastening at throat and waist, with armholes. I rose and placed it on her shoulders.

She stepped back, removed it, and laid it gently at my feet—perhaps out of modesty. I put it back on her, showing her where to place her arms. She smiled, touched the feathers softly, then took it off again, placing it beside her. When she left, she carried it with her. I saw nothing more of it for days.

Then one morning, she returned wearing it—her own creation beside it. She had woven a second garment from the tough, dried leaves of an evergreen. It was strong as leather, patterned like scale armor. I slipped it on at once. From that day forward, we wore these cloaks whenever we rode.

For on the edge of the forest, horses had appeared—full-grown, calm, unafraid of creatures unlike themselves. I quickly befriended them, training two of the finest for Lona and myself. She already knew how to ride a small one, but when she first sat atop the giant beast, her joy was unmistakable. The horse, proud of its rider, moved with dignity and trust.

Each day we rode, building confidence until they obeyed without hesitation, fearless in battle or march. We rode them in review, in formation—ready.

Still, I sometimes doubted. Was this not madness? Yet the woman of Bulika’s confidence—real or feigned—held me. She insisted the princess’s magic would fail against the children. As for any army she might raise, our animal allies alone would give us the edge. “Even I,” she said, “could face two men of Bulika with a good stick.” She admitted fear of the leopardess—but I was ready for her.

Yet I hesitated to take all the children. “Shouldn’t you stay here?” I asked Lona. “With your baby and the smallest ones?”

“No,” she replied. “Their sight will move the women—especially the mothers. When they see the darlings, their hearts will break open. I must be there, encouraging them to stand. If courage remains in Bulika, it will be in the women.”

“You must not carry any child,” I said. “You’ll be needed everywhere.”

But she had two infants besides her own, and even on horseback, she always held one close.

“I’ve never been without a child,” she said simply. “But when we reach the city, I’ll do as you wish.”

Her trust—after all I had failed to do—shamed me. Yet I had not begun this. I could not oppose it. I had no choice but to give it my best.

For myself, I was ready to live or die with her. Her humility, her faith, humbled me. I gave myself fully to her cause.

Our path lay across a wide grassland. No need to feed the horses or the two cows we brought for the infants. But the elephants required care. The grass was good, but short—too short for their long, one-fingered trunks to gather enough. So we set the whole colony to work, gathering grass, drying it into hay. The elephants themselves could carry enough to last several days, supplemented by fresh harvests at each halt.

For the bears, we stored nuts. For ourselves, we dried fruits. We tamed more great horses, loaded them and the elephants with supplies. We were ready.

Then Lona and I held a final review. I addressed the children.

“I’ve learned much about you,” I began. “I know now where you came from.”

“We didn’t come from anywhere!” they shouted. “We’re here!”

“But each of you has a mother,” I said. “Like the mother of the newest baby. I believe you were all taken from Bulika when you were so small you can’t remember. The princess there fears babies—hates them. She wants to destroy them. So your mothers fled, carrying you away, hiding you where she couldn’t find you. Now we go to Bulika—to find your mothers, to free them from the evil giantess.”

“But danger lies ahead,” I warned. “We may have to fight hard to take the city.”

“We can fight! We’re ready!” the boys cried.

“Yes,” I said, “you can—and you will. Mothers are worth fighting for. But stay together.”

“Yes, yes—we’ll watch over each other!” they answered. “No one touches one of us unless it’s his own mother!”

“Listen,” I said. “Obey your officers at once.”

“We will! We will! We’re ready! Let’s go!”

“One more thing,” I added. “When you strike, make it a clean, hard blow. When you shoot, draw the bow to your head. When you sling, throw strong and straight.”

“That we will!” they roared, voices bright and fearless.

“You may be hurt.”

“We don’t mind!” they shouted.

“Some of you may die.”

“I don’t mind being killed!” said one boy, riding a little bull that leapt like a horse.

“Neither do I!” echoed the others.

Then Lona, queen and mother and sister to them all, spoke from her great horse beside me:

“I would give my life,” she said, “to see my mother again. She could kill me—I’d just kiss her and die.”

“Come on, boys!” called a girl. “We’re going home!”

A pang pierced my heart. But I could not turn back. To retreat now would be moral ruin—for them, for us, for everything we stood for.

The morning dawned clear and bright as we set forth across the wide plain, a vibrant procession beneath a sky of endless blue and grass that shimmered like emerald silk. We moved with purpose—marching through the morning light, resting in the afternoon heat, then pressing on under the starlit night. Our rhythm was deliberate: travel by day, pause by sun, journey again at twilight. By the time we reached the city’s edge, we aimed to arrive just as the first golden rays touched the horizon, slipping through the gates before anyone could raise alarm.

It seemed the entire forest had come with us. Birds surged ahead in flocks, mistaken for vanguards; butterflies and dragonflies danced around our heads like living sparks; and four-legged creatures trailed behind, drawn by curiosity or instinct. As dusk fell, most of the beasts turned back, but the birds and insects stayed, fluttering and humming all the way to the city walls.

We halted mid-afternoon and slept soundly—a true march, yet no one felt weariness. At night, the cold urged us forward. Some drifted off atop their mounts, waking refreshed with the morning light. Not a single rider tumbled from their beast. Bears, shaggy and slow-moving, carried some, yet kept pace with the elephants. Others rode deer, eager to race, until I stopped them. Those perched high above on hay-laden elephants, unable to see below, chatted endlessly with their unseen companions. Once, during a rest, I heard a small voice whisper into the bundle of straw: “Nosy dear, I’m digging you out of the mountain now—just a little more! Soon you’ll lift your nose up, and we’ll kiss like good elephants, won’t we?”

That night, a storm of noise erupted—elephant roars, horse whinnies, children’s cries echoing far across the still land. Fearing we might be too close to the city, I silenced the clamor swiftly, lest it betray our presence.

Then, one morning, the sun rose—and so did the city, rising from the earth like a dream made stone. To the children, the walls were merely a great rock formation. But when I told them it held countless stone nests within, fear flickered in their eyes for the first time—perhaps the first real fear in their short lives. How could they find mothers in such a place? Yet they pressed on, guided by faith in Lona, and in me, though I knew I scarcely deserved it.

We rode through the archway. The thunder of hooves, the pad of paws and feet, shook the ancient stones. Horses startled at their own echoes, some reared, others veered wildly—but soon calmed. The Little Ones stood frozen, silent as shadows. The girls clutched their infants tighter. Only bears and butterflies remained unafraid. The woman beside me wore a mask of dread, and I, too, felt its weight—this army was mine, and I bore its fate. I had led them to danger, and now its shadow stretched over us.

But I found strength in vision—the kingdom I dreamed of, where the Little Ones ruled, the giants served, and animals bowed in loyalty. Alas, I who spoke of freedom had not learned obedience myself. My pride had placed me at the head of this innocent host. I was no king—I was a slave, like any ruler in the world I’d left behind, doing only what pleased me.

Yet beside me rode Lona—truly a child, truly free. Calm, watchful, fearless. She needed no words, no reassurance. Her presence steadied me.

We neared the heart of the city before any inhabitant noticed us. Then windows flew open, sleepy faces peered out. At first, blank astonishment spread across each face. But when they saw the animals—bears, deer, elephants—their expressions twisted into horror. Still, upon seeing that our force was almost entirely children, women poured into the streets, followed by men. They kept to the edges, leaving the center clear—afraid to approach the beasts.

Then, a boy—no older than five, his mind fixed on finding his mother—spied a woman in the crowd whose face stirred recognition. He leapt from his antelope and clung to her neck. She embraced him instantly, kissing him fiercely. But a man’s hand seized the boy by the throat. A girl nearby drove her spear into the man’s arm. He howled, and in seconds, several others struck him down. He fled, screaming.

“They are just bad giants,” Lona said, her voice sharp as steel, as she spurred her horse toward a towering figure blocking her path. The giant flinched, stepped aside—and moments later crumpled under a hail of stones.

Another brute stepped between me and the boy riding behind me, speaking in a language harsh and guttural. The boy told him to speak to the king. The giant swung a hammer, striking the horse’s head. It collapsed. Before he could strike again, an elephant charged, knocking him flat and trampling him underfoot. He didn’t rise until long after we passed.

But when the women came into view, Lona’s face darkened. Not one looked kind, not one seemed worthy. Could her darlings find mothers among such faces?

No sooner had we entered the central square than two girls galloped up, breathless. Two boys had been taken by women. We turned—only to realize the woman we had trusted had vanished with her baby.

Then, from a narrow lane leading to the palace, a white leopardess bounded toward us. The Little Ones remembered the battle in the forest—some recoiled, their ranks wavering. But I had given orders. They stood firm.

We watched. Then Odu, a swift and brave boy, leapt from his bear and ran to meet her. The leopardess braked so sharply she rolled over twice. When she regained her footing, the child was already on her back.

Who could doubt the power of transformation when a child of the enemy rode the creature they feared most?

Confident now, we continued. At the house our guides pointed to, a scream tore through the air. I dismounted and pounded on the door. My horse pushed me aside, turned, and began battering the wood with his heels.

Then—Odu arrived, riding the leopardess. He froze, trembling. But the animal had heard the cry. She forgot the child on her back and lunged at the door. The boy was thrown against it, knocked unconscious.

Before I could move, Lona had him in her arms. When he woke, she placed him back on his bear, which had followed faithfully.

The leopardess charged the door a third time—and it gave way. She darted inside.

We rushed after her. The house was empty. Upstairs, nothing. Downstairs, we found a hidden door beneath the stairs, leading into a maze of tunnels.

Not far in, we met the leopardess again—carrying the boy we sought.

He told us the woman he thought was his mother threw him into a pit, saying she would give him to the leopardess. But the leopardess was good. She pulled him out.

We searched for the other boy. Found him too late. One of the savages had killed him.

Lona sighed—not with grief, but relief. “At least,” she said softly, “he won’t grow into a bad giant.” Death had spared him the worst.

The leopardess leapt onto the murderer, seized him by the throat, dragged him into the street, and carried him like a cat dragging a rat.

“Let us leave this place,” Lona said. “There are no mothers here. This people is not worth saving.”

She dropped the body. The leopardess charged into the crowd, slashing through the throng, scattering slaves like leaves in a gale.

Back at the army, all stood in order, waiting. But I was uneasy. The princess had not shown herself. What secrets might she hide? We would keep watch through the night.

The Little Ones needed no beds. We told them to lie down with their animals where they stood. In an instant, they were asleep—breathing in soft, rhythmic waves, like water flowing over grass, or wind through leaves.

Their beasts slept lightly, ever alert. Older children wandered quietly among the sleeping mass. Silence fell. The whole city seemed to breathe with us—still, quiet, and heavy with unease.

Lona was repulsed by the people—especially the women—and longed to flee the city at once. But I could not abandon hope so easily. To retreat would be to surrender without a fight, and worse, to shatter the courage of the Little Ones, leaving them exposed to greater peril. If we fled, the princess would surely pursue us. But if we faced her, perhaps the prophecy might still be fulfilled. Mother and daughter must meet. Maybe Lona’s beauty could awaken something in Lilith—something softer than hatred. If she turned violent, I would stand between them. And if all else failed, I would strike her down—without mercy, without hesitation—for the sake of my Lona. I knew her fate was sealed; perhaps it was written that she would fall by our hands.

I told Lona this plainly, without revealing the truth of their bond. She listened, then nodded. “We go,” she said. And so we did.

From the highest tower of the palace, the princess had seen the army of the Little Ones approach at dawn, while the city still slept. The sight struck her with a terror beyond measure. Her plan to destroy them had failed. Now they were upon her. The prophecy loomed close. When she regained herself, she descended to the black hall and took her place in the northern focus of the elliptical chamber, beneath the skylight. There she would think. But for her, *thinking* meant seeing herself not as she was, but as she wished to be—radiant, perfect, divine. To aid this illusion, she had hung a mirror above and slightly before her, invisible in the darkness, to catch the sunlight reflected from her own form. Only when the sun reached its zenith would she see herself in full glory.

The shadows danced around her, but she ignored them. With her inner vision, she perceived their presence, yet refused to acknowledge them. Close beneath the mirror stood the Shadow—the silent companion who followed her wherever she went—but she did not see him. The city had fallen. Its people cowered. The Little Ones and their strange beasts occupied the square. The sun rose, and for a brief moment, the princess saw herself as she desired: magnificent, flawless, untouched by time or sin. Then the vision faded. Darkness returned. Yet she remained seated. The night deepened, and the glass held only void. Still she sat. The palace groaned with unseen presences. Servants trembled, too afraid to leave, bound by fear of the creatures outside. All night long, she waited—motionless—desperate to see her beauty again, desperate to think again. But courage had abandoned her. Will had fled. She was hollow.

At dawn, we chose twelve of the tallest, bravest boys to accompany us. We rode our great horses; they mounted smaller steeds and elephants. As we climbed toward the palace, the sun rose higher, creeping across the dome of the great hall. It pierced the eye of the structure—and suddenly, the light flooded in.

The princess gasped. She sprang up with a cry of horror. Her whiteness—her perfect skin—was marred. A vast black spot covered half her side, like a brand burned into flesh. She clutched her robe, collapsed back into her chair. The Shadow slipped away—unseen, unheeded.

We entered through the open gate, passed the paved grove, and stepped into the vestibule. There, in her cage, lay the spotted leopardess—still, motionless, perhaps dead. The Little Ones paused. The beast leapt up, snarling, against the bars. Horses reared. Elephants stepped back. Then, with a sudden convulsion, she fell backward, twitching, and lay still.

We rode into the great hall. The princess still sat in the shaft of sunlight when the sound of hooves echoed from the courtyard. She jerked upright, listening, trembling. No such noise had ever disturbed her palace. She pressed a hand to her side, breath catching in her throat. The trampling drew closer—into the hall itself. Figures emerged from the dark, not shadows, but living beings.

To us, she was a vision—a woman of radiant beauty, centered in darkness. Lona dismounted, running forward. I followed.

“Mother! Mother!” she called, her voice pure and clear, echoing through the dome.

The princess froze. Her face twisted with rage. Her brows met in a line of hatred. She rose.

“Mother! Mother!” Lona cried again, leaping onto the dais, arms outstretched.

I was almost there—just one step—when I saw it: Lona lifted high, thrown violently onto the marble floor. The sound—crash, thud, silence—shattered me.

She fell at my feet, still. I dropped to my knees, lifted her, cradled her in my arms. My eyes burned with fury as I looked at the princess. She smiled—sweet, cruel, triumphant.

I would have torn her apart. But love for Lona overpowered hatred. I held her tighter. Her arms hung limp. Blood seeped from her temple, dripping slowly onto the stone—soft, quiet plashes.

The horses smelled it. Mine reared, wild-eyed, and charged blindly down the hall. The others followed. One of Lona’s small horses stood frozen, staring at her, trembling. The boys jumped off—too late. They crashed into the wall, broken, lifeless. The elephants advanced to the dais, roaring in panic. The Little Ones scrambled up, horrified. The princess slumped back in her seat—face pale as death, eyes blazing with malice. She was shriveled, wasted, her side scarred as though branded by fire.

But I saw none of it. I saw only Lona.

“Mother… mother…” she whispered, then her breath stopped.

I carried her into the courtyard. The sun lit her white face. A ghostly smile seemed to touch her lips. Her head fell back. She was gone—dead as earth.

I forgot the Little Ones. Forgot the princess. Forgot the body in my arms. I wandered, searching for my Lona—my lost queen.

The windows and doors were lined with brutish faces, mocking me, but不敢 speak. Behind me, the white leopardess trailed, head low, eyes fixed on me. I kicked her. She hesitated, then came again.

I reached the square. Empty. The little army was gone. Where were the Little Ones? *Her* children? I had lost them.

I stared, helpless. Staggered to the pillar, sank to its base.

Then—through the stillness—I thought I saw her smile. Just for a moment. Not real, yet true. A message: *You will see her alive.* It was not she who was lost—it was I. She would find me.

I rose. I would follow the Little Ones. Instinct led me to the gate we’d entered by. I turned—no leopardess. The street filled with a surging crowd. They saw me burdened with the dead, but dared not attack—at first.

Then, a man pushed past me, brushing against Lona. I kicked him—hard. He screamed, fell back.

But the mob closed in. Fearing harm to the body, I clutched her tighter, freed my right arm.

Then—behind me—chaos erupted. The crowd parted. Through it came the Little Ones.

Ten rode four elephants. On the other two lay the princess—bound, silent, her eyes rolling in their sockets. Two more rode behind her on Lona’s horse. Every now and then, the elephants turned their trunks and tested her bonds.

I walked ahead, out of the city.

What an end to the hopes that brought us here. We captured the wicked princess. But we lost our beloved queen. My world was stripped bare. My heart—empty.

A soft murmur of joy stirred among my companions—someone had spotted the others in the distance. Two riders, mounted on Lona’s horse, spurred forward to join their kin. But as they neared, the cheer faltered into silence. Then came a sound like waves breaking against stone: quiet, broken sobs.

When I reached them, I saw at once that something terrible had happened. Their faces—so young, so bright just days before—were now hollowed by dread, eyes wide with a fear too deep for words. Grief could not have carved such emptiness into their features. A few approached slowly, arms outstretched, seeking to take the burden from me. I gave it over. The way they looked at it—gentle, tender, yet hopeless—made my chest tighten with sorrow, even as my own heart lay shattered.

They wept over her—their mother-queen—kissed her hands, pressed their cheeks to hers, called her name, but she did not stir. Not one flicker of life. One child carried each arm, stroking them softly as if to coax warmth back. Others cradled her body beneath their small frames. Those left behind crowded close, forming a silent ring around her.

On a patch of thick, yielding grass, they laid her down. And there, the Little Ones gathered—sobbing, whispering, clinging to one another in the dimming light. Outside the circle stood the elephants, massive and still. I stood near them, staring across the sea of tiny heads at Lona’s pale face.

Then Odu, closest to me, froze. His eyes locked onto the princess. “I’ve seen her before,” he whispered, voice trembling. “She was the one who fought the white leopardess—the night they woke us with their screaming!”

“Foolishness,” replied his neighbor. “That was a beast. Spotted.”

“Look at her eyes!” Odu insisted. “She’s a giantess—evil—but still a beast. The same one. The spotted one!”

The other stepped closer. Odu yanked him back. “Don’t look at her!” he cried, recoiling yet unable to turn away. “She’ll devour you! That was *her* shadow! She’s the wicked princess!”

“That can’t be,” said another. “They said she was beautiful.”

“It *is* the princess,” I said quietly, stepping forward. “But wickedness has turned her ugly.”

She heard. And when she did—oh, what a glance she cast! Not anger, not sorrow… but a terrible, ancient hunger.

Odu lowered his head. “It was wrong of me to run,” he murmured.

“What made you run?” I asked.

“I thought I’d find you where I left you,” he said, voice small.

He paused. Then another spoke. “I don’t know why I ran. I was scared.”

“A man came down the hill from the palace,” said a third.

“How did he scare you?”

“I don’t know.”

“He wasn’t a man,” Odu said. “He was a shadow. No thickness. Just black.”

“Tell me more.”

“He walked down the hill—black as night, moving like a bad giant, but flat. Like smoke stretched thin. We saw him and were afraid—but we didn’t run. We watched. He came on, like he meant to walk over us. But then… he began to spread. Bigger. Wider. Until he filled the sky. Then he vanished. And then—he was *on* us.”

“What do you mean?”

“He was between us. Black through everything. We couldn’t see each other. And then—he was *inside* us.”

“How did you know?”

“He changed me. I felt… bad. Not Odu. Not the Odu I knew. I wanted to tear Sozo apart—not really, but… like.”

He turned and clutched Sozo tightly. “It wasn’t me, Sozo,” he sobbed. “Deep inside, it was still Odu—loving you. And then Odu rose up and knocked Naughty away. I grew sick. I thought I had to die to get free. Then—I heard a laugh. A horrible laugh. It echoed in my mind. And then I was running. I didn’t know I was running until I found myself flying across the grass, all of us fleeing. I wanted to stop—but I didn’t think of it until I was outside the gate. Then I realized: I had run from a shadow that tried to become me, but failed. I was still Odu. Still loving Sozo. The shadow had crawled inside me, hated him from within—it wasn’t *me*. And now I know I shouldn’t have fled. But I didn’t truly understand what I was doing until it was done. My legs—they acted without me. They were frightened. They forgot me. They ran. Naughty legs! There! And there!”

He kicked both feet sharply, as if punishing them.

“What became of the shadow?” I asked.

“I don’t know,” he said. “I suppose it went back into the night. Where there is no moon.”

I knelt beside Lona, took her cold form into my lap, and whispered into her ear: “Where are you, Lona? I love you.”

No answer. Her lips remained sealed.

I kissed them—still warm, barely touched by death—and laid her gently back upon the grass. I posted a guard, then rose to ensure the safety of her people through the long night.

Before the sun sank, I placed sentinels around the camp, stationed a watch near the princess, and told the rest to sleep. They fell instantly, sprawled on the earth like children worn out by sorrow.

When the moon rose, I saw something white gliding past the sleeping forms. The leopardess. Silent, swift. She circled the camp three times—always between Lona and the Little Ones. Then she vanished.

I dismissed the watch, let them rest. And I lay beside Lona, watching the stars, waiting for dawn—or whatever might come after.

At dawn, we set forth, pressing through the forest with urgent haste. I rode Lona’s horse, her lifeless form cradled before me in her own cloak. I would carry her to her father—where she would rest among the honored dead, or if he refused her, I would leave her body in the desert until time reduced it to dust. Yet I believed he would welcome her. After all, she had long since passed beyond breath and light.

And Lilith—I bore her too, though not by choice. I could not force her repentance. I lacked even the right to end her life, much less release her into a world that might tremble at her presence. To be her keeper forever felt like a curse. Yet I had no other path.

On the road, I offered her food again and again. She only answered with eyes aflame—hungering, hateful, never still. Her gaze darted endlessly across the void, unblinking until we crossed the scorching river. Then, at last, they closed. Not again until we reached the House of Bitterness.

One night, as we camped beneath the stars, I saw a small girl approach her. I rushed forward, fearing harm—but too late. The child had placed something to Lilith’s lips and screamed in agony. “Please, king,” she whimpered, clutching her tiny finger, “suck it! The giantess made a hole!”

I drew the wound close to my mouth and soothed it. “There!” she cheered, already reaching for another fruit. But this time, Lilith snatched her hand back. The fruit fell. The girl was named Luva.

The next day, we crossed the hot stream. On their native ground, the Little Ones danced with joy. But their nests lay far ahead, so we stopped at the ivy-hall, where clusters of grapes hung heavy and sweet. As soon as they saw them, the children surged forward, devouring the fruit greedily. Within minutes, they slumbered—some on the mossy floor, others curled beneath the trees.

I had hoped to witness the dance once more. Expecting them to sleep through it, I left a wide circle in the center. I laid down beside Lona, but sleep did not come. Night fell, and suddenly—the company arrived.

I wondered: would they dance eternally? Would I one day be drawn into their endless motion, bound by my own stubbornness? Then the children stirred. Their eyes snapped open. They leapt up, and each seized a dancer. Together, they bounded into the rhythm, skipping and laughing. The spectres seemed to recognize them, to welcome them. Perhaps they remembered childhood themselves—once lost, now returning. Or perhaps the innocence of the Little Ones brought comfort to souls stripped of present and future, clinging only to memory.

They played harmless tricks, never cruel. When they disrupted the dance, the ghosts smiled—not with laughter, but with relief. They had nothing else to smile with.

Just before dawn, I sat up—startled. In the doorway stood the skeleton-princess, eyes wide and glowing, the black mark upon her side unmistakable. She glided forward as if to join the dance. I rose in alarm. A cry of horror erupted from the children—and the lights vanished. Only the low moon remained, casting pale light over the scene. I saw them huddled together, trembling. The ghosts were gone. The princess had disappeared.

I dashed to where she had lain. She rested there, eyes shut, as if untouched. I returned to the hall. The children were already settling into sleep.

Next morning, as we moved on, two skeletons appeared in a thicket nearby. The Little Ones broke ranks and ran toward them. I followed, now walking without pain or aid. I recognized them—the pair I had seen before. The children clung to their arms, stroked their bony fingers. The creatures welcomed their touch. They walked together, united by shared loss. Deprived of everything, they had found something new: companionship.

They gathered herbs, likely to rub upon their bones—how else could nourishment reach such hollow frames? The children examined the plants closely, then collected the same kinds, filling the skeletons’ outstretched hands. They bid farewell, promising to return. “We didn’t know nice people lived here,” one whispered.

When we reached the nest-village, I stayed a night to see them settled. Lona still looked untouched by time. There was no need to rush.

Lilith had eaten nothing. Her eyes remained closed. Fearing she might die before our journey ended, I went to her in the dark. I placed my bare arm against her lips. She bit down—fiercely. I screamed. How I escaped, I do not know. I woke miles away, lying beyond her reach. Dawn was breaking. I began our departure at once.

I chose twelve of the sweetest, merriest Little Ones, mounted six on elephants, and took two wise Clumsies—those the children called "wise"—to bear the princess. I still rode Lona’s horse, carrying her body wrapped in her cloak. I took the direct route across the left branch of the dead river, aiming for the House of Bitterness. I hoped to learn how best to cross the wider, rougher stretch and avoid the basin of monsters—a place I feared for the elephants, and worse, for the children.

That third night, in the desert between the river branches, was terrible. We halted the elephants in a sheltered spot and let the princess fall between them, to lie on the sand until morning. She seemed dead—but I knew better. I lay some distance from her, Lona’s body beside me, guarding both the dead and the dangerous.

The moon hung low in the west, pale and thoughtful, casting shadows across the dunes. Suddenly, it was eclipsed—not hidden, but veiled. A thin, translucent film covered its face, distorting its beauty. Then I saw the edge—jagged, wing-like, torn and hooked.

A cold wind blew, stinging with heat. Lilith was upon me.

Her hands were bound, but with her teeth, she tore the cloak Lona had made for me from my shoulder and sank her fangs into my flesh. I lay paralyzed. Life flowed from me into her. Then I remembered—and struck her hand.

She shrieked, gurgling, and shivered. I flung her off and rose. She knelt, rocking back and forth. Another blast of icy fire swept over us. The moon reappeared, clear and bright. I saw her face—gaunt, bloodied, ghastly.

“Down, devil!” I shouted.

“Where are you taking me?” she whispered, voice like an echo from a tomb.

“To your first husband,” I said.

“He will kill me!” she moaned.

“At least he’ll take you off my hands.”

“Give me my daughter!” she screamed, grinding her teeth.

“Never. Your doom is at hand.”

“Loose my hands—for pity’s sake! I’m in torment. The cords are deep in my flesh.”

“I dare not. Lie down.”

She collapsed like a log. The rest of the night passed quietly. By morning, she seemed dead again.

Before evening, we sighted the House of Bitterness. Instantly, one of the elephants came alongside my horse.

“Please, king,” whispered the rider, “you’re not going *there*?”

“I am,” I replied. “We’ll stay the night.”

“Oh, please don’t! That must be where the cat-woman lives!”

“If you’d seen her, you wouldn’t call her that.”

“Nobody sees her. She’s lost her face. Her head is all around—back and side.”

“She hides it from dull, discontented souls. Who taught you to call her ‘cat-woman’?”

“The bad giants.”

“What did they say?”

“That she has claws to her toes.”

“False. I know her. I spent a night in her house.”

“But she *might* have claws! You could see her feet, and her claws folded inside her shoes!”

“Then perhaps you think I have claws?”

“No! You’re good!”

“The giants told you that about me?”

“We wouldn’t believe them about you!”

“Are the giants good?”

“No. They love lies.”

“So why believe them about her? I know she’s good. She cannot have claws.”

“How do you know?”

“How do you know I’m good?”

I rode on. He waited, then told his friends what I had said. They hurried after me.

“I wouldn’t bring you here if I didn’t trust her,” I said.

“We know that,” they answered.

“If I did something that frightened you—what would you say?”

“The beasts scared us at first,” one said. “But they never hurt us.”

“That was before we knew them,” added another.

“Exactly. When you see the woman in that cottage, you’ll know she’s good. You may wonder at what she does—but she will always be good. I know her better than you know me. She won’t hurt you. Or—if she does…”

“Oh, you’re not sure, king dear! You think she *might* hurt us!”

“I’m certain she’ll never be unkind—even if she harms you.”

They were silent.

“I’m not afraid of being hurt—just a little! A lot!” Odu declared.

“But I don’t want scratches in the dark! The giants say the cat-woman has claw-feet all over her house!”

“I’m taking the princess to her,” I said.

“Why?”

“Because she is her friend.”

“How can she be good then?”

“Little Tumbledown is her friend. So is Luva. I’ve seen them try to feed her grapes.”

“Little Tumbled down is good! Luva is very good!”

“And that’s why they’re friends.”

“Will the cat-woman—no, the woman who isn’t the cat-woman—give her grapes?”

“She’s more likely to give her scratches.”

“Why? You say she’s her friend!”

“Precisely. A friend gives what is needed. The princess needs a terrible scratching.”

Silence.

“If any of you are afraid,” I said, “you may go home. I won’t stop you. But I cannot take one who trusts giants over me—or calls a good woman the cat-woman.”

“Please, king,” one said, “I’m so afraid of being afraid.”

“My boy,” I replied, “there’s no harm in fear. Only in obeying it. Fear is not your master. Laugh in its face—and it flees.”

“There she is—in the door, waiting!” one cried, covering his eyes.

“How ugly she is!” another gasped, doing the same.

“You don’t see her,” I said. “Her face is covered.”

“She has no face!”

“She has a beautiful face. I’ve seen it. As beautiful as Lona’s.”

“Then why does she hide it?”

“I think I know. She has reason.”

“I don’t like the cat-woman! She’s frightful!”

“You can’t like or dislike what you haven’t seen. And you must not call her that.”

“What should we call her?”

“Lady Mara.”

“A pretty name!” said a girl. “I’ll call her Lady Mara. Maybe she’ll show me her beautiful face!”

Mara stood in the doorway, wrapped in white. She stepped forward, took Lona’s body from my arms, carried it into the house, then returned for Lilith. The princess shuddered, but offered no resistance. The beasts lay down by the door. We followed, the Little Ones solemn.

Mara placed Lilith on a rough settle, unbound her, then turned to us.

“Mr. Vane,” she said, “and you, Little Ones—I thank you. This woman would not yield to kindness. Now harder measures must follow. I shall do what I can to make her repent.”

The children began to sob.

“Will you hurt her very much, Lady Mara?” asked the girl who had spoken earlier, placing her warm hand in mine.

“Yes. I fear I must. I fear she will make me. It would be cruel to hurt her too little. We’d have to do it again—worse.”

“May I stay with her?”

“No, child. She loves no one. Therefore, she cannot be *with* anyone. There is One who will be with her—but she will not be with Him.”

“Will the shadow that came down the hill be with her?”

“The great Shadow will be *in* her, I fear. But He cannot be *with* her—or with anyone. She will know I am near—but that will not comfort her.”

“Will you scratch her very deep?” Odu asked, stepping close, hand in hers.

“Please, don’t make the red juice come!”

She lifted him, turned her back to us, and lowered the muffling from her face. She held him at arm’s length so he might see.
