For a moment, he stared—mouth agape. Then, a divine wonder bloomed in his eyes, swiftly turning to rapture. He gazed, entranced. Then she set him down. He stood, transfixed, looking up at her—then ran to us, face radiant with a bliss he could not name.

Mara pulled her veil back into place and turned to the others.

“You must eat and drink before sleep,” she said. “You’ve had a long journey.”

She brought bread—hard, dry—and water in a jug. They had never seen either. Yet they ate the bread without complaint, drank the water with faces lit by astonishment.

Then she led the youngest to the garret. With gentle hands, she tucked them onto the floor. And the Little Ones, for the first time, slept under a roof that held no fear—only peace.

That night was one of unrest and dread, a time when sleep offered no refuge and waking brought no peace. The children, huddled in their beds, spoke afterward of things they could not name—things that slipped through the silence like shadows, that filled the house with unseen movement and unspoken terror. They did not hear a sound, yet they felt it all: a presence, a pressure, a slow unraveling of what was real.

Once, the house trembled as if struck by a wind so silent it made no noise at all—a wind that shook the beams and rattled the shutters without a moan. It passed like a breath held too long, vanishing into the dark. Then, they woke to water rising from beneath the floor, swelling upward until it filled the garret, reaching the very rafters. Yet there was no splash, no ripple—only the cold certainty of immersion. And then, just as suddenly, it receded, leaving them dry and warm, though none could explain how.

Later, the air grew thick with cats—hundreds of them, crawling across walls, slipping down the ceiling, pressing against the bedclothes. Their claws scraped at the fabric of their nightgowns, but never broke skin. In the morning, no mark remained. Only once did sound break the stillness: a distant, mournful howl from the desert—the call of the great-grandmother-cat, ancient and vast, summoning her offspring. At that moment, the cats froze, and all was quiet again.

They slept then, deeply, until dawn crept over the edge of the world.

But I had not slept. I watched with Mara, the veiled woman, and Lilith, the princess, throughout the long night. What I saw, what I felt, cannot be told in full. Some truths are beyond words; some moments belong only to silence.

When Mara left the room with the children, my gaze fell upon the white leopardess—once thought lost, now crouched in the corner, trembling. She seemed afraid not of the darkness, but of what might come out of it. A lamp burned on the mantel, casting shifting shapes across the walls—now sharp, now blurred, now melting into forms that were neither flesh nor spirit. The princess lay motionless on the settle, her body rigid, her face pale as stone. Time stretched thin. Every breath felt like an effort.

When Mara returned, she moved the settle to the center of the room, sat opposite me across the hearth, where a small fire flickered. Between us, the flames danced low, yet they did not warm. Something was coming—not from outside, but from within the house, from within the air, from within the earth itself. The shadows wavered. A silvery creature, like a worm of pure light, crawled from the gloom, crossed the floor without sound, and vanished into the fire.

We waited. The hours passed. Midnight approached. No wind stirred. No crack split the silence. But deep inside, something heaved—like the breath of a sleeping giant, or the pulse of the world’s hidden heart. I felt it in my bones, in my blood, in my soul. A weight pressed down, not of fear, but of judgment. And yet, I was not afraid. For I knew: this was the hour.

At midnight, Mara rose. Slowly, deliberately, she unwound the cloth that covered her face. It fell to the floor, and she stepped over it. She stood before the princess, whose feet pointed toward the fire. Mara went to her head, turned, and stood behind it. Then I saw her face.

It was beautiful—beyond description. White, sorrowful, yet not broken. Tears traced paths down her cheeks, but she wiped them away, and her expression stilled. Her eyes held a depth that could not be pierced. She laid her hand gently on the princess’s forehead, breathed upon her sallow brow.

“Will you turn from the wicked things you have done?” Mara asked, softly.

No answer came.

She repeated the question. Still nothing.

A third time, the same voice, gentle as rain.

Then, the princess opened her mouth—and spoke. Not in sound, but in essence. Words formed not from air, but from the core of being. I heard them, though I could not name them.

“I will not,” she said. “I will be myself, not another.”

“You are not yourself,” Mara replied. “You are a shadow. Will you not return to who you were?”

“I will be what I mean to be.”

“You do evil because your nature is good, yet you twist it.”

“I will do as my Self desires.”

“And your Self is ruled by the Shadow?”

“I will do what I will.”

“You have killed your daughter—Lilith.”

“She is mine.”

“She was never yours.”

“I am not another’s. I am my own.”

“Then your hour has come.”

“I care not. I am what I am. No one can take that from me.”

“You are not free. You are bound by the lie you believe.”

“I defy you! I am free!”

“You are a slave to your own pride.”

The silence deepened. Then, a presence filled the house—not of sound, but of flame. The fire on the hearth glowed still, but the silver worm reappeared, now blazing white-hot, crawling slowly across the floor, up the settle, to the princess’s feet. It did not burn. It crept upward, over her robe, into her chest, disappearing into the folds.

I lunged forward to stop it. Mara blocked me. She parted the edges of the robe—no trace remained. The worm had entered not through flesh, but through the black spot on her chest, piercing bone and marrow, thoughts and intent.

The princess shuddered. Then, slowly, tears began to form on her skin—tiny droplets, like dew, growing into pearls, then streams. I saw her pain, but I could not reach her.

“She sees herself,” Mara whispered. She drew me back three steps.

Suddenly, the princess arched her back, sprang to her feet. Her face twisted in horror. Her hair dripped, then sparked, then poured sweat. Her breath came in gasps, yet no air escaped. She stood rigid, torn apart by inner fire.

“Do not go near her,” Mara said. “She is far away—in the hell of self-knowledge. The fire of the universe burns in her now. She sees the truth: she is both good and evil. She is the fire, and she is burning in it. But she does not know that the Light is in the fire.”

Time lost meaning. Minutes? Years? I could not tell. Finally, she collapsed, face down. Mara knelt beside her. Tears fell from Mara’s eyes onto the woman who had never wept.

“Will you change?” she asked.

“I would have made myself different,” Lilith gasped. “I am glad He made me, not I. He alone is to blame. I would never have made such a thing! He meant me to suffer. Let Him kill me!”

“Unmake yourself,” Mara said.

“I cannot. The tyrant keeps me alive. I curse Him. Kill me.”

“Had He not made you, you could not even hate Him. But He did not make you thus. You made yourself.”

“I will not be remade.”

“Then let Him restore you.”

“I will not be His creation.”

“Are you not willing to undo what you have done?”

Silence.

“Put yourself back on the settle,” Mara said.

“I will not.”

Then, a wind arose—soundless, invisible. Water rose from below, cold but not freezing, rising without wave or sound. It lifted her, bore her gently, and set her down on the settle. Then it sank away.

Her mind warred with itself—accusing, defending, breaking. Her soul was stripped bare under the light of truth. She moaned, sighed, murmured to a phantom self. Her kingdom was shattered. One moment she laughed like a demon, the next she wept like a child.

She began to speak—a tale in a language half-forgotten, shaped like dreams I once knew. It touched on things Adam had read in the broken manuscript, on powers and vices I could not name. Then she stopped.

Again, the horror in her hair—sparks, then flow. I looked to Mara.

“Those are not tears of repentance,” she said. “True sorrow gathers in the eyes. These are bitter—self-loathing. But it is a step. The prodigal forgets himself when he returns.”

She leaned close. “Will you give back what you stole?”

“I took only what I had right to,” Lilith said through clenched teeth. “My power proved my right.”

Mara withdrew.

Then came the worst: a darkness deeper than night, a Nothingness that pressed inward. It touched me—Death Absolute. Not absence, but presence of void. I felt it. I knew it.

With a cry that tore her soul, Lilith threw herself from the settle.

“For pity’s sake!” she shrieked. “Tear my heart out—but let me live!”

And then—calm. A perfect stillness, like a summer night after storm. Suffering had reached its peak, and a hand had emptied the cup.

She rose. Looked around. Stood tall. Conqueror. Victorious.

She raised her arm—then froze.

Her eyes fixed on something unseen.

I looked—and saw: before her stood two reflections—one of her true self, radiant, whole; the other, her present form, broken, corrupted. She trembled. Fell to the floor.

She knew now: what God intended, and what she had become.

For the rest of the night, she lay still.

At dawn, she rose. Turned to Mara.

“You have conquered,” she said. “Let me go into the wilderness and mourn.”

Mara studied her. “Begin,” she said. “Set right what is wrong.”

“I do not know how.”

“Open your hand. Let go what is in it.”

She hesitated. Her hand—more paw than hand—clenched tight.

“I cannot,” she said. “I have no power.”

“Then open it for me.”

She extended it. Mara did not look.

“You must open it yourself,” she said. “You can, if you will.”

“I cannot!”

“You have opened it before. It takes effort. Pain. But you CAN.”

“I will not try what I know impossible.”

“You have been playing the fool all your life.”

Defiance flashed.

“I know what you wanted! You failed. I am still queen of Hell!”

Then came the horror—Life in Death. Alive, yet dead. Existing, yet gone. I felt it—something I could not imagine, something that would kill me if it touched me.

It was in her.

Something departed from me—life, warmth, sense. I felt myself fading. The eternal fire dimmed. I was becoming aware only of what had been.

Yet I turned to Lilith.

She stood rigid. Life had withdrawn. All that remained was the dregs—dead, corrupted, conscious. She was a corpse that could not die. She had killed her own life and lived on.

She knew it.

She saw it.

She was a dead thing, knowing it.

She had tried to unmake herself—and could not.

She was trapped.

In her face, I saw more than misery—I saw the abyss behind it. Her eyes were wide open, staring into the heart of horror. Her hand—still clenched—held nothing, yet everything.

Then, without warning, she walked toward Mara.

Mara rose.

“I yield,” Lilith said. “I cannot hold out. I am defeated. Yet—I cannot open my hand.”

“Have you tried?”

“I am trying now.”

“Then I will take you to my father. You have wronged Him most. Therefore, He can help you best.”

“How?”

“He will forgive you.”

“If He would only let me cease…”

“You shall die—but not as you think. You shall die out of death into life. Now is the Life, which was never against you.”

Like her mother, Mara embraced her. Kissed her forehead.

The fiery-cold agony left her eyes. Tears filled them.

She carried her to her bed, laid her down, closed her eyes with tender hands.

Lilith wept.

Mara opened the door.

Outside, dawn waited—with Spring in her arms.

Gently, they slipped in. A soft wind flowed in their garments, rippling around Lilith, stirring the sea of her eternal life. It touched her, awakened her, called her home.

She breathed. She listened.

In the wind, rain fell—soft, healing rain. It soaked the desert, the sands of her heart. It drank in, and healed.

When Mara returned, Lilith’s tears flowed slower than the rain. Soon, she slept.

And the night passed.

The Mother of Sorrows rose, her face veiled, and went to wake the Little Ones. They slept as if untouched by time, yet at her voice they stirred—awakening fresh and bright, as though newly made. With cheerful steps, they followed her down the stairs, their small feet echoing in the quiet cottage. She led them to where the princess lay, still weeping in her sleep, her tears tracing paths like silver rivers down her cheeks.

The children’s joyful faces turned solemn as they gazed upon her. One looked from the princess to the storm-wracked sky beyond the window. “The sky is falling!” he declared.

“Look,” another whispered, eyes wide with awe, “the white juice is running out of the princess!”

“Is it rivers?” asked Odu, tilting his head, staring at the thin streams that ran from her hollowed cheeks.

“Yes,” Mara replied softly, “the most wonderful rivers of all.”

“But I thought rivers were bigger,” Odu said, turning to me—his only source of such knowledge—“like a thousand Little Ones rushing together, making loud noises!”

“Look at the rivers of the sky,” Mara said gently. “See how they fall to awaken the waters beneath the earth. Soon, rivers will flow everywhere—merry, loud, like countless happy children. Oh, how glad you’ll be when you see them! You’ve never known water, but it is the loveliest thing in the world.”

“That must be the gladness of the ground,” Odu mused, “because the princess has grown good.”

“See the gladness of the sky!” said another.

“Are these rivers the princess’s gladness?” Luva asked.

“They are not her blood, for they are not red,” Mara answered.

“They are the juice within the juice,” she added.

Odu touched his eye, studied his fingertip, then shook his head. “Princess won’t bite now,” he said.

“No,” Mara agreed. “She will never do that again. But now we must take her closer to home.”

“Is that a nest?” Sozo asked.

“A very big one,” Mara said. “But first, we must go somewhere else.”

“What is that place?”

“The biggest room in all this world. But I think it will soon be pulled down—it will be too full of little nests. Go and fetch your clumsies.”

“Please—are there any cats in it?” Luva asked.

“Not one,” Mara said. “The nests are too full of lovely dreams for even one cat to enter.”

“We’ll be ready in a minute,” Odu called, darting out, followed by all but Luva.

Lilith was awake now, her eyes open, watching us with a quiet, sorrowful smile.

“But her rivers are flowing so fast,” Luva murmured, standing beside her, unable to look away. “Her robe is all… I don’t know what. The clumsies won’t like it!”

“They won’t mind,” Mara said. “Those rivers are so clean—they cleanse the whole world.”

I had been asleep by the fire, but had woken long ago and listened. Now I rose.

“It is time to mount, Mr. Vane,” our hostess said.

“Tell me,” I asked, “is there no way to avoid the channels and the den of monsters?”

“There is an easy path across the riverbed,” she replied. “I will show you. But you must pass through the monsters once more.”

“I fear for the children,” I said.

“Fear will not come near them,” she answered.

We left the cottage. The beasts waited by the door. Odu was already mounted on one of the two elephants meant to carry the princess. I climbed onto Lona’s horse. Mara lifted Lilith’s body and placed it into my arms. When she returned with the princess, the children cried out in delight—she was no longer veiled!

Gazing at her, entranced by her beauty, the boys forgot to receive her. But the elephants stepped forward, lifting Lilith gently with their trunks—one around her waist, one around her knees—and with Mara’s help, laid her between them.

“Why does the princess want to go?” a small boy asked. “She’d stay good if she stayed here!”

“She wants to go, and she doesn’t want to go,” Mara said. “We are helping her. She cannot stay here.”

“What are you helping her to do?”

“To go where she can receive more help—help to open her hand, which has been closed for a thousand years.”

“So long?” the boy said. “Then she’s learned to live without it. Why open it now?”

“Because it holds something that is not hers.”

“Please, Lady Mara,” Luva said, “may we have some of your very dry bread before we go?”

Mara smiled and brought four loaves and a great jug of water. “We’ll eat as we walk,” the children said—but they drank the water with joy.

“This must be elephant-juice!” one exclaimed. “It makes me so strong!”

We set out. The Lady of Sorrow walked beside us, more radiant than the sun, with the white leopardess trailing behind. I thought she would only guide us to the path across the channels, but soon I realized she would accompany us all the way. I offered to dismount so she could ride, but she refused.

“I carry no burden,” she said. “The children and I will walk together.”

It was the most beautiful morning—the sun shone brightly, the wind blew sweetly—but the desert remained dry, lifeless. We crossed the channels easily, the children dancing around Mara, until we reached the ridge above the bad burrow just as the sun began to vanish.

I made the Little Ones mount their elephants—fearful the moon might delay. The Lady of Sorrow now walked beside me, the elephants following—those carrying the princess in the center, the leopardess bringing up the rear. As we neared the edge of the basin, the moon rose, pale and hesitant, revealing the shallow, silent depression before us.

Mara stepped into it. No ripple stirred beneath her feet or under my horse. But the moment the elephants bearing the princess touched the ground, the earth began to heave and boil. From the depths, the monstrous brood stirred—hideous creatures rising on all sides, necks stretched, claws extended, mouths agape.

Long-billed heads, gnarled faces, writhing tentacles surged toward Lilith. She lay in terror, paralyzed. Whether the creatures saw the children, I did not know—but none touched them. Not one vile limb passed the living wall of her guardians.

“Little Ones,” I shouted, “keep close to the princess. Be brave—they will not harm you.”

“What will not harm us?” they asked. “We don’t know what to be brave against!”

And then I understood: they could not see the horrors. Their blindness was their protection. What they could not perceive could not wound them.

Yet I saw them—those awful forms! Mara walked ahead of me when suddenly a bodiless head bounced between us. The leopardess lunged from behind, but the creature twisted with a hideous shriek, vanished into the ground.

The death in my arms calmed my fear. I stood unmoved, though never had I seen such a cursed gathering.

Mara continued forward. The leopardess now walked close behind her, trembling often, for the night was cold. Then, to my left, the earth began to rise—a wave of soil slithering toward us. It grew taller, and from it emerged a terrible head with fleshy tubes for hair, its mouth gaping wide, snapping at me.

The leopardess sprang—but fell short. Almost beneath our hooves, a colossal snake reared up, its eyes glowing with a sickly light. Again the leopardess charged—only to find nothing. A third time she attacked, failed, and gave up.

But I knew the danger. I urged haste. The moon, too, seemed strange—rising weakly, sinking back as if struggling to remain. Its arc was low, and it now began to descend quickly.

We were nearly across when, between us and the edge, a long neck rose. Atop it sat a corpse-like head, half-open mouth filled with jagged teeth.

I pressed on. It retreated, then stepped aside.

The lady stepped onto firm land. The leopardess, roused again, turned and flew at the monster’s throat.

I paused to ensure the elephants, with the princess and children, reached safety. Then I turned to watch the leopardess.

That instant, the moon disappeared.

For a breath, I saw the leopardess and the serpent tangled in a cloud of dust. Then darkness swallowed them.

Trembling, my horse wheeled and raced to catch up with the elephants.

As we approached, a shapeless jelly dropped onto the princess. Instantly, a white dove swooped down, pecking it with its beak. It squelched, sucked, and fell away.

Then I heard a woman’s voice speaking with Mara. I recognized it.

“I fear she is dead,” Mara said.

“I will send to find her,” the mother replied. “But why should you fear? Death cannot harm one who dies doing the work given her.”

“I shall miss her deeply,” Mara said. “She is wise and good. Yet I would not have her live beyond her time.”

“She has gone down with the wicked,” the mother said. “She will rise with the righteous. We shall see her again soon.”

“Mother,” I said, though I could not see her, “we come to you many—most are Little Ones. Will you welcome us all?”

“You are welcome,” she answered. “Sooner or later, all become little ones. All must sleep in my house. It is well to sleep young and willingly. My husband prepares Lilith’s couch. She is neither young nor fully willing—but it is well she has come.”

I heard no more. Mother and daughter vanished into the dark.

But ahead, a light glimmered. Toward it we stumbled over the moor.

Adam stood in the doorway, candle in hand, guiding us. He spoke with his wife, who behind him placed bread and wine on the table.

“Happy children,” I heard her say, “to have seen my daughter’s face! Truly, it is the loveliest in the world.”

When we reached the door, Adam welcomed us—almost cheerfully. He set the candle on the threshold, then went to the elephants to take the princess. But she pushed him away, stepping between them, erect and proud. The elephants moved aside, leaving her alone with the man who had once been her husband—ashamed of her gaunt form, yet unyielding.

He stood, his eyes shining with a fierce welcome beneath their sternness.

“We have long waited for thee, Lilith,” he said.

She gave no reply.

Eve and her daughter came to the door.

“The mortal foe of my children!” Eve murmured, radiant in her beauty.

“Your children are no longer in danger,” Mara said. “She has turned from evil.”

“Do not trust her too soon, Mara,” Eve answered. “She has deceived many.”

“But will you open the mirror of the Law of Liberty, mother? Let her enter it and abide there! She consents to open her hand and restore. Will not the great Father restore her to His children?”

“I do not know Him,” Lilith whispered, voice trembling with doubt.

“Therefore you are miserable,” Adam said.

“I will return whence I came!” she cried, wringing her hands, turning to leave.

“That is exactly what I would have you do—return to Him from whom you came. In your agony, did you not cry out for Him?”

“I cried for Death—to escape Him and you!”

“Death is already coming to lead you to Him. You know neither Death nor the Life that dwells in Death. Both are friends to you. I am dead—and I love you. You are weary, burdened—ashamed? Is the being you corrupted not finally become an evil to you? Would you endure eternal disgrace? You cannot cease—will you not be restored and BE?”

She stood silent, head bowed.

“Father,” Mara said, “take her in your arms and carry her to her bed. There she will open her hand and die into life.”

“I will walk,” the princess said.

Adam turned and led the way. She followed, weakly.

Eve came to me where I sat with Lona in my arms. She took the child from me and carried her inside.

I dismounted. The children did too. The horse and elephants shivered. Mara stroked each, and they lay down, sleeping.

She led us into the cottage and gave the children bread and wine.

Adam and Lilith stood together—silent.

Eve returned from the chamber of death, where she had laid Lona. She offered food to the princess.

“Your beauty kills me! I seek death, not food!” Lilith said, turning away.

“This food will help you die,” Eve replied.

But Lilith refused.

“If you will not eat nor drink,” Adam said, “come and see where you will lie in peace.”

He led the way through the door of death. She followed, subdued.

But when her foot crossed the threshold, she drew it back, pressing her hand to her chest—struck by the cold immortal.

A wild wind howled across the roof, then died into a moan.

She stood pale with terror.

“It is he!” her lips formed—voiceless, but I read them.

“Who, princess?” I whispered.

“The great Shadow,” she murmured.

“He cannot enter here,” Adam said. “Here, no one can be harmed. Even over him, power is given me.”

“Are the children in the house?” Lilith asked.

At that word, Eve’s heart softened.

“He never dared touch a child,” she said. “Nor have you ever harmed one. Your own daughter—you sent her into the loveliest sleep. She was already dead when you killed her. And now, Death shall be the atonemaker. You shall sleep together.”

“Wife,” Adam said, “let us first put the children to bed—so she may see them safe.”

He returned to fetch them.

As soon as he left, the princess knelt before Eve, clasped her knees.

“Beautiful Eve,” she said, “persuade your husband to kill me. He will listen to you. I truly wish to open my hand—but I cannot.”

“You cannot die without opening it,” Eve replied. “To kill you would not help you.”

“But he cannot—no one can kill you but the Shadow. And whom he kills never knows she is dead. She lives to do his will, thinking it her own.”

“Then show me my grave,” Lilith said. “I am so weary—I cannot live longer. I must go to the Shadow—yet I do not want to.”

She did not understand. She tried to rise, but collapsed at Eve’s feet.

The Mother lifted her and carried her inward.

I followed Adam, Mara, and the children into the chamber of death. We passed Eve holding Lilith, and went deeper.

“You shall not go to the Shadow,” I heard Eve say.

“Even now, his head is under my heel.”

The dim light in Adam’s hand flickered over sleeping faces. As he moved forward, darkness swallowed them.

The air felt dead—was it because none breathed it?

Profound sleep filled the vast space. It was as if no one had awakened since I last visited. The forms I remembered still lay there.

My father rested as I had left him—closer now to perfect peace. The woman beside him looked younger.

The darkness, the cold, the silence, the stillness, the faces of the lovely dead—made the children’s hearts beat softly, but their voices whispered.

“What a curious place to sleep!” one said. “I’d rather be in my nest.”

“It’s so cold!” another complained.

“Yes,” Adam said, “but you won’t feel cold in sleep.”

“Where are our nests?” several asked, looking around.

“Find places,” Adam replied. “Sleep wherever you choose.”

They scattered instantly, moving beyond the light with fearless steps. Yet we still heard their soft voices—they saw what I could not.

“Oh!” one cried. “Such a beautiful lady! May I sleep beside her? I’ll creep in quietly and not wake her.”

“Yes, you may,” Eve said behind us.

We reached the couch as the child crept under the sheet. He laid his head beside the lady’s, looked up at us, and fell still. His eyelids closed—he slept.

We walked farther. Another child had climbed onto a woman’s couch.

“Mother! mother!” he cried, kneeling beside her. “She’s so cold—she can’t speak,” he said, looking up. “But I’ll make her warm!”

He lay down, pressed close, and wrapped his arm around her. In seconds, he slept—smiling with absolute content.

We came to Luva. She stood on tiptoe, leaning over a couch.

“My own mother wouldn’t have me,” she whispered. “Will you?”

No reply. She looked up at Eve.

The great mother lifted her to the couch. She slid beneath the snowy covering.

Each child, save three boys, found a resting place beside a still, white woman. The little orphans had chosen mothers.

One tiny girl chose a father—mine.

A boy lay beside the matron with the slow-healing hand.

On the middle of three empty couches, Lona rested.

Eve set Lilith beside her.

Adam pointed to the vacant couch on Lona’s right.

“There, Lilith,” he said, “is the bed I have prepared for you.”

She glanced at her daughter—like a statue carved from alabaster—and shuddered.

“How cold it is,” she murmured.

“You will soon grow comfortable in the cold,” Adam said.

“Promises to the dying are easy,” she replied. “But I know it. I have slept. I am dead.”

“I believed you dead long ago,” Adam said. “But I see you alive.”

“More alive than you know,” she said. “I was scarce alive when you first knew me. Now I have slept—and awakened. I am dead—and truly live.”

“I fear that child,” she whispered, pointing to Lona. “She will rise and terrify me.”

“She is dreaming love for you.”

“But the Shadow!” she moaned. “I fear the Shadow—he will be angry with me!”

“He whose sight makes the horses of heaven rear and flee—dares not disturb a dream in this quiet chamber.”

“I shall dream then?”

“You will dream.”

“What dreams?”

“I cannot tell—but none HE can enter. When the Shadow comes here, it will be to sleep also. His hour will come—and he knows it.”

“How long shall I sleep?”

“You and he will be the last to wake in the morning of the universe.”

The princess lay down, drew the sheet over her, stretched straight, and stared with open eyes.

Adam turned to his daughter.

“Lilith,” Mara said, “you will not sleep—no matter how long you lie—if you do not open your hand and give up what is not yours.”

“I cannot,” she said.

“I would, if I could. I am weary. The shadows of death gather about me.”

“They will gather—but they cannot enfold you while your hand remains shut. You may think you are dead—but it is only a dream. You may think you are awake—but it is still a dream. Open your hand, and you will sleep truly—then wake truly.”

“I am trying,” she said. “But my fingers have grown together, fused into the palm.”

“Then use the strength of your will,” Mara urged. “For the love of life, draw all your forces and break the bonds!”

“I have struggled in vain,” Lilith said. “I can do no more. I am very weary. Sleep weighs heavy on my lids.”

“The moment you open your hand, you will sleep. Open it. Make an end.”

A flush rose on her parchment-like face. Her contorted hand trembled with effort.

Mara took it, seeking to help.

“Hold, Mara!” Adam said. “There is danger!”

Lilith turned her eyes to Eve, pleading.

“There was a sword,” she murmured, “once in your husband’s hand. I fled when I saw it. I heard him say it would divide whatever was not one and indivisible.”

“I have the sword,” Adam said. “The angel gave it to me when he left the gate.”

“Bring it, Adam,” Lilith pleaded. “Cut off this hand—so I may sleep.”

“I will,” he said.

He gave the candle to Eve and went.

The princess closed her eyes.

Minutes passed. Adam returned, holding an ancient weapon. The scabbard looked like aged vellum, but the hilt shone like untarnished gold. He drew the blade—it flashed like a pale blue northern streamer. The light made Lilith open her eyes.

She saw the sword, shuddered, and held out her hand.

Adam took it. The blade gleamed once. A small spurt of blood—then he laid the severed hand in Mara’s lap.

Lilith gave one moan—and was already deep in sleep.

Mara covered the arm with the sheet. The three turned away.

“Will you not dress the wound?” I asked.

“A wound from that sword,” Adam said, “needs no dressing. It is healing, not hurting.”

“Poor lady,” I said. “She will wake with only one hand.”

“Where the dead deformity clung,” Mara replied, “the true, lovely hand is already growing.”

We heard a childish voice behind us. We turned.

Eve’s candle lit Lilith’s sleeping face—and the waking faces of three Little Ones grouped on the other side of her couch.

“How beautiful she has become!” one said.

“Poor princess,” another murmured. “I’ll sleep with her. She won’t bite anymore!”

He climbed into her bed and fell asleep instantly.

Eve covered him.

“I’ll sleep on her other side,” the third said. “She’ll have two to kiss her when she wakes!”

“And I’m left alone!” the first said mournfully.

“I’ll put you to bed,” Eve said. She handed the candle to her husband and led the child away.

We turned to return to the cottage. I was sad—no one had offered me a place in the house of the dead.

Eve joined us and walked ahead with her husband. Mara walked beside me, cradling Lilith’s hand in her robe.

“Ah, you’ve found her!” we heard Eve say as we entered the cottage.

The door stood open. Two elephant-trunks emerged from the night.

“I sent them with the lantern,” Eve said to her husband. “To search for Mara’s leopardess. They’ve brought her.”

I followed Adam to the door. Between us, we took the white creature from the elephants and carried her to the chamber we had just left—women leading the way, Eve with the light, Mara still holding the hand.

There, we laid the leopardess across the feet of the princess, her paws outstretched, her head resting between them.

I turned to Eve and said, “Mother, one couch beside Lona is empty. I know I am unworthy—but may I not sleep tonight in your chamber with my dead? Will you forgive both my fear and my boldness, and take me in? I surrender myself. I am weary of being alive, and would fain rest in the stillness.”

“The couch next to Lona was meant for you,” she replied softly. “But there is a task to be done before you may sleep.”

“I am ready,” I answered.

“How do you know you can do it?” she asked, a faint smile touching her lips.

“Because you ask it,” I said.

“What is it?”

She turned to Adam. “Is he forgiven, husband?”

“From the depths of my heart,” Adam said.

Then he spoke to me: “Come, Mara—give me your hand.”

She placed it gently in his lap. He took it with care, as though holding something sacred.

“Come with me,” he said to me. “We will go to the cottage. There I will tell you what must be done.”

As we walked, a sudden wind swept through the valley—wild and sharp—and above us, on the roof, came the sound of great wings flapping. But like before, the storm passed quickly, fading into a low, mournful moan that lingered in the air.

When the door of the death-chamber closed behind us, Adam sat down. I stood before him, still.

“You remember,” he began, “how after leaving my daughter’s house, you came upon a dry rock marked by the scars of an ancient waterfall. You climbed it and found yourself in a vast sandy desert. Now, go back to that rock. From its peak, walk deep into the desert—but do not take many steps before lying down. Place your head upon the sand and listen. If you hear the whisper of water beneath, go a little farther and listen again. If the sound remains, you are on the right path. Every few yards, stop. Lie down. Listen. If at any point the sound fades, you have strayed. Then listen in every direction until you hear it again. Follow the murmur closely—never retrace your steps. The sound will grow stronger, guiding you to where it is loudest. That is the place. Dig with the spade I will give you until you reach moisture. In that wet earth, lay the hand. Cover it level with the desert floor, and return home.

“But heed this well: carry the hand with reverence. Never set it down, no matter how safe the place may seem. Let nothing touch it. Do not pause or turn aside for any obstacle. Never look behind you. Speak to no one. Answer none. Walk straight ahead, unswerving.

“It is dark now, and dawn is far off—but you must begin at once.”

He gave me the hand. It was cold, yet not lifeless. And then he handed me a spade.

“This is my gardening spade,” he said. “With it, I have brought many beautiful things into the light.”

I took it, and stepped out into the night.

It was bitterly cold, and utterly black. To fall here would be to vanish into nothingness. The path ahead was treacherous—even in daylight, it was perilous! Yet I had not chosen this burden. The moment I stepped forward, I knew I was not alone. A pale, ghostly light rose from the ground with each footfall, illuminating my way. Through the heather and over jagged stones, I walked without stumbling, guided by unseen hands.

The bad burrow lay silent. No wave stirred. No face broke the surface as I crossed it.

A moon rose—pale and silver—and lit the path ahead. By morning’s edge, I was near the dried channels of the river’s first branch, close, I thought, to Mara’s cottage.

The moon hung low, just above the horizon, when I saw a figure blocking the narrow trail. Cloaked from head to toe in a veil of mist, shimmering under the moonlight, it stood motionless.

I did not slow. I did not look.

The veil fell away.

“Have you already forgotten me?” whispered the princess—or what seemed her.

I said nothing. I kept walking.

“You meant to leave me in that terrible tomb!” she cried. “Do you not understand? Wherever I wish to be, I am. Take my hand—I am alive, as you are!”

For a moment, I almost said, *Give me your left hand.* But I stopped myself. I clenched my jaw and pressed forward.

“Give me my hand!” she shrieked, lunging at me. “Or I will tear you apart—you are mine!”

She seized at me—but nothing touched my skin. I shuddered, but did not falter. Her form dissolved into air, and I saw her no more.

Then, filling the path ahead, came a long column of armed men—marching with measured tread, their armor clashing in the silence. I walked through them as if they were shadows. I do not know if they parted for me, or if they were not truly there. But they turned and followed. I could feel their steps behind me, hear the clang of steel, yet I cast no glance backward. Their sounds faded into the distance.

A little farther on, where the moon was nearly gone and shadows swallowed the path, I saw a woman seated where the trail narrowed—her face wrapped in cloth.

“Oh,” she said, “you’ve come at last. I’ve waited here for you—perhaps an hour, perhaps more. You’ve done well. Your trial is over. My father sent me to meet you so you might rest. Give me your charge. Lay your head in my lap. I’ll guard both until the sun rises. I am not always bitterness—nor to all men.”

Her voice was sweet poison. I was exhausted. How tempting it was to believe her—to surrender. What if, in rigid obedience, I trampled the very essence of the Lady of Sorrow beneath my feet?

My heart trembled. It felt as though it would burst from my chest.

Yet my will hardened. My step did not waver.

I bit down on my tongue, lest I speak. I moved on.

If Adam sent her, he could not blame me for refusing her. And if even the Lady of7895364210
Sorrow could not turn me aside, then perhaps she would love me all the more.

Just as I neared her, she pulled back the cloth.

Her beauty was overwhelming—but those were not Mara’s eyes. No lie could imitate them so perfectly. I walked past her as if she were not there. My foot found only empty space.

I was almost across when a Shadow blocked the way.

I think it was *The Shadow*.

It wore a helmet—but as I drew closer, I saw it was no helmet. It was the head itself—twisted, distorted, barely human. A chill wind struck me—damp, foul, like the breath of a tomb. My limbs grew weak. My bones threatened to collapse. I felt as if I were passing through him—but now I wonder if he passed through me. For a moment, I was one of the damned.

Then—softly—a gentle breeze stirred, like the first breath of spring. And before me, the sky began to glow.

Dawn was breaking.

Now my path led past Mara’s cottage. The door stood wide open. On the table, I saw a loaf of bread and a pitcher of water. But no cry, no wail—no sign of mourning.

I reached the precipice where the river once flowed. I climbed its weathered face and entered the desert.

At last, after listening again and again, I found the spot where the hidden water murmured most clearly. I tied Lilith’s hand around my neck and began to dig.

It was a long, grueling labor. The sand was loose, and I had to make a deep hole. But finally, I struck damp earth.

I threw the spade aside and laid the hand down. Water oozed from beneath its fingers.

I leapt up and hurried to fill the grave.

Then, utterly spent, I collapsed beside it—and slept.

When I awoke, the earth was damp beneath me, and the path to the grave was turning into quicksand. The ancient river swelled with urgency, pushing against its burden. Soon it would roar over the precipice, splitting into two currents—one plunging into the orchard valley to reclaim it, the other sweeping toward the monstrous horde, perhaps drowning them. Between these torrents, the Evil Wood would stand as an island of silence.

I set out immediately for those who had sent me. At the edge of the precipice, I chose the path between the water’s branches—my way led past Mara’s cottage. I wanted one last glimpse of her before I surrendered to sleep. Now I knew how to cross the channels, even if the river had already filled them.

But when I arrived, the door stood open. Bread and water remained untouched on the table. Silence wrapped the place like a shroud. I called aloud, but no answer came. I turned away.

A little farther on, I found a gray-haired man sitting on the sand, weeping. “What troubles you, sir?” I asked. “Are you abandoned?”

“I weep,” he said, “because they will not let me die. I’ve been to the house of death, and though I am old, its mistress refuses me. Intercede for me, if you know her.”

“I cannot,” I replied. “She accepts only those she is allowed to take.”

“How do you know this? You’ve never sought death! You’re too young to desire it.”

“Perhaps,” I said, “your words mean that if you were young again, you still wouldn’t wish to die.”

“Indeed, young sir, I wouldn’t! And I’m certain you never will.”

“I may be too young to want death,” I answered, “but I am young enough to desire life. That is why I come now—to see if she will finally receive me. You wish to die because you no longer care to live. She wonches you, for no one dies who does not long to live.”

“It ill becomes your youth to mock an old man in need. Cease your riddles!”

“Did not the Mother tell you something similar?”

“In truth, she did—but I paid little heed to her words.”

“Then,” I said, “it is clear you have not yet learned how to die. And I grieve for you. I once stood where you are—until I met the Lady of Sorrow. I am young, yes, but I have known sorrow deeply. Please, hear me: go to her cottage. It lies yonder. Her door stands open. There is bread and water on her table. Go inside. Sit. Eat. Drink. Wait. When she comes, ask her counsel. She is true. Her wisdom is great.”

He began to weep anew. I left him there.

The sun had set, the moon not yet risen, when I reached the abode of the monsters. The air was still as stone until I stepped across the threshold. Then, behind me, I heard the rush of many waters—and a great cry—but I did not turn.

Before I reached the house of death, the cold bit deep, the darkness thick. Cold and dark merged into one, seeping into my bones. Yet the candle of Eve glowed from a window, guiding me, shielding my heart from frost and shadow.

The door stood open. The cottage was empty. I sat down, disconsolate.

And then—loneliness rose within me like a tide. Thousands were near, yet none were with me. True, I was dead, not they. But by their life or my death, we were separated. They lived; I was not dead enough to feel their presence. Doubt crept in. They were distant, beyond reach. No one could help lay me beside them.

Never before had I known desolation so fully.

I tried to reason with myself: “This solitude is only seeming. You are awake; they sleep. That is all. They lie still. They do not speak. But they are here. Soon, soon you will be with them.”

I dropped Adam’s old spade. Its dull thud echoed through the clay floor, reverberating from the chamber beyond. A childish fear seized me. I stared at the coffin door.

But father Adam, mother Eve, sister Mara—soon they would come. Then—welcome the cold world, the white neighbors!

I forgot my fear. I lived, briefly. I loved my dead.

Something stirred in the chamber of the dead. From within came a sound—not quite sound, yet felt. My soul leapt into my ears. Was it merely a tremor in the air, too faint for hearing, yet vibrating through every sense?

I *knew*, without hearing, without feeling—it was coming. Closer.

In the depths of my isolation, a fragile hope awakened.

The silent thrum reached the coffin door—then became sound, striking my ear.

The door creaked open slowly, softly.

I stopped listening. I watched.

It opened just a crack—and a face appeared.

It was Lona’s.

Her eyes were closed, yet her face looked upon me, as if she saw.

White as Eve’s, white as Mara’s—but without their light.

She spoke. Her voice was like a night wind drifting through grass.

“Are you coming, king?” she whispered. “I cannot rest until you join me, gliding down the river to the great sea, to the dream-land. Sleepiness is full of beauty. Come and see.”

“Ah, my darling!” I cried. “Had I known—how could I have thought you were dead?”

She lay on my chest—cold as ice fused to marble. Her arms, pale and thin, curled weakly around me. She sighed.

“Carry me back to my bed, king. I want to sleep.”

I carried her, clutching her tightly, afraid she might dissolve from my grasp. Without knowing, I walked straight to her couch.

“Lay me down,” she murmured, “and cover me from the warm air. It hurts—just a little. Your bed is next to mine. I’ll see you when I wake.”

She was already asleep.

I fell onto my own couch—blessed beyond measure, as if on the eve of a wedding.

“Come, sweet cold,” I whispered, “still my heart swiftly.”

Instead, a soft light filled the room. I saw Adam approaching. He held no candle, yet I saw him clearly.

At the side of Lona’s bed, he smiled down at her—then greeted me across the space.

“We went to the top of the hill,” he said. “We heard the waters rushing. They will reach the den of the monsters tonight. But why didn’t you wait for us?”

“My child couldn’t sleep,” I replied.

“She is fast asleep,” he said.

“Yes—now,” I said. “But she was awake when I laid her down.”

“She was asleep all along,” he insisted.

“Perhaps dreaming of you—and came to you?”

“She did.”

“And you didn’t notice her eyes were closed?”

“Now that I think of it… I did.”

“If you’d looked before laying her down, you’d have seen her sleeping on the couch.”

“That would have been terrible.”

“You would have found she was no longer in your arms.”

“That would have been worse.”

“Perhaps it’s worth thinking about. But seeing it wouldn’t have troubled you.”

“Dear father,” I said, “why am I not sleepy? I expected to fall asleep the moment I laid my head down—like the Little Ones.”

“Your hour has not yet come. You must eat first.”

“Oh—I should not have lain down without your leave. I cannot sleep without your help. I’ll get up at once!”

But I could not move. My body felt heavier than stone.

“There’s no need,” Adam said. “We’ll serve you here. Do you feel cold?”

“Not cold enough to move, but perhaps too cold to eat.”

He came to my side, leaned over, and breathed upon my heart. Instantly, warmth returned.

As he stepped away, I heard a voice—soft, low, sweet. It was the Mother’s song. I thought she sat beside my bed in the dark. But as the melody rose, it soared—becoming the voice of a woman-angel, higher than larks, higher than any human heart had ever climbed.

I heard every word, but only one line stayed:

*Many a wrong, and its curing song;
Many a road, and many an inn;
Room to roam, but only one home
For all the world to win!*

I felt I had heard it before.

Then all three came—Adam, Eve, Mara—bearing bread and wine. I sat up to eat.

Adam stood at my right. Eve and Mara at my left.

“You are truly kind, father Adam, mother Eve, sister Mara,” I said. “I am ashamed and sorry.”

“We knew you would return,” Eve said.

“How could you know?”

“Because I was born to care for my brothers and sisters,” Mara smiled.

“Every creature must one night surrender,” Adam said. “He was made for freedom, and must not remain a slave.”

“It will be late, I fear, before all have lain down,” I said.

“There is no early or late here,” Adam replied. “True time begins only when one lays himself down. Men return slowly. Women come faster. A vast, desolate space divides him who dies from him who lives. The former may hurry—but here, there is no haste.”

“To us,” Eve said, “you were always coming. We knew Mara would find you. You had to come.”

“How long since my father lay down?”

“Years mean nothing here,” Adam said. “We do not count them. Your father will wake when his morning arrives. Your mother—lying beside you—”

“Ah! So it *is* my mother!”

“Yes—the one with the wounded hand,” Adam said. “She will rise long before your morning ripens.”

“I am sorry.”

“Be glad.”

“It must be a sight for God Himself—to see such a woman awaken!”

“It is indeed,” Adam said. “A sight that brings joy to her Maker. He sees the travail of His soul—and is satisfied. Look at her once more… and sleep.”

He let the candlelight fall upon her face.

“She looks younger,” I said.

“She *is* younger,” he replied. “Even Lilith begins to look younger.”

I lay down, drowsy with bliss.

“But when you see your mother again,” Adam continued, “you will not recognize her at first. She will grow steadily younger—until she reaches the perfection of her womanhood—a splendor beyond imagining. Then she will open her eyes, see her husband on one side, her son on the other—and rise to go to a father and brother more dear than they.”

I listened as in a dream. I was cold—but the cold no longer hurt.

I felt them clothe me in the white garment of the dead.

Then I forgot everything.

The night around me was pale with sleeping faces.

But I was asleep too—nor knew that I slept.

I awoke—not to light, but to an endless cold, so profound it seemed not absence of warmth, but the very essence of being. I was blessed beyond measure, more so than memory could now grasp. Warmth existed only as a forgotten idea, no longer tied to pleasure. I had known it once—once—but its meaning slipped like smoke through my fingers. This cold, though absolute, cradled me. It dissolved sorrow, erased pain, and hushed every restless thought. *Comforted?* No. Sorrow was not merely eased—it was swallowed whole by something greater: a life drawing near, ready to restore all that had ever been good or beautiful, multiplied a hundredfold.

I lay still, breath slow, body stretched upon the damp earth, breathing in the rich, moist scents of the world’s deep bosom. I felt the souls of primroses, daisies, snowdrops—all asleep beneath the soil, waiting for spring. How can one describe the joy of such a frozen wakefulness? I had nothing left to do but be. No effort, no striving—only presence. The cold grew deeper, yet I welcomed it, embracing it as a lover might embrace a long-lost friend. My awareness of self faded, replaced by a growing sense of bliss—unimaginable, yet undeniable. I had not earned it. I had not prayed for it. It simply was. Because I existed, I was graced. And because I existed, I was held within a Will that dwelled inside me.

Then the dreams began.

I was naked atop a mountain of snow, windless, silent. Below, white mist swelled like a sea of clouds. Above, the moon hung close, pale and cold. Beyond her, the sky—colder still—held us both in its infinite silence. I was Adam, waiting for God to breathe into my nostrils the breath of life. But then I was not Adam—I was a child nestled in the arms of a mother whose whiteness glowed with inner radiance. Then again, I was a youth on a white horse, leaping from cloud to cloud across a blue heaven, moving calmly toward some radiant destination.

Centuries passed—or were they millennia? Or just one eternal night? Time meant nothing. I was beyond dimensions, beyond senses. I think I was where I am—deep within the heart of God.

I dreamed within the core of a melting glacier. The spectral moon drew nearer, its light thinning the ice. The wind howled, and a torrent roared in my ears. I listened—no fear, only peace. The wind, the water, the moon—they sang together, a hymn of quiet anticipation, a song of redemption nearing.

But suddenly—without breaking my serenity—the sum of all my wrongs rushed in. Every lie, every betrayal, every hurt I had ever caused, from before memory to this moment, flooded my soul. I stood in full confession. Each act lived again, and with it, I wept. I lamented the dead. I made atonement to every soul I had wounded. To each person I had offended, I bowed low, begging forgiveness, promising service.

I would build a house for one—a structure never seen on any world. For another, I would train horses of impossible grace and speed. For a third, I would create a garden alive with running streams and pools so still they mirrored the stars. I would write songs that stirred hearts, tales that set minds aflame. I would redirect the forces of nature into wonders that would make even gods laugh in awe.

Love consumed me. Love was my life. Love was everything—just as it must be for the One who made me.

Then darkness—absolute, solid blackness. No trace of light, not even the faintest glow in the eyes. Yet my heart remained unafraid, full of hope. I imagined what light would bring when it came, what new creation would emerge—when suddenly, without thought, I sat up.

The moon peered through the lowest windows of a crypt-like chamber. Her long silver beam stretched across the floor—over fallen sheaves, I thought, ripening still under the care of the great husbandman. But no. There were no sheaves. Not one. The harvest was gone. Gathered in, or swept away by storm—none remained. My dead were gone.

I was alone.

Desolation pierced me deeper than I had ever known. Had there never been any dead? Had I only dreamed them, their beauty, their peace? Why then these walls? These empty beds?

No. They were awake. They were out in the new eternal day—and had forgotten me. They had left me behind.

The tomb, now empty, became ten times more terrible. The quiet ones had brought peace to my mind; now, with them gone, I had no friend, no love near. A moment I sat, staring in horror.

I had been alone with the moon on a peak in the sky. Now I was alone with her in a vast cenotaph. She too stared about, searching, her gaze hollow, lost.

I rose, staggering from the place. The cottage was empty. I ran into the night. No moon. As I stepped out, a thick cloud had risen, covering her. But from far over the heath came a shimmering light, mingled with a ghostly music—like rain falling from the moon, plashing softly on the ground.

I stumbled across the moor, found a lake—still, clear, surrounded by reeds. The moon, hidden behind the cloud, gazed down upon it. But the music continued, drawing me forward. I walked around the edge, climbed the hills.

And then—what I saw!

Where once I had crossed dry riverbeds, cracked and sun-baked, now flowed rivers, torrents, still pools—“a river deep and wide.” The moon flashed upon the water, and the water flashed back—white bursts breaking everywhere, dancing against the rocks. From its depths arose a great song, jubilant, wild—music of freedom born.

I stood, transfixed. My heart swelled. My life was not a failure. I had helped set this river free. My dead were not lost. I had only to follow—follow until I found them. Our meeting might be thousands of years off, but at last—*at last*—I would hold them.

Why else did the floods clap their hands?

I hurried down the hill. My pilgrimage had begun. I knew not which way to turn—but I must go. I must go until I found my living dead.

A swift torrent lay at the foot of the ridge. I rushed in—it did not hold me. I waded through. The next, I leapt across. The third, I swam. The fourth, I waded again.

I paused, enchanted by the ceaseless flow, the broken symphony of rushing water. At times, a melody seemed to rise—almost clear—then dissolve back into the roar. The waters did not overwhelm me with force, but with silence—great, echoing silence that wandered into sound.

As I stood, lost in wonder, a hand touched my shoulder.

I turned.

A man stood before me—strong, radiant, young as if untouched by time. He wore a white robe, and the moon shone in his hair.

“Father,” I said, “where is she? Where are the dead? Has the resurrection come and gone? I cannot bear this loneliness! I fled the chamber—where shall I go to find them?”

“You mistake, my son,” he answered, his voice like calm after storm. “You are still in the chamber of death. Still upon your bed. Asleep. Dreaming. The dead are around you.”

“But when I dream—how can I know?” I pleaded. “The best dream feels truer than waking!”

“When you are truly dead,” he said, “you will dream no false dream. A true soul cannot generate falsehood. The false cannot enter it.”

“But how,” I faltered, “can I tell truth from dream when both feel real?”

“You cannot,” he smiled, “not while you are not yet fully dead. Nor will you when you are—because then the false will not appear. You are on your bed. In the house of death.”

“I believe you,” I whispered, “though I cannot see it.”

“Do not blame yourself. Even in a dream, you believe me. That is why I help you. Extend your left hand—open it—close it gently. It will clasp the hand of your Lona.”

I reached out. My fingers closed—soft, firm, warm—on hers.

“But father,” I cried, “she is warm!”

“Your hand is warm to hers. Cold does not exist in our land. Neither of you is yet in the fields of home. But to each other, you are alive, warm, whole.”

My heart lifted. But doubt pricked—sharp, sudden.

“Father,” I said, “how can I know this is not part of the dream? That even this touch is not illusion?”

“You doubt,” he said, “because you love the truth. Others would gladly believe life a phantom, if only it gave them endless pleasant dreams. But you are not such. Be content for now not to know. The hour will come—soon—when you will see the truth face to face. Then doubt will die. You will remember only the shadow of this doubt. You have not yet looked Truth in the eye. You have seen him through a veil. That which cannot be known except by its own light—you must doubt until you see it directly. But to him who has glimpsed even a shadow of truth, and tries to obey it—even in absence—he will come. And abide forever.”

“I think I understand,” I said.

“Remember,” he replied. “Trials await you—heavy, unknown. Remember what you have seen. You do not know them—yet you know what they meant. Remember also what you will see. Truth is all. And the truth lies hidden and revealed in appearance.”

“How can that be?” I asked, lifting my eyes.

He was gone. Only the sound of water remained—swift, singing, eternal.

I stretched my hands—nothing. Alone. In the land of dreams.

To myself, I seemed awake. But I believed I dreamed—because he had told me so.

Yet even in dreams, one must act. So I resumed my journey.

I crossed many channels, reached a wide rock plateau. Tired, I lay down—longing to wake.

Just as I rose, I noticed a pit beside me—deep, dark, bottomless. Like a grave.

In childhood dreams, I had learned: fall, and you wake. So, with one glance at the sky, one at the rushing waters, I rolled myself over the edge.

For a moment—darkness.

Then—light.

I stood in the garret of my own house. In the wooden chamber. The cowl. The mirror.

Despair crashed over me. Between me and Lona—now—lay an abyss. Space. Time. Being itself. Walls of adamant, impenetrable. I could not reach her.

True, I might return—through the door of light—back to the chamber of the dead. But even then, only a heath and a starry night separated me from the mirror. And only the sun, far across the world, could open it.

But here—here the chasm was wider. She slept. I was awake. I was no longer worthy to share her sleep. I could not hope to awaken with her again.

I had failed. I had fled the dream. The dream was not mine to make—nor was my life. I should have seen it to the end. By fleeing, I had abandoned the holy sleep itself.

I would return to Adam. Confess. Submit.

I crept to my room, threw myself on the bed, and slept—dreamless.

I rose. Listlessly, I went to the library. No one met me. The house felt dead.

I sat with a book, waiting for noon. But no words made sense.

The mutilated manuscript appeared—offered from the masked door. Its sight sickened me. What did the princess matter? Her devilry—mere dust.

I rose, looked out the window.

Brilliant morning. The fountain shot high, fell roaring. The sun sat in its feathery crown. No bird sang. No creature moved. Raven nor librarian came near.

The world was dead around me.

I took another book. Sat. Waited.

Noon approached.

I climbed the stairs to the roof. Closed the door behind me. Turned to open the door out of this dreary world.

I left the chamber with a heart of stone.

I pulled the chains. Adjusted the hood. Rearranged the mirrors. Nothing happened.

I waited. And waited. The mirror remained blank—only my haggard face, and the opposite mirror, staring back.

No vision came.

I returned to the library. The books repelled me—once loved, now hateful.

That night, I lay awake. The next day, I tried again—last time.

Still nothing.

Hours passed. No sound. No one. No movement.

Not weary. Only desolate.

Another sleepless night.

Morning came. I went to the chamber—last time.

No door opened.

My heart died.

I had lost Lona.

Was she anywhere? Had she ever been—except in the decaying cells of my brain?

“I must die one day,” I thought. “And then—straight from my death-bed—I will set out to find her. If she is not there, I will go to the Father and say: ‘Even thou canst not help me. Let me cease. I pray thee.’”

On the fourth night, I slipped into sleep—and this time, I truly awoke.

I opened my eyes. Darkness surrounded me, thick and absolute, yet I knew without doubt that I lay within the house of death. Every moment since I had closed my eyes had been a dream—now, at last, I was awake.

"At last!" I whispered to my heart, and it leapt in joy.

I turned my gaze—and there she stood: Lona. She waited by my side, her presence a beacon in the void. I had never lost her—not really. Only for a brief while had I lost sight of her. How foolishly I had mourned!

The darkness held no power over her. Her eyes blazed with the same light as the Mother’s, and that radiance poured from her face, from her very being. Even her shroud—the garment of death—was transformed. It shimmered like snow, glowing with an inner fire, tenfold brighter now that her soul had risen into fullness. She had slept as a girl; she awakened as a woman, matured in beauty, radiant with the essence of life itself.

I pulled her close, and in that embrace, I felt certain: I lived.

"I woke first," she said, smiling with wonder.

"You did," I replied, "and you woke me."

"I only looked at you… and waited," she murmured.

Then, from the shadows, a candle floated toward us, gliding on unseen currents. Moments later, Adam, Eve, and Mara appeared beside us.

They greeted us quietly, with soft smiles—used to such awakenings, as if they were born anew each dawn.

"I hope your darkness was pleasant?" the Mother asked.

"Not very," I admitted. "But waking from it is heavenly."

"Only just begun," she replied. "You are scarcely awake yet."

"He is clothed upon with Death," Adam said, his voice warm and knowing. "And that is the radiant robe of Life."

He embraced Lona—his child—then placed an arm around me. He paused, studying the princess with quiet intent, then gently patted the head of the leopardess.

"I think we shall meet again soon," he said, looking first at Lona, then at me.

"Must we die again?" I asked.

"No," Adam answered, his smile mirroring the Mother’s. "You have died into life. You will not die again. You must only remain dead—once you’ve died, all dying is done. Now, you have only to live. And live fully, with every blessed strength you possess. The more you live, the stronger you become."

"But won’t I grow weary?" I wondered aloud. "What if I fail to live with all my might?"

"The will is enough," the Mother said. "Strength follows. Pure life knows no fatigue. Our life is fed by the great Life. Those who refuse to die—die many times, constantly, deeper each time, never ending. But here—there is only upward motion, love, and joy."

She smiled, and her glance seemed to say: *We understand. We are mother and son. No farewell can ever pass between us.*

Mara kissed my forehead, laughing softly. "I told you, brother—everything would be well. Next time you offer comfort, say: 'What will be well, is already well.'"

She sighed—a small sound, but heavy with meaning. I thought it carried a silent truth: *They will not believe you.*

"And now," she added, her smile like her mother’s, "you know me."

"I do," I said. "You are the voice crying in the wilderness before the Baptist came. You are the shepherd whose wolves bring the lost sheep home before the night deepens."

"My work will one day end," she said. "Then I shall rejoice with the Great Shepherd who sent me."

"All night long," Adam said, "the morning draws near."

"What is that flapping of wings?" I asked.

"The Shadow hovers," Adam replied. "There is one here he claims as his own. But once ours—she can never be his again."

I turned to look at my parents’ beds, wanting to kiss them before we left. But their places were empty—only the Little Ones remained, having claimed the space with fearless affection.

For a moment, the old terror returned—the dream of desolation, the emptiness. I stepped aside.

"What troubles you?" Lona asked.

"Their empty beds frightened me," I confessed.

"They rose long ago," Adam said. "They kissed you before they went. They whispered, 'Come soon.'"

"And I could neither feel nor hear them," I murmured.

"How could you? You were far away—in your cold, old house. You thought the dread place had reclaimed you. But now—go find them. Your parents, my child," he added, turning to Lona, "must come to find you."

Our hour had come.

Lona approached the couch where the woman who had slain her lay. She bent down and kissed her gently—then nestled into her father’s arms.

"That kiss will draw her home," Adam said.

"Who were her parents?" Lona asked.

"My father," Adam replied, "is hers too."

She turned and placed her hand in mine. I knelt, humbly thanking the three for helping me die.

Lona knelt beside me. Together, they breathed upon us—life, warmth, blessing.

"Hark!" Adam said. "I hear the sun."

I listened. The sun was coming—not slowly, but in a rush, like a thousand times ten thousand distant wings beating the air. A roaring wave of molten fire, millions of miles away, surged toward us. His approach was a crescendo—a symphony of a hundred harmonies rising in one grand chord.

Adam, Eve, and Mara exchanged glances and smiled. That smile lifted into the sky like a three-petaled flower—the family’s morning offering.

From their lips, their faces, their bodies, light spread outward, shining through their garments.

Before I could speak—"Lo, they change!"—Adam and Eve stood before me as angels of resurrection. Mara stood beside them, like the Magdalene at the tomb.

Adam’s face flashed like lightning. Eve held a napkin that scattered sparks of brilliance across the room.

A wind began to moan—deep, pulsing gusts.

"You hear his wings now," Adam said—and I understood he meant not the wings of dawn, but something older, darker.

"It is the great Shadow stirring to depart," he continued. "Wretched creature—he carries himself within him, and cannot rest."

"But is there not something deeper within him?" I asked.

"Without substance," Adam replied, "a shadow cannot exist. Nor without light behind the substance."

He listened, then called out with joy: "Hark! The golden cock!"

Silent for eons, perched on the clock of the universe, the golden cock had finally stirred. Now, at last, he flapped his wings—and soon, he would crow. And men would hear him, again and again, until the eternal day broke.

I listened. Far off, in the heart of endless silence, I heard the clear, jubilant cry—the golden throat shouting defiance at death, at dark. It sang infinite hope. It sang calm. It was the longing of creation finding voice at last—the cry of chaos yearning to become kingdom.

Then—great flapping.

"The black bat has flown!" Mara said.

"Amen, golden cock, bird of God!" Adam cried—and the words echoed through the silent house, rising into the heavens.

At that Amen—like doves lifting from broken pottery on silver wings—the Little Ones sprang up from their beds, kneeling in unison.

"Crow! Crow again, golden cock!" they shouted, as if they had seen and heard him in their dreams.

Each turned to their sleeping companion, gazed with loving eyes, kissed them gently, then leapt from the bed.

Those who had lain beside my parents stared blankly at the empty spaces—sad, confused—for a moment. Then they slid slowly to the floor, falling into each other’s arms. At last, through each other’s eyes, they confirmed: *We are alive. We are awake.*

Suddenly seeing Lona, they rushed to her, radiant with joy.

Odu, spotting the leopardess at the princess’s feet, bounded forward. He threw an arm over her great, sleeping head, fondling and kissing her.

"Wake up, wake apart, darling!" he cried. "It's time to wake!"

But the leopardess did not stir.

"She’s slept herself cold!" Odu said, turning to Mara with wide, pleading eyes.

"She waits for the princess to wake," Mara said gently.

Odu looked at the princess—and saw two of his companions still asleep beside her.

He flew at them.

"Wake up! Wake up!" he shouted, pushing and pulling, frantic.

But they would not move.

Soon, his joy faded. He turned to me, eyes misty.

"They won’t wake," he whispered. "And why are they so cold?"

"They wait for the princess too," I said.

He reached out, laid his hand on her face.

"She’s cold too!" he cried. "What is happening?"

He looked around in horror.

Adam approached.

"Her awakening is not yet ripe," he said. "She is busy forgetting. When she forgets enough to remember—then she will wake."

"Remember?" Odu asked.

"Yes—but not too much at once."

"But the golden cock has crowned!" the child argued, rushing back to his friends. "Peter! Peter! Crispy! Wake up! We’re all awake—except you two! The golden cock has crowed so loud! The sun is coming! Oh, why won’t you wake?"

But Peter did not wake. Neither did Crispy.

Odu wept—openly, helplessly.

"Let them sleep, darling," Adam said softly. "Would you want the princess to wake and find no one? They are happy. So is the leopardess."

Odu wiped his tears—though they were new, and strange, and would soon vanish forever.

We followed Eve to the cottage. There, she offered no bread, no wine—only radiant silence, urging us onward.

So, without word or farewell, we stepped outside.

The horse and elephants waited at the door.

But we were too joyful to mount. Instead, we walked—and they followed.

The darkness had lifted, not into full daylight, but into a soft, luminous twilight where the breath of spring filled the air with quiet wonder. It was as if the world itself had awakened—not just the earth, but something deeper within us. We walked barefoot across the land, and every blade of grass, every heather bush, every low shrub glowed with an inner light. Not from the sky, nor from any sun yet risen, but from within themselves—like the burning bush Moses once saw, untouched by flame yet blazing with divine presence. Or perhaps it was our eyes that now emitted their own light, seeing not merely shapes and colors, but the living thoughts that shaped them—the soul of each thing made visible.

There were no shadows. Nothing cast one upon another. Instead, all things exchanged light, like whispers between friends. I could see in the form and hue of every plant the idea that gave it life—the thought that birthed it, sustained it, and sent it forth into being. My feet, touching the soil, felt a tenderness for each plant they passed over, as though they knew them intimately. The world and I were no longer separate. Life flowed through me, and I through life. The microcosm—the self—was at last united with the macrocosm—the whole. Harmony had been restored. I lived in all things; all things lived in me. To perceive a creature was to know its essence, my own, and the source from which we both came. We were what we were because Another was what He was.

Senses long dormant stirred awake—one after another, beyond naming, beyond description. There were no words for these new perceptions, no images to mirror them. Yet they flooded through me: a vast, ever-expanding awareness, like a chamber with countless doors opening simultaneously, letting in light, sound, feeling, memory—everything entering, everything welcomed.

When a breeze brushed against a cluster of heather, setting its purple bells to chiming softly—*tin-tin-tin*—I did not hear it; I *was* it. I felt the joy of the wind, the vibration of the flowers, the delight of the sensation, and the soul that received them all. I became the hall where joy could celebrate. I was a calm ocean, endlessly stirred by waves of living gladness—each wave different, yet all born of the same eternal joy. Life was no longer struggle or sorrow. It was celebration—a cosmic holiday.

Now I understood: life and truth are one. Pure life is bliss. Where there is no bliss, there is no true life—only existence in death. With every breath drawn from the dark wind, I exhaled thanksgiving. At last, I *was*. I lived—and nothing could take that life from me.

Beside me walked my beloved, and together we journeyed home—to the Father.

Already, before the first sun rose upon our freedom, we possessed more than we could have dreamed. What wonders must await us in the eternal day?

We reached the hollow where ancient monsters once roamed—where nightmares had bred beneath the earth. But now it was transformed. A lake lay there, clear and still, its waters so pure I could see deep into its depths. A great whirlpool had swept away the soil where those abominations once burrowed. And there, at the bottom, lay the entire horrid brood—coiled, folded, knotted, stretched—forms more grotesque than any fevered poet’s imagination could conjure. Their shapes twisted in unnatural spirals, their bodies bloated, their eyes wide with sepulchral horror. Tentacles curled like serpents, limbs fused into monstrous unions. They were the very embodiment of hatred and decay—yet none moved.

They were not dead. Not truly. So long as men and women harbor unclean minds, this lake will remain their tomb. But even in their stillness, they spoke of a deeper truth: evil persists only when it is nurtured by the heart.

Then—listen! The herald of dawn! The auroral wind, soft as a trumpet call, announced the coming of the sun. The master-minister of the human body approached. Rising like a ship launched from the hand of creation, he surged upward, carrying a wave of crimson and gold ahead of him. He paused—gazed down upon the world.

A storm of molten metal, white-hot and roaring—he was but a coal from the eternal altar, offered by the Father to His children. See how every flower straightens its stem, lifts its head, and waits. Not merely for light, but for something greater—for the One who is coming. Not soon, not tomorrow, not in ten thousand years—but *He is coming*, surely, inevitably. What does time matter to Life Himself? To Love Himself?

He is coming. And humanity stretches its necks to watch.

Every morning they will stretch. Every evening they will wait. Until He comes.

Is this only a vision painted on the air? When He arrives, will He find them still watching?

It was resurrection morning. The night had been spent preparing for this moment.

Before us, the children danced, laughing, running free. Behind us, the beasts followed—silent companions in our return. Butterflies fluttered like rainbows, dragonflies darted like sparks, swirling around us in a cloud of color and motion—now descending like a snowfall of prismatic flakes, now rising into the damp air like vapor thick with perfume.

This was summer—not as man had known it, but as it was meant to be: perfect, ideal, untouched by decay. I walked upon the new earth under the new heavens, and found them unchanged in shape—but now open to me. Their hearts, their souls, revealed themselves. Each thing I met greeted me with its spirit, saying, “We come from the same Source. We move toward the same purpose.” They spoke of the One they always served, the One they always returned to. “We are lightning,” they said, “flashes of His fire, taking form as we pass from Him to Him.”

The dark rocks drank in the sunlight like sponges. The world soaked up the light and released life. Lona and I were two fires of joy. Earth breathed upward, sweet smoke ascending to heaven. We breathed inward, longing for home. Our very consciousness was gratitude.

We came to the dry channels—once cracked and barren—now rushing with living water. The streams shouted in their joy, foaming, leaping, singing as they raced over stones. As far as sight could reach, rivers thundered, roaring, dashing—water turned vocal by the rock it touched.

We did not cross. Instead, we walked along the right bank, “in glory and in joy,” until we reached the great cataract at the edge of the sandy desert. There, the river split into two branches, plunging and swirling down in thunderous falls.

We climbed the height—and found no desert.

Instead, a green plain stretched before us, bordered by grassy banks. A wide, deep river flowed silently, full to the brim. Then, for the first time, the Little Ones saw the glory of God in the clear, limpid flow of water.

Instinctively, they plunged in. They swam. The beasts followed. The desert rejoiced. It blossomed—like a rose unfurling in the morning.

Forests sprang up overnight—towering trees, thick with flowering undergrowth, alive with birds whose songs wove through the air like golden threads. Every thicket gave birth to a rivulet, every rivulet sang its own song. The place where the buried hand had lain showed no trace.

Beyond, the river continued—stronger, fuller, flowing from lands unseen. We ascended further—along grassy shores, through forests of noble trees. The grass grew sweeter, the flowers more radiant, the trees taller, the wind heavier with meaning.

At last, we entered a forest unlike any other. Its trees were giants—pillars of living wood, arching overhead into a vaulted roof so dense that few sunbeams pierced through. Into this aerial canopy, the children climbed, scrambling, leaping, calling out to elephants hidden below—whose deep trumpet-answers echoed back.

Lona understood their conversation. I only guessed.

The Little Ones chased squirrels, who played with them, leading them on—then allowed themselves to be caught, petted, loved. Often, a bird of exquisite beauty would alight upon one of them, sing a song of what was coming, then vanish into the air.

And not one monkey—of any kind—could we see.

Lona and I, walking beneath the towering canopy of the forest, heard a sudden cry from above. Moments later, the Little Ones began to tumble down through the leaves, their faces alight with wonder. They had climbed the highest tree in the grove and seen something extraordinary far across the plain—a strange shape on the side of a solitary mountain. The mountain, they said, rose so high it pierced the sky, which seemed to gather thickly around its peak as if trying to hold it back, until it shattered the top clean off.

“It might be a city,” one whispered, “but not like Bulika.”

I climbed to see for myself. And there it was—vast and impossible, rising into blue clouds where sky and stone blurred into one. Mountain and palace, cliff and dwelling, cloud and shadow twisted together in a dreamlike confusion of light and form.
